Characteristic p p analogues of the Mumford–Tate and André–Oort conjectures for products of ordinary GSpin Shimura varieties
Ruofan Jiang

Abstract
. Let p p be an odd prime. We state characteristic p p analogues of the Mumford–Tate conjecture and the André–Oort conjecture for ordinary strata of mod p p Shimura varieties. We prove the conjectures for arbitrary products of GSpin Shimura varieties (and their subvarieties). Important subvarieties of GSpin Shimura varieties include modular and Shimura curves, Hilbert modular surfaces, U ⁡ ( 1 , n ) \mathrm{U}(1,n) unitary Shimura varieties, and moduli spaces of principally polarized Abelian and K3 surfaces. The two conjectures are both related to a notion of linearity for mod p p Shimura varieties, about which Chai has formulated the Tate-linear conjecture. Though seemingly different, the three conjectures are intricately entangled. We will first solve the Tate-linear conjecture for single GSpin Shimura varieties, above which we build the proof of the Tate-linear conjecture and the characteristic p p analogue of the Mumford–Tate conjecture for products of GSpin Shimura varieties. We then use the Tate-linear and the characteristic p p analogue of the Mumford–Tate conjectures to prove the characteristic p p analogue of the André–Oort conjecture. Our proof uses Chai’s results on monodromy of p p -divisible groups and rigidity theorems for formal tori, as well as Crew’s parabolicity conjecture which is recently proven by D’Addezio.



1.
Introduction

We begin by introducing the Mumford–Tate conjecture and the André–Oort conjecture in characteristic 0. For a smooth projective variety
Y
over a number field, the Mumford–Tate conjecture states that the base change to
\mathbb{Q}_{l}
of the Mumford–Tate group has the same neutral component with the
l
-adic étale monodromy group of
Y
. Only special cases of this conjecture are known, and all the non-trivial ones are related to Abelian motives. The result that is mostly relevant to us is
[
46
]
, which proves the Mumford–Tate conjecture for Abelian varieties that correspond to number field valued points of certain Shimura varieties. The André–Oort conjecture, on the other hand, arises in the relatively new field of unlikely intersections. It is related to the distribution of special subvarieties in a Shimura variety. It states that a subvariety of a Shimura variety that contains a Zariski dense collection of special subvarieties must itself be special. In particular, a subvariety containing a Zariski dense set of special points is special. The conjecture has been solved recently, and the proof uses lots of ideas and techniques from different areas, see
[
41
]
.

The main results of this paper concern characteristic
p
analogues of the two conjectures. There are apparent difficulties in formulating the analogues.

For a smooth projective variety
Y
over a finitely generated field of characteristic
p
, it is hard to state an analogue of the Mumford–Tate conjecture, since there is no notion of Hodge structure or Mumford–Tate group. Of course, one can still ask if the
l
-adic étale and crystalline monodromy groups admit rational models over
\mathbb{Q}
, i.e., whether there exists a
\mathbb{Q}
-group whose base changes to
\mathbb{Q}_{l}

resp
.
\mathbb{Q}_{q}
coincide with the
l
-adic étale
resp
. crystalline monodromy groups up to neutral components. If
Y
is an Abelian variety, this is essentially the conjecture stated in the introduction of
[
30
]
. If
Y
is furthermore an ordinary principally polarized Abelian variety, one can actually define a characteristic
p
analogue of Mumford–Tate group via Hodge theory, and formulate a characteristic
p
analogue of the Mumford–Tate conjecture. Let
A_{g}
and
\mathcal{A}_{g}
be the Siegel modular variety and its canonical integral model, with suitable level structures. The following conjecture is already implicit in an early work of Chai (
[
4
, Conjecture 7.7]
):

Conjecture 1.1

(Characteristic
p
analogue of the Mumford–Tate conjecture for ordinary stratum of
\mathcal{A}_{g,\mathbb{F}_{p}}
)
.

Suppose
X_{0}
is a smooth geometric connected variety (or a finite generated field) over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
with a morphism
f_{0}
into
\mathcal{A}_{g,\mathbb{F}_{p}}
, whose image lies in the ordinary locus. Let
\mathscr{A}
be the pullback Abelian scheme over
X_{0}
. Then the
l
-adic étale resp. crystalline monodromy group of
\mathscr{A}
has the same neutral component with the base change to
\mathbb{Q}_{l}
resp.
\mathbb{Q}_{p}

of the generic Mumford–Tate group of the smallest special subvariety of

\mathcal{A}_{g}
whose mod
p
reduction contains the image of
f_{0}
.
In particular, the generic Mumford–Tate group of this special subvariety is a rational model over
\mathbb{Q}
of the
l
-adic étale and crystalline monodromy groups of
\mathscr{A}
.

Now we consider the characteristic
p
analogue of the André–Oort conjecture. Naïvely, one can formulate the conjecture as follows: if a subvariety
X
of the mod
p
reduction of a Shimura variety contains Zariski dense collection of special subvarieties
1
1

1

Here, a special subvariety is defined as the mod
p
reduction of a special subvariety in characteristic 0.
, then
X
is special. Unfortunately, this is not true: since every
\mathbb{F}
-point
2
2

2

\mathbb{F}
stands for an algebraic closure of
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
in the mod
p
reduction of a Shimura variety is already special, any positive dimensional subvariety contains a Zariski dense set of special points. To make it possibly true, one need to put extra conditions on the collection of special subvarieties. A natural condition that one can put is that the special subvarieties in the collection are positive dimensional. However, even this is not enough to guarantee that
X
is special, see Example
1.5
. Nevertheless, one can at least expect that
X
is “somewhat special”:

Conjecture 1.2

(Characteristic
p
analogue of the André–Oort conjecture for ordinary stratum of
\mathcal{A}_{g,\mathbb{F}_{p}}
)
.

Suppose
X\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{g,\mathbb{F}}
is a generically ordinary subvariety which contains a Zariski dense collection of positive dimensional special subvarieties, then
X
admits a positive dimensional special factor
3
3

3

This will be made precise in Definition
2
, and will be termed
quasi-weakly special
.
.

Conjecture
1.2
is not sharp in some cases. For example, one of our main results (Theorem
1.4
) shows that if
X
lies on a single GSpin Shimura variety, then a subvariety containing a Zariski dense collection of positive dimensional special subvarieties is already special. However, if
X
lies on a triple product of modular curves, or more generally, a triple product of Shimura varieties, one can not expect more, see again Example
1.5
.

Besides ours, there are other attempts of formulating characteristic
p
analogues of the André–Oort conjecture. In
[
18
]
, a “mod
p
special point” is defined to be the mod
p
reduction of the whole Galois orbit of a CM point (see also
[
43
]
). The authors then prove a mod
p
variant of André–Oort conjecture for product of two modular curves assuming GRH. Our formulation of the mod
p
analogue is very different from theirs.

1.1.
Main results

In this paper, we prove the above mentioned conjectures for products of GSpin Shimura varieties. In the following, a (locally closed) irreducible subvariety of
\mathcal{A}_{g,\mathbb{F}}
is said to be
special
, if its Zariski closure is a irreducible component of
the reduction of the Zariski closure in
\mathcal{A}_{g}
of a
special subvariety of Hodge type
in the sense of
[
35
, §1.1]
. Loosely speaking, a subvariety is special if it comes from a Shimura subvariety.

Let’s briefly explain what a GSpin Shimura variety is. Let
(L,Q)
be an even quadratic
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice which is self-dual at
p
and has signature
(2,b)
. Associated to it is a Shimura variety
\mathcal{S}
that admits a Hodge embedding into
A_{g}
. This Shimura variety is called the GSpin Shimura variety. Pulling back the universal Abelian scheme over
A_{g}
gives rise to an Abelian scheme over
\mathcal{S}
, called the Kuga–Satake Abelian scheme. In
[
29
]
, Kisin proved that after choosing a suitable level structure, there exists a canonical integral model
\mathscr{S}
of
\mathcal{S}
, together with an embedding of smooth integral models
\mathscr{S}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{g}
that extends the Hodge embedding
\mathcal{S}\hookrightarrow A_{g}
. The Kuga–Satake Abelian scheme over
\mathcal{S}
also extends to
\mathscr{S}
, which will be denote by
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}
.

Let
\mathbf{I}
be a finite index set. We associate to each
i\in\mathbf{I}
a GSpin Shimura variety
\mathcal{S}_{i}
and its canonical integral model
\mathscr{S}_{i}
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{I}}=\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\mathcal{S}_{i}
and
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}}=\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\mathscr{S}_{i}
. There is a Hodge embedding
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{g^{\prime}}
by gathering the Hodge embeddings over each index. We have proved the following:

Theorem 1.3

(Characteristic
p
analogue of the Mumford–Tate conjecture for ordinary strata of products of GSpin Shimura varieties)
.

Conjecture
1.1
is true if
f_{0}
factors through
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}_{p}}
.

In particular, if
Y
is an arbitrary product of principally polarized ordinary Abelian and K3 surfaces over a finite generated field
F/\mathbb{F}_{p}
, then the
l
-adic and crystalline monodromy groups of
Y
admit a rational model over
\mathbb{Q}
, which is (a quotient of
4
4

4

This is just because the monodromy of a K3 surface is a quotient of the monodromy its Kuga–Satake Abelian variety.
) the generic Mumford–Tate group of the smallest special subvariety of

\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{I}}
whose mod
p
reduction contains the image of
f_{0}:\Spec F\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}_{p}}
that corresponds to
Y
.

On the other hand, the characteristic
p
analogue of the André–Oort conjecture is a joint consequence of Theorem
1.3
and the Tate-linear conjecture (see Conjecture
1.8
, Theorem
1.9
). Let’s begin by stating the single GSpin case, which is most akin to the classical characteristic
0
story:

Theorem 1.4

(Characteristic
p
analogue of the André–Oort conjecture for ordinary strata of single GSpin Shimura varieties)
.

Suppose
X
is a generically ordinary closed subvariety of a single GSpin Shimura variety
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
that contains a Zariski dense collection of positive dimensional special subvarieties, then
X
is special. In particular, Conjecture
1.2
is true.

However, the characteristic
p
analogue of the André–Oort conjecture for products of GSpin Shimura varieties is much more subtle, even in the case of products of modular curves. As noted before, a subvariety containing a Zariski dense collection of positive special subvarieties may fail to be special. The author learned the following example from a conversation with Chai:

Example 1.5
.

Consider the case where
\mathbf{I}=\{1,2,3\}
and each
\mathcal{S}_{i}
is a modular curve. Let
C
be a generically ordinary non-special curve in
\mathscr{S}_{1,\mathbb{F}}\times\mathscr{S}_{2,\mathbb{F}}
and let
X=C\times\mathscr{S}_{3,\mathbb{F}}
. Since every point on
C
is special,
X
contains a Zariski dense collection of special curves
\{x\times\mathscr{S}_{3,\mathbb{F}}|x\in C(\mathbb{F})\}
. However,
X
is not special. More generally, a subvariety which is the product of a positive dimensional special subvariety with a nonspecial subvariety is nonspecial, while containing a Zariski dense collection of positive dimensional special subvarieties.

However, we show that Example
1.5
is the only obstruction towards having a special subvariety:

Theorem 1.6

(Characteristic
p
analogue of the André–Oort conjecture for ordinary strata of products of modular curves)
.

Suppose
X
is a generically ordinary closed subvariety of a product of modular curves
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}
that contains Zariski dense positive dimensional special subvarieties. Let
\mathbf{I}_{S}\subseteq\mathbf{I}
be the set of indices
i
such that
X
contains a Zariski dense collection of special subvarieties whose projections to
\mathscr{S}_{i,\mathbb{F}}
are
positive dimensional. Then
X
is the product of a special subvariety of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}_{S},\mathbb{F}}
and a subvariety of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{I}_{S},\mathbb{F}}
. In particular, Conjecture
1.2
is true.

For a single Shimura variety, group theory guarantees that the phenomena in Example
1.5
don’t happen. This is the reason why in Theorem
1.7
, the existence of a Zariski dense collection of positive dimensional special subvarieties is enough to guarantee the specialness of
X
. Now, we are ready to state the result for products of GSpin Shimura varieties:

Theorem 1.7

(Characteristic
p
analogue of the André–Oort conjecture for ordinary strata of products of GSpin Shimura varieties)
.

Suppose
X
is a generically ordinary closed subvariety of a product of GSpin Shimura varieties
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}
that contains a Zariski dense collection of positive dimensional special subvarieties, then Conjecture
1.2
is true. In fact, we proved a quantitative version similar to Theorem
1.6
that gives precise description of the special factor of
X
in terms of
\mathbf{I}_{S}
, which is too complicated to be stated here, but see Conjecture
4.10
for a precise version.

1.2.
Linearity of Shimura varieties and Tate-linear conjecture

Linearity is a fundamental concept which characterizes special subvarieties of a Shimura variety. It will play a crucial role in our treatment of the conjectures. Several different notions of linearity exist. We will give a brief review of them. For simplicity, in the following we consider linearity for subvarieties of
A_{g}
. This is already enough for dealing with Shimura subvarieties of Hodge type.

1.2.1.
Linearity in char 0

Consider the uniformization map
\pi:\mathbb{H}_{g}\rightarrow A_{g}
with deck group an arithmetic subgroup of
\text{GSp}_{2g}(\mathbb{Z})
. One can make sense of algebraic subvarieties of
\mathbb{H}_{g}
, cf.
[
45
, §3]
. A subvariety
V\subseteq A_{g}
is called bi-algebraic if
\pi^{-1}(V)
is algebraic. Since the morphism
\pi
is highly transcendental, bi-algebraic subvarieties are rare, and have very special properties. In fact, being bi-algebraic puts a strong linear condition on
V
(or
\pi^{-1}(V)
). This linearity can be understood better from more classical settings. Indeed, consider an Abelian variety
A
over
\mathbb{C}
with a uniformization map
e:\mathbb{C}^{n}\rightarrow A
. It is a classical consequence of Ax–Schanuel theorem that a irreducible subvariety
V\subseteq A
is bi-algebraic if and only if
V
is a translation of an Abelian subvariety, and in this case,
e^{-1}(V)
is a linear subspace of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. In the case of Shimura varieties, similar phenomena happen. As a consequence of
[
45
]
, a subvariety
V\subseteq A_{g}
is bi-algebraic if and only if
V
is a weakly special subvariety. In this case,
\pi^{-1}(V)
is a “linear subspace” of
\mathbb{H}_{g}
in the sense that it is totally geodesic. Indeed, a subvariety of a Euclidean space is linear if and only if it is totally geodesic. Therefore the property of being totally geodesic is a natural generalization of linearity in the classical sense.

The interpretation of linearity in terms of totally geodesic property can be found in much earlier works of Moonen. In
[
33
]
, Moonen showed that a subvariety of a Shimura variety is weakly special if and only if it is totally geodesic. In this and a subsequent paper
[
35
]
, Moonen also investigated the notion of “formal linearity”. To state it, we recall that Serre–Tate theory implies that the completion of
\mathcal{A}_{g}
at an ordinary mod
p
point admits the structure of a formal torus, cf.
[
24
]
. It is usually called
Serre–Tate formal torus
or
Serre–Tate formal coordinates
or
canonical coordinates
. A subvariety of
A_{g}
is called
formally linear
if the completion of its Zariski closure in
\mathcal{A}_{g}
at an ordinary mod
p
point is a union of torsion translates of subtori of the Serre–Tate formal torus. By an earlier result of Noot (
[
39
]
), special subvarieties of
A_{g}
are formally linear. In
[
35
]
, Moonen showed the converse, i.e., formal linearity characterizes special subvarieties.

1.2.2.
Linearity in char
p

Linearity in terms of bi-algebraicity or totally geodesic property doesn’t generalize easily to mod
p
Shimura varieties. However, formal linearity does generalize directly to the ordinary Newton stratum: a subvariety
V\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{g,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
is called formally linear, if its completion at an ordinary point is a subtorus of the (mod
p
) Serre–Tate formal torus. We will use Chai’s terminology, and call it
Tate-linear
at that point, cf.
[
4
, Definition 5.1]
. Tate-linearity appears naturally in Chai’s work on Hecke orbit conjecture, since the Zariski closure of an ordinary Hecke orbit is Tate-linear, see
[
5
]
. Note that Noot’s result (
[
39
]
) implies that a irreducible component of the mod
p
reduction of a special subvariety of
A_{g}
is Tate-linear at a smooth ordinary point, if the reduction is generically ordinary. It is natural to ask the converse: does Tate-linearity characterize mod
p
reductions of special subvarieties? This is very much the content of Chai’s Tate-linear conjecture:

Conjecture 1.8

(Tate-linear conjecture, see
[
4
, Conjecture 7.2, Remark 7.2.1, Proposition 5.3, Remark 5.3.1]
)
.

If a irreducible subvariety of
\mathcal{A}_{g,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
is Tate-linear at a point, then it is special.

The conjecture is still open. In §
1.2.3
, we will discuss our new progress on this conjecture.

So far we have only been taking about linearity for ordinary stratum. Linearity for higher Newton strata is much more subtle. This is because there is no notion of Serre–Tate coordinates for higher Newton strata. Nevertheless, there are generalizations of Serre–Tate formal torus to higher Newton strata, cf.
[
37
]
. We will refer the readers to Chai’s more recent paper
[
7
]
for linearity in a more general sense. We won’t be using linearity for higher Newton strata in this paper.

1.2.3.
Tate-linear conjecture

As a main ingredient of the proofs of characteristic
p
analogues of the Mumford–Tate conjecture and the André–Oort conjecture, we will establish the Tate-linear conjecture for products of GSpin Shimura varieties:

Theorem 1.9

(Tate-linear conjecture for products of GSpin Shimura varieties)
.

Let
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}
be a product of GSpin Shimura varieties, and let
X\subseteq\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
be an irreducible subvariety which is Tate-linear at a point. Then
X
is special. In particular, Conjecture
1.8
holds in this case.

In fact, we are able to show a stronger result and deduce Theorem
1.9
as a special case where
f
is a locally closed immersion and
\mathscr{T}_{f,x}=X^{/x}
:

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
X
be a smooth connected variety over
\mathbb{F}
that admits a morphism

f
into
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. Let
x
be an
\mathbb{F}
-point of
X
and
\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
be the smallest formal subtorus of the Serre–Tate torus
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}^{/x}
through which
f^{/x}
factors. Then there is a special subvariety whose formal germ at
f(x)
coincides with
\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
.

1.3.
Method and strategies

For simplicity, we only discuss the proof strategies of the conjectures for single GSpin Shimura varieties. Suppose
X_{0}
is a smooth geometric connected variety over a finite field with a morphism
f_{0}
into
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
, whose image lies in the ordinary locus.
Let
X,f
be the base change to
\mathbb{F}
of
X_{0},f_{0}
, and let

\eta
be the generic point of
X
. We first construct a reductive group
{\MTT}(f)
over
\mathbb{Q}
together with a representation
{\rho}_{f}:{\MTT}(f)\rightarrow\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{Q}})
. This group gives rise to the correct special subvariety for both Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.10
, and is also the correct characteristic
p
analogue of the Mumford–Tate group. The construction of
({\MTT}(f),{\rho}_{f})
can be summarized as follows:

Construction 1
.

Pick an
\mathbb{F}
-point
x
of
X
and let
\tilde{x}
be its canonical lift.
The endomorphism algebra of the Kuga–Satake Abelian variety
\mathscr{A}_{x}^{\KS}
is equal to the endomorphism algebra of the Kuga–Satake Abelian scheme
\mathscr{A}_{\tilde{x}}^{\KS}
. On the other hand, the endomorphism algebra of the Kuga–Satake Abelian variety
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\overline{\eta}}
is a subalgebra of the endomorphism algebra of
\mathscr{A}_{x}^{\KS}
. So
\End(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\overline{\eta}})
admits a lift to a subalgebra of
\End(\mathscr{A}_{\tilde{x}}^{\KS})
. As a result,
\End(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\overline{\eta}})
acts faithfully on
H_{\mathbb{Q}}
, the rational Hodge structure of
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}}
. Note that
\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{Q}})
also acts on
H_{\mathbb{Q}}
. We define
{\MTT}^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)
as the largest connected subgroup of
\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{Q}})
that commutes with
\End(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\overline{\eta}})
. It gives rise to a special subvariety
\mathcal{X}_{f}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
, whose generic Mumford–Tate group will be denoted by
\MTT(f)
(which in fact equals
{\MTT}^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)
). Let
{\rho}_{f}
be the embedding
\MTT(f)\subseteq\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{Q}})
.

The above construction only uses endomorphisms of the Kuga--Satake Abelian variety, but not higher motivic cycles
5
5

5

There is no theory of CM lift for higher motivic cycles. However, see
[
20
, §2]
for some conjectures.
. However, we still expect
{\MTT}(f)
to be the correct analogue of the Mumford–Tate group. The reason behind this is that the classical Mumford–Tate group for a number field valued point of a GSpin Shimura variety is already determined by endomorphisms of the Kuga–Satake Abelian variety (so one doesn’t need to consider higher Hodge tensors). This is a consequence of group theory and the classification of the Mumford–Tate group for K3 Hodge structures (cf.
[
48
]
), and is a special property of GSpin Shimura varieties.

1.3.1.
Proof strategies for the Tate-linear conjecture and the characteristic
p
analogue of the Mumford–Tate conjecture

If we pick the point
x
in Construction
1
to be the point
x
in Theorem
1.10
, then the special subvariety
\mathcal{X}_{f}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
is exactly the special subvariety that we are looking for in Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.10
. Using deformation theory, one can show that
f
factors through the mod
p
reduction of
\mathcal{X}_{f}
. The essential step is then to show that
\mathcal{X}_{f}
is cut out by enough motivic cycles, so that the formal germ of the mod
p
reduction of
\mathcal{X}_{f}
at
f(x)
admits
\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
as an irreducible component. This also implies that
\mathcal{X}_{f}
is the smallest special subvariety whose mod
p
reduction contains the image of
f
.

To show that
\mathcal{X}_{f}
is cut out by enough motivic cycles, we need a good understanding of the global monodromy group of a certain
p
-divisible group (namely, the formal Brauer group, see §
2.2
) over
X
. The structure of the local monodromy group of this
p
-divisible group is determined by
\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
. Using Chai’s results on local and global monodromy of
p
-divisible groups (cf.
[
4
, §3-4]
), the parabolicity conjecture proven by D’Addezio in
[
16
]
, and explicit group theory, we are able to largely understand the structure of this global monodromy group. We then use this to construct enough endomorphisms of the Kuga–Satake Abelian variety over
\overline{\eta}
. Using deformation theory, we are able to establish Theorem
1.10
.

To show Theorem
1.3
, it suffices to show that
\MTT(f)
is the correct rational model over
\mathbb{Q}
of the
l
-adic étale and crystalline monodromy groups. The structure of the global monodromy group that we mentioned in the last paragraph is closely related to the structure of
\MTT(f)
. Using the known structure of this global monodromy group, and independence of monodromy groups in a compatible system of coefficient objects (see for example
[
17
, Theorem 1.2.1]
), we are able to establish Theorem
1.3
.

1.3.2.
Proof strategies for the characteristic
p
analogue of the André–Oort conjecture

The main ingredients of the proof of the characteristic
p
analogue of the André–Oort conjecture are the Tate-linear conjecture, the characteristic
p
analogue of the Mumford–Tate conjecture, Chai’s rigidity theorem on formal tori (cf.
[
6
]
) and global Serre–Tate coordinates (cf.
[
4
, §2]
). We begin by a baby example
6
6

6

The author learned this from Ananth Shankar and Yunqing Tang
:

Example 1.11
.

Let
\mathscr{S}
be the Siegel modular variety
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, which is also a GSpin Shimura variety. This example shows that if a divisor
X\subseteq\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
contains Zariski dense positive dimensional special subvarieties that pass through the same ordinary
\mathbb{F}
-point
x
of
X
, then
X
is special. In fact, each special subvariety gives rise to a (union of) formal subtorus of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}}^{/x}
which is also contained in
X^{/x}
. One can show that these formal subtori are Zariski dense in
X^{/x}
. Since each subtorus is invariant under scaling by
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}
,
X^{/x}
is also invariant under scaling by
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}
. By Chai’s rigidity result,
X^{/x}
is a formal subtorus of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}}^{/x}
. Theorem
1.9
then implies that
X
is special.

In general, the Zariski dense collection of positive dimensional special subvarieties of
X
may not pass through the same point. Even if they do, the collection of formal subtori thus arise may not be Zariski dense in
X^{/x}
. Hence
the strategy in Example
1.11
won’t work. Instead, we use the Zariski dense collection of special subvarieties to construct certain arithmetic
p
-adic étale lisse sheaves on (an étale open subset of)
X
. The construction can be summarized as follows:

Construction 2
.

Consider
(X\times\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}})^{/\Delta}
, where
\Delta
is the graph of the immersion
X\subseteq\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
. For each special subvariety
Z\subseteq X
, we consider
(Z\times Z)^{/\Delta}
, where
\Delta
stands for the diagonal. We then take the Zariski closure of the union of all
(Z\times Z)^{/\Delta}
inside
(X\times\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}})^{/\Delta}
, and call it
\mathfrak{Z}
. Now Chai’s theory of global Serre–Tate coordinates implies that
(X\times\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}})^{/\Delta}
is a lisse family of formal tori over
X
, and
(Z\times Z)^{/\Delta}
is a lisse family of formal tori over
Z
. The scaling-by-
\mathbb{Z}^{*}_{p}
map on
(X\times\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}})^{/\Delta}
preserves each
(Z\times Z)^{/\Delta}
, hence
\mathfrak{Z}
. Let
\eta
be the generic point of
X
, we use Chai’s rigidity result on formal tori to show that
\mathfrak{Z}\times_{X}\overline{\eta}
is a union of formal tori. Each irreducible component then gives rise to a
p
-adic lisse sheaf over an étale open subset of
X
. These are essentially
p
-adic lisse sheaves that we want.

Since
\mathfrak{Z}\subseteq(X\times X)^{/\Delta}
, a special feature of the
p
-adic lisse sheaf
\mathscr{F}
constructed as above is that
\mathscr{F}_{x}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}\subseteq X^{/x}
for any
x\in X(\mathbb{F})
. We use the Theorem
1.3
and representation theory to show that above inclusion is an equality. This will imply that
X^{/x}
is a formal torus, and Theorem
1.9
will imply Theorem
1.7
.

For products of GSpin Shimura varieties, the construction of the
p
-adic lisse sheaves is essentially the same as above. However, the representation theory is different, so one cannot deduce that
\mathscr{F}_{x}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}=X^{/x}
as in the GSpin case. Of course, this meets our expectation, since the existence of Zariski dense positive special subvarieties doesn’t guarantee that
X
is special, as in Example
1.5
. The following is a concrete example for the construction of the
p
-adic lisse sheaf for a triple product of modular curves:

Example 1.12
.

Let
X
be as in Example
1.5
. We replace
X
by its ordinary stratum. Consider the projection
\pi_{3}:X\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{3,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. Then
\mathfrak{Z}
in Construction
2
is nothing other than the pullback via
\pi_{3}
of
(\mathscr{S}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{3,\mathbb{F}}\times\mathscr{S}_{3,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}})^{/\Delta}
. It is a family of rank 1 formal tori over
X
. The arithmetic
p
-adic lisse sheaf
\mathscr{F}
thus arise is the pullback via
\pi_{3}
of the obvious
p
-adic lisse sheaf over
\mathscr{S}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{3,\mathbb{F}}
arising from the
p
-adic étale cohomology of the universal family. Note that for any point
x\in X(\mathbb{F})
,
\mathscr{F}_{x}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}\subsetneq X^{/x}
is a strict inclusion.

1.4.
Organization of the paper

In §
2
we study the arithmetic completion of GSpin Shimura varieties. In §
3
we review previous works on monodromy of
F
-isocrystals. In §
4
we construct
\MTT(f)
and
\mathcal{X}_{f}
. We also intoduce the notion of quasi-weakly special subvarieties. We establish a bunch of properties about these objects, and use them to state precise versions of the conjectures. In §
5
we establish several group theoretical lemmas. In §
6
we prove the Tate-linear and characteristic
p
analogue of the Mumford–Tate conjecture for single GSpin Shimura varieties. In §
7
we prove the Tate-linear and characteristic
p
analogue of the Mumford–Tate conjecture for products of GSpin Shimura varieties.
In §
8
we prove the characteristic
p
analogue of the André–Oort conjecture for products of GSpin Shimura varieties.

1.5.
Notations and conventions

We use
p
to denote an odd prime and
q
to denote a positive power of
p
. We fix an algebraic closure of
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, and denote it by
\mathbb{F}
. We denote by
W
the ring of Witt vectors of
\mathbb{F}
. We fix an embedding
W\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{p}
, the completion of
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. The bold-case letters
\mathbf{I},\mathbf{J}
are reserved for denoting finite index sets. We also make the following conventions:

•

(Algebraic closures)
We fix once and for all an identification of
{\mathbb{C}}_{p}
with
\mathbb{C}
. As a result, we have fixed an embedding of
W
into
\mathbb{C}
.

•

(Tori and formal tori) We use
\mathbb{G}_{m}

resp
.
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}
to denote the the rank 1 torus
resp
. formal torus over
W
,
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
or
\mathbb{F}
, depending on the context. Sometimes we will also write
\mathbb{G}_{m,W}
,
\mathbb{G}_{m,\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
and
\mathbb{G}_{m,\mathbb{F}}
to emphasize the base scheme.

•

(Formal completions) Suppose
\mathscr{Y}
is a
W
-scheme and
y\in\mathscr{Y}(\mathbb{F})
. We write

{\mathscr{Y}}^{/y}
for the completion of
\mathscr{Y}
along
y
. If
Y
is a
\mathbb{F}
-scheme and
Z
is a closed subscheme, we write
Y^{/Z}
for the completion of
Y
along
Z
. If
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a morphism of varieties over
\mathbb{F}
and
x\in X(\mathbb{F})
, we write
f^{/x}:X^{/x}\rightarrow Y^{/x}
for the completion of
f
at
x
. Note that
Y^{/x}
actually stands for
Y^{/f(x)}
.

2.
Preliminaries

This section concerns the background results. In §
2.1
and §
2.2
we review the notion of GSpin Shimura varieties and formal Brauer groups on their special fibers. In §
2.3
and §
2.4
, we establish a canonical isomorphism between the arithmetic completion of a GSpin Shimura variety at an ordinary point and the arithmetic deformation of the extended formal Brauer group. We show that the isomorphism preserves the formal group structures.

2.1.
Definitions and first properties

We review the definitions, notations, and basic properties of GSpin Shimura varieties, following
[
42
]
,
[
32
]
and
[
2
]
. Let
p\geq 3
be a prime. For an integer
b\geq 1
, let
(L,Q)
be a quadratic
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice of rank
b+2
and signature
(2,b)
with a bilinear form
(,):L\otimes L\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

such that for
x\in L
,
Q(x)=(x,x)/2\in\mathbb{Z}
, and that
(L,Q)
is self dual at
p
. Let
\GSpin(L\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(p)},Q)
be the group of spinor similitude of
L\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
, which is a reductive group over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
, and a subgroup of
\Cl(L\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})^{\times}
, where
\Cl(\cdot)
is the Clifford algebra. The group
\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{R}})
acts on the symmetric space

\mathcal{D}_{L}=\{z\in\mathbb{P}(L_{\mathbb{C}})|(z,z)=0,(z,\overline{z})<0\}

via
c:\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\SO(L_{\mathbb{Q}})
. This gives rise to a Shimura datum
(\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{Q}}),\mathcal{D})
with refex field
\mathbb{Q}
. Consider a hyperspecial level structure
\mathbb{K}\subseteq\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{A}_{f}})\cap\Cl(L_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}})^{\times}
, i.e. a compact open subgroup such that
\mathbb{K}_{p}=\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}})
. Then we have a Deligne-Mumford stack
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}h(\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{Q}}),\mathcal{D}_{L})_{\mathbb{K}}
over
\mathbb{Q}
, called the GSpin Shimura variety, with
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{C})=\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{Q}})\backslash\mathcal{D}_{L}\times\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{A}_{f}})/\mathbb{K}
.

In what follows, the level structure
\mathbb{K}
is always chosen to be sufficiently small and, when clear from the context, will be dropped from the subscript. Let
H=\Cl(L)
, with the action of itself on the right. Equip
\Cl(L)
with the action of
\GSpin(L)
on the left. There exists a choice of symplectic form on
H
that gives rise to a map
\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\GSp(H_{\mathbb{Q}})
. This induces an embedding of Shimura data, hence an embedding of
\mathcal{S}
into the corresponding Siegel modular variety
A_{g}
, called the
Kuga–Satake embedding
.

The canonical integral model of
\mathcal{S}
is the Zariski closure of
\mathcal{S}
in
\mathcal{A}_{g}
, the canonical integral model of
A_{g}
(
[
29
,
47
]
). The canonical integral model will be denoted by
\mathscr{S}
. It is a smooth scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
. Pulling back the universal Abelian scheme over
\mathcal{A}_{g}
yields a Kuga–Satake Abelian scheme
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
with left
\Cl(L)
-action, whose first
\mathbb{Z}
-coefficient Betti cohomology is the local system induced by
H
. Let
\mathbf{H}_{\text{B}},\mathbf{H}_{\text{dR}},\mathbf{H}_{l,\text{\'{e}t}}
be the integral Betti, de Rham,
l
-adic étale (
l\neq p
) relative first cohomology of
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
, and let
\mathbf{H}_{\text{cris}}
represent the first integral crystalline cohomology of
\mathscr{A}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}^{\KS}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
.

The natural action of
L
on
H
produces a
\GSpin(L)
invariant embedding
L\hookrightarrow\End_{\Cl(L)}(H)
. Correspondingly, for each
\bullet=

\text{B},\text{dR},\{l,\text{\'{e}t}\}
and cris, there is a local system
\mathbf{L}_{\bullet}
. The local system is equipped with a natural quadratic form
\mathbf{Q}
such that
f\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}f=\mathbf{Q}(f)\Id
for a section
f
of
\mathbf{L}_{\bullet}
. It also admits an embedding
\mathbf{L}_{\bullet}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}nd_{\Cl(L)}(\mathbf{H}_{\bullet})
, which is compatible with various
p
-adic Hodge theoretic comparison maps, see
[
2
, §4.3]
and
[
32
, Proposition 3.11, 3.12, 4.7]
. To simultaneously handle
l
-adic and crystalline local systems, we utilize the following convention: let
u
be a finite place of
\mathbb{Q}
, then

(2.1.1)

\mathbf{L}_{u}:=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathbf{L}_{l,{\text{\'{e}t}}},\,\,&u=l,\\
&\mathbf{L}_{{\mathrm{cris}}},\,\,&u=p.\end{aligned}\right.

We adopt the same notation convention for
\mathbf{H}_{u}
. We will use the symbols
\mathbb{H}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
to denote the rational local systems corresponding to
\mathbf{H}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{L}_{\bullet}
.

Remark 2.1
.

Modular curve, Hilbert modular surfaces and Siegel modular variety
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are special cases of GSpin Shimura varieties when
b=1,2,3
, respectively.

Remark 2.2
.

A natural isomorphism exists between the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces
\wedge L_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\Cl(L_{\mathbb{Q}})
. This can be realized by selecting an orthogonal basis
\{e_{1},...,e_{b+2}\}
of
L_{\mathbb{Q}}
, and identifying
e_{i_{1}}\wedge e_{i_{2}}\wedge...\wedge e_{i_{k}}
with
e_{i_{1}}e_{i_{2}}...e_{i_{k}}
(it is independent of the choice of the orthogonal basis). The natural action of
\wedge L_{\mathbb{Q}}
on
H_{\mathbb{Q}}
results in a
\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{Q}})
invariant embedding
\wedge L_{\mathbb{Q}}\hookrightarrow\End_{\Cl(L_{\mathbb{Q}})}(H_{\mathbb{Q}})
. As a consequence, there is a natural embedding of local systems
\wedge\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}nd_{\Cl(L)}(\mathbb{H}_{\bullet})
.

2.2.
Ordinary locus of the mod
p
fiber

Notation being the same as §
2.1
. Let
\mathscr{S}
be the canonical integral model of a GSpin Shimura variety. An
\mathbb{F}
point
x
in the special fiber
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
is called
ordinary
, if the Kuga–Satake Abelian variety
\mathscr{A}_{x}^{\KS}
is an ordinary Abelian variety. Another equivalent condition would be if the
F
-crystal
7
7

7

Strictly speaking,
{\mathbf{L}}_{p,x}
is not an
F
-crystal, whereas
{\mathbf{L}}_{p,x}(1)
is. Nevertheless, we will still call
{\mathbf{L}}_{p,x}
an
F
-crystal.

\mathbf{L}_{p,x}
has slopes -1, 0, and 1. The ordinary locus
\mathscr{S}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{\mathbb{F}}
is a Zariski open dense subset of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
.

2.2.1.
Formal Brauer groups

By
[
25
, Theorem 2.4.2]
, the
F
-isocrystal
\mathbf{L}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}:=\mathbf{L}_{p}|_{\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}}
admits a slope filtration

\Fil_{-1}\mathbf{L}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\subseteq\Fil_{0}\mathbf{L}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\subseteq\Fil_{1}\mathbf{L}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}=\mathbf{L}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}.

Let
\mathbf{D}
be the (contravariant) crystalline Dieudonne functor over
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. By
[
10
, Theorem 1]
, there exists a rank
b+1
ordinary
p
-divisible group
\Psi
on
\mathscr{S}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{\mathbb{F}}
, with
\formalBr:=\Psi^{\loc}
, such that

\mathbf{D}(\Psi)=\mathbf{L}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}/\Fil_{-1}\mathbf{L}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ord}}},\,\,\,\mathbf{D}(\formalBr)=\mathbf{L}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}/\Fil_{0}\mathbf{L}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}.

We also have
\mathbf{D}(\formalBr)^{\vee}=\Fil_{-1}\mathbf{L}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
from the existence of the pairing
\mathbf{Q}
.

2.3.
Arithmetic deformation theory at an ordinary point

Notation being the same as §
2.1
. Let
x\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{F})
. We denote by
\Def(\mathscr{A}_{x}^{\KS}[p^{\infty}]/W)
the universal deformation space of the the
p
-divisible group
\mathscr{A}_{x}^{\KS}[p^{\infty}]
. There is a Hodge cocharacter
{\mu}_{\mathbb{F}}:\mathbb{G}_{m,\mathbb{F}}\rightarrow\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{F}})
splitting the Hodge filtration of
\mathscr{A}_{x}^{\KS}
. Pick an arbitrary lifting of
\mu_{\mathbb{F}}
to a cocharacter
\tilde{\mu}_{W}:\mathbb{G}_{m,W}\rightarrow\GSpin(L_{W})
. Let
U_{\GSpin,\tilde{\mu}_{W}^{-1}}
be the unipotent group corresponding to the inverse cocharacter
\tilde{\mu}_{W}^{-1}
. It coincides with the opposite unipotent group corresponding to
\tilde{\mu}_{W}
. From
[
32
, §4]
, we have

(2.3.1)

\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}=\widehat{U}_{\GSpin,\tilde{\mu}_{W}^{-1}}\subseteq\Def(\mathscr{A}_{x}^{\KS}[p^{\infty}]/W),

where
\widehat{U}_{\GSpin,\tilde{\mu}_{W}^{-1}}
is the completion at the identity section of
U_{\GSpin,\tilde{\mu}_{W}^{-1}}
. If
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}}:=\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\mathscr{S}_{i}
is a finite product of integral GSpin Shimura varieties and
\mathscr{A}_{\mathbf{I},x}^{\KS}:=\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\mathscr{A}_{i}^{\KS}
is the product Kuga–Satake Abelian scheme. Pick a point
x\in\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}}(\mathbb{F})
and let
S\subseteq\End(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{I},x}[p^{\infty}])
. Denote by
\Def(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{I},x}[p^{\infty}],S/{W})
the subspace of
\Def(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{I},x}[p^{\infty}]/{W})
parametrizing deformations of
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{I},x}[p^{\infty}]
such that the endomorphisms within
S
also deform. We define

(2.3.2)

\Def(S/\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{\mathbf{I},W}):=\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{\mathbf{I},W}\cap\Def(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{I},x}[p^{\infty}],S/{W}).

In this section, we will show that

Proposition 2.3
.

If
x
is ordinary, then there is a canonical isomorphism

\pi_{x}:\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}\simeq\Def(\Psi_{x}/W)
.

See §
2.3.3
for the definition of
\pi_{x}
. Proposition
2.3
can be seen as a generalization of
[
40
, Theorem 1.6]
.

2.3.1.
The canonical Hodge cocharacter

Let
\mathscr{G}
be an ordinary
p
-divisible group over
\mathbb{F}
, and let
\mathscr{G}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}
,
\mathscr{G}^{\loc}
be its étale and local part. Let
F
be the Frobenius on the Dieudonné module
\mathbf{D}(\mathscr{G})
. Define a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-module

(2.3.3)

\omega(\mathscr{G})=\{v\in\mathbf{D}(\mathscr{G})|Fv=v\text{ or }pv\}.

There are canonical identifications

(2.3.4)

\omega(\mathscr{G}^{\loc})=X^{*}(\mathscr{G}^{\loc}),\,\,\,\omega(\mathscr{G}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}})=T_{p}(\mathscr{G}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}})^{\vee},

where the symbols
X^{*}
and
T_{p}
stand for the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-lattices of character and
p
-adic Tate module. Note that
\omega(\mathscr{G})=\omega(\mathscr{G}^{\loc})\oplus\omega(\mathscr{G}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}})
. The
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-module
\omega(\mathscr{G})
is a
canonical

\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-structure of
\mathbf{D}(\mathscr{G})
, in the sense that
\mathbf{D}(\mathscr{G})=\omega(\mathscr{G})\otimes W
and

(2.3.5)

F=\begin{bmatrix}\Id_{\omega(\mathscr{G}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}})}&0\\
0&p\cdot\Id_{\omega(\mathscr{G}^{\loc})}\end{bmatrix}\sigma.

Define the
canonical Hodge cocharacter
as

(2.3.6)

\mu:\mathbb{G}_{m,\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\rightarrow\GL(\omega(\mathscr{G})),\,\,\,t\rightarrow\begin{bmatrix}\Id_{\omega(\mathscr{G}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}})}&0\\
0&t\cdot\Id_{\omega(\mathscr{G}^{\loc})}\end{bmatrix}.

Let
x
be an ordinary point of
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{F})
and
\mathscr{G}=\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{x}[p^{\infty}]
. We can identify
\omega(\mathscr{G})
with the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-lattice
H_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
, the corresponding canonical Hodge cocharacter
\mu:\mathbb{G}_{m,\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\rightarrow\GL(H_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}})
lands in
\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}})
, and serves as a canonical lift of the Hodge cocharacter
\mu_{\mathbb{F}}
associated to
x
. The scalar extension
\mu_{W}
is also refered to as the canonical Hodge cocharacter. The cocharacters
\mu
and
\mu_{W}
induce filtrations
\Fil^{\bullet}H_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
and
\Fil^{\bullet}\mathbf{H}_{p,x}
, respectively. These will be called the
canonical Hodge filtrations
. Consequently, the Dieudonné module
\mathbf{H}_{p,x}
is equipped with a canonical
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-structure:

\mathbf{H}_{p,x}=(\mathbf{H}_{p,x}(W),\Fil^{\bullet}\mathbf{H}_{p,x},F_{x})=(H_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}},\Fil^{\bullet}H_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}},\mu(p))\otimes W.

The Hodge filtration of the canonical lifting of
x
is exactly the canonical Hodge filtration induced by
\mu_{W}
. More details pertaining to the theory of canonical liftings can be found in
[
44
]
.

Define
\mu^{c}
as the composition of
\mu
with the projection
c:\GSpin(L_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})\rightarrow\SO(L_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})
. This subsequently gives rise to a three-step filtration
\Fil^{\bullet}{L}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
. The cocharacters
\mu^{c}
and
\mu_{W}^{c}
are again called the canonical Hodge cocharacters, whereas the induced filtrations
\Fil^{\bullet}{L}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
and
\Fil^{\bullet}\mathbf{L}_{p,x}
are again termed as the canonical Hodge filtrations.

On the other hand, the inverse cocharacters
\mu^{-1}
and
\mu^{-1}_{W}
(
resp
.
\mu^{c,-1}
and
\mu^{c,-1}_{W}
) induce the
slope filtrations

\Fil_{\bullet}H_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
and
\Fil_{\bullet}\mathbf{H}_{p,x}
(
resp
.
\Fil_{\bullet}L_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
and
\Fil_{\bullet}\mathbf{L}_{p,x}
). These are ascending filtrations that should not be confused with the canonical Hodge filtrations.

2.3.2.
The
F
-crystals
\widehat{\mathbf{H}}_{p,x}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{L}}_{p,x}

Let
x\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{F})
be an ordinary point. We use the theory of explicit deformation of
p
-divisible groups – as developed in

[
21
, §7]
,
[
36
, §4]
and
[
29
, §1.4-§1.5]
– to describe several important crystals over
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}
.

We start by considering
\widehat{\mathbf{H}}_{p,x}
, the Dieudonné crystal of
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}[p^{\infty}]\times_{\mathscr{S}}\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}
. Recall that we have a canonical Hodge cocharacter
\mu:\mathbb{G}_{m,\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\rightarrow\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}})
associated to
x
. Let
U_{\GSpin,{\mu}^{-1}}
be the opposite unipotent of
\mu
in
\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}})
, and let
\widehat{U}_{\GSpin,{\mu}^{-1}}
be its completion at the identity section. If we let
\tilde{\mu}_{W}=\mu_{W}
in (
2.3.1
), we then obtain

(2.3.7)

\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}\simeq\widehat{U}_{\GSpin,{\mu}^{-1},W}.

Denote by
R=\mathcal{O}(\widehat{U}_{\GSpin,{\mu}^{-1},W})
the ring of formal functions with a choice of Frobenius
\varphi
. Let
\Fil^{\bullet}\mathbf{H}_{p,x}
be the canonical Hodge filtration associated to
\mu_{W}
. Consider the module
\mathbf{H}_{R}:=\mathbf{H}_{p,x}\otimes_{W}R
, which is equipped with a filtration
\Fil^{\bullet}\mathbf{H}_{R}:=\Fil^{\bullet}\mathbf{H}_{p,x}\otimes_{W}R
and a Frobenius
F_{R}:=u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(F_{x}\otimes\varphi)
, where
F_{x}
is the Frobenius on
\mathbf{H}_{p,x}
and
u
is the tautological element of
\widehat{U}_{\GSpin,{\mu}^{-1}}(R)
. The results from
[
36
, §4]
guarantees the existence of a unique connection
\nabla_{R}
over
\mathbf{H}_{R}
such that

(2.3.8)

\widehat{\mathbf{H}}_{p,x}\simeq(\mathbf{H}_{R},\Fil^{\bullet}\mathbf{H}_{R},\nabla_{R},F_{R}).

We also note the readers that
\widehat{\mathbf{H}}_{p,x}
is equipped with a slope filtration
\Fil_{\bullet}\mathbf{H}_{p,x}\otimes_{W}R
.

We then give a construction of
\widehat{\mathbf{L}}_{p,x}
, the universal K3 crystal over
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}
. Let
\bm{\pi}_{{\mathrm{cris}},x}\in\mathbf{H}_{p,x}^{\otimes(2,2)}
be the crystalline tensor as per
[
32
, Proposition 4.7]
. The constructions in
[
36
, §4.8]
imply that
\mathbf{H}_{R}
is equipped with a constant Hodge tensor
\bm{\pi}_{R}=\bm{\pi}_{{\mathrm{cris}},x}\otimes 1\in\mathbf{H}_{R}^{\otimes(2,2)}
which is horizontal and
F_{R}
-invariant, and furthermore lies in
\Fil^{0}\mathbf{H}_{R}^{\otimes(2,2)}
. Viewing
\bm{\pi}_{R}
as an idempotent operator over
\mathbf{H}_{R}^{\otimes(1,1)}
, we define
\mathbf{L}_{R}:=\bm{\pi}_{R}\mathbf{H}_{R}^{\otimes(1,1)}
. Clearly,
\mathbf{L}_{R}
is a direct summand of
\mathbf{H}_{R}^{\otimes(1,1)}
, and coincides with
\mathbf{L}_{p,x}\otimes_{W}R
. Let
\Fil^{\bullet}\mathbf{L}_{p,x}
be the canonical Hodge filtration over
\mathbf{L}_{p,x}
. Define a filtration on
\mathbf{L}_{R}
by
\Fil^{\bullet}\mathbf{L}_{R}:=\Fil^{\bullet}\mathbf{L}_{p,x}\otimes_{W}R
. Since
\bm{\pi}_{R}
is horizontal,
\mathbf{L}_{R}
is stable under the connection
\nabla_{R}^{c}
over
\mathbf{H}_{R}^{\otimes(1,1)}
induced from
\nabla_{R}
. Furthermore, we define a Frobenius
F_{R}^{c}
on
\mathbf{L}_{R}
by

F_{R}^{c}:=u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(F_{x}^{c}\otimes\varphi)
, where
F_{x}^{c}
is the Frobenius on
\mathbf{L}_{p,x}
. Then
\mathbf{L}_{R}[p^{-1}]
is invariant under
F^{c}_{R}
. Putting all of these together, we define

(2.3.9)

\widehat{\mathbf{L}}_{p,x}:=(\mathbf{L}_{R},\Fil^{\bullet}\mathbf{L}_{R},\nabla_{R}^{c},F_{R}^{c}).

Again,
\widehat{\mathbf{L}}_{p,x}
admits an embedding into
\mathcal{E}nd_{\Cl(L)}(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}_{p,x})
and is equipped with a paring
\widehat{\mathbf{Q}}_{x}
. We further note that the slope filtration of
\widehat{\mathbf{L}}_{p,x}
is
\Fil_{\bullet}\mathbf{L}_{p,x}\otimes_{W}R
.

2.3.3.
Defintion of
\pi_{x}

The slope -1 submodule
\Fil_{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{L}}_{p,x}
is preserved under the Frobenius and connection of
\widehat{\mathbf{L}}_{p,x}
. So the quotient
\widehat{\overline{\mathbf{L}}}_{p,x}=\widehat{\mathbf{L}}_{p,x}/\Fil_{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{L}}_{p,x}
is again a Frobenius module with connection. There is a two-step descending filtration over
\widehat{\overline{\mathbf{L}}}_{p,x}
defined as

\widehat{\overline{\mathbf{L}}}_{p,x}\supseteq\Fil^{1}\widehat{\overline{\mathbf{L}}}_{p,x}:=\widehat{\mathbf{L}}_{p,x}/\Fil_{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{L}}_{p,x}.

It is easy to check that
\widehat{\overline{\mathbf{L}}}_{p,x}
is a Dieudonné crystal over
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}
. By
[
10
, Theorem 1]
,
\widehat{\overline{\mathbf{L}}}_{p,x}
is the Dieudonné crystal of a formal
p
-divisible group
\widehat{\Psi}_{x}
over
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}
which deforms
\Psi_{x}
. Clearly,
\widehat{\Psi}_{x}
induces a morphism of formal schemes

(2.3.10)

\pi_{x}:\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}\rightarrow\Def(\Psi_{x}/W)

via which
\widehat{\Psi}_{x}
is the pullback of the universal bundle over
\Def(\Psi_{x}/W)
.

2.3.4.
Finale

Recall that there is a slope filtration

\Fil_{\bullet}L_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}
induced by
\mu^{c,-1}
. Let
\overline{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}=L_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}/\Fil_{-1}L_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}
. We denote by
U_{\SO,{\mu}^{c}}

resp
.
U_{\SO,{\mu}^{c,-1}}
the unipotent
resp
. opposite unipotent of
\mu^{c}
in
\SO(L_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}})
. Similarly, write
U_{\GL,\overline{\mu}}

resp
.
U_{\GL,\overline{\mu}^{-1}}
for the unipotent
resp
. opposite unipotent of
\overline{\mu}
in
\GL(\overline{L}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}})
. We will use
r
to denote both of the natural maps
U_{\SO,{\mu}^{c}}\rightarrow U_{\GL,\overline{\mu}}
and
U_{\SO,{\mu}^{c,-1}}\rightarrow U_{\GL,\overline{\mu}^{-1}}
. We omit the proof of the following easy group theory lemma:

Lemma 2.4
.

The morphisms
r
and
c
induce the following chains of isomorphisms:

(1)

U_{\GSpin,\mu^{-1}}\xrightarrow{c}U_{\SO,\mu^{c,-1}}\xrightarrow{r}U_{\GL,\overline{\mu}^{-1}}
,

(2)

U_{\GSpin,\mu}\xrightarrow{c}U_{\SO,\mu^{c}}\xrightarrow{r}U_{\GL,\overline{\mu}}
.

Proof of Proposition
2.3
.
We decompose
\pi_{x}
according to the following commuting diagram:

(2.3.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Def(\Psi_{x}/W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{U}_{\GSpin,{\mu}^{-1},W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{U}_{\SO,{\mu}^{c,-1},W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{U}_{\GL,{\overline{\mu}}^{-1},W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{r}}

The result follows from Lemma
2.4
(1).
\hfill\square

2.4.
Canonical coordinates

The theory of canonical coordinates implies that the deformation spaces
\Def(\mathscr{A}_{x}^{\KS}[p^{\infty}]/W)
and
\Def(\Psi_{x}/W)
both admit structures of formal tori. In this section, we will show that
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}
is a formal subtorus of
\Def(\mathscr{A}_{x}^{\KS}[p^{\infty}]/W)
. Furthermore, with this induced subtorus structure on
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}
, the morphism

\pi_{x}
in (
2.3.11
) is an isomorphism of formal tori.

2.4.1.
Canonical coordinates on the deformation spaces of ordinary
p
-divisible groups

Let
\mathscr{G}
be an ordinary
p
-divisible group over
\mathbb{F}
. We denote by
\mathscr{G}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}
and
\mathscr{G}^{\loc}
its étale and local part, respectively. Let
\omega(\mathscr{G})
,
\omega(\mathscr{G}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}})
,
\omega(\mathscr{G}^{\loc})
and
\mu
be the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-lattices and the canonical Hodge cocharacter defined in §
2.3.1
. Then the formal deformation space
\Def(\mathscr{G}/W)
admits the structure of a formal torus:

(2.4.1)

\displaystyle\Def(\mathscr{G}/W)\simeq

\displaystyle U_{\GL,\mu}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m,W}^{\wedge}

(2.4.2)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\left(U_{\GL,\mu^{-1}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p}),\mathbb{G}_{m,W}^{\wedge}\right).

Here the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-algebraic groups
U_{\GL,\mu},U_{\GL,\mu^{-1}}
are the unipotent and opposite unipotent of
\mu
in
\GL(\omega(\mathscr{G}))
. Note that
U_{\GL,\mu}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
and
U_{\GL,\mu^{-1}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
can be canonically identified with the cocharacter and character lattices of
\Def(\mathscr{G}/W)
. In the same spirit as (
2.3.4
), we can also canonically identify

U_{\GL,\mu}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})

resp
.
U_{\GL,\mu^{-1}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
with the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-linear space
X_{*}(\mathscr{G}^{\loc})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}T_{p}(\mathscr{G}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}})^{\vee}

resp
.
X^{*}(\mathscr{G}^{\loc})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}T_{p}(\mathscr{G}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}})
, where
X_{*}
stands for the cocharacter lattice.

The identifications (
2.4.1
)
\sim
(
2.4.2
) will be called the
canonical coordinates
over
\Def(\mathscr{G}/W)
. The group law of the formal torus comes from Baer sums of the extensions. The unique element in
\Def(\mathscr{G}/W)
corresponding to identity will be called the
canonical lifting
. For a formal
W
-algebra
R
and a
p
-divisible group
{\widehat{\mathscr{G}}}
over
\Spf R
deforming
\mathscr{G}
, (
2.4.2
) yields a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-linear map

(2.4.3)

q_{\widehat{\mathscr{G}}}:U_{\GL,\mu^{-1}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\simeq X^{*}(\mathscr{G}^{\loc})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}T_{p}(\mathscr{G}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}})\rightarrow\mathbb{G}^{\wedge}_{m,W}(R),

which is termed as
canonical pairing
. If
\mathscr{G}
is equipped with a family of endomorphisms
S
such that each
s\in S
decomposes as

s^{\loc}\times s^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}
, then
S
deforms to a family of endomorphisms of
{\widehat{\mathscr{G}}}
if and only if
q_{\widehat{\mathscr{G}}}
fixes every
s\in S
. More precisely,
S
deforms to
{\widehat{\mathscr{G}}}
if and only if

(2.4.4)

q_{\widehat{\mathscr{G}}}(s^{\loc}x\otimes y)=q_{\widehat{\mathscr{G}}}(x\otimes s^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}y),\text{ for all }x\in X^{*}(\mathscr{G}^{\loc}),y\in T_{p}(\mathscr{G}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}),s\in S.

Therefore, there is a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-sublattice
\Lambda_{S}\subseteq U_{\GL,\mu^{-1}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
such that

\Def(\mathscr{G},S/W)=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\left(U_{\GL,\mu^{-1}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})/\Lambda_{S},\mathbb{G}_{m,W}^{\wedge}\right).

Let
\overline{\Lambda_{S}}\subseteq U_{\GL,\mu^{-1}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
be the saturation of
\Lambda_{S}
. Then
U_{\GL,\mu^{-1}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})/\Lambda_{S}
decomposes as a direct sum of the free
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-module
U_{\GL,\mu^{-1}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})/\overline{\Lambda_{S}}
and the torsion
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-module

\overline{\Lambda_{S}}/\Lambda_{S}
. It follows that

(2.4.5)

\Def(\mathscr{G},S/W)=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(U_{\GL,\mu^{-1}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})/\overline{\Lambda_{S}},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge})\times\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\overline{\Lambda_{S}}/\Lambda_{S},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}).

Here the left hand side is a formal subtorus of
\Def(\mathscr{G}/W)
, while the right hand side is a finite flat subgroup scheme. In particular, the induced reduced structure
\Def(\mathscr{G},S/W)_{\red}
is a formal subtorus of
\Def(\mathscr{G}/W)
. The following lemma is well-known, whose proof is left to the readers:

Lemma 2.5
.

Recall that
U_{\GL,\mu}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
acts on
\omega(\mathscr{G})
. The cocharacter lattice of
\Def(\mathscr{G},S/W)_{\red}\subseteq\Def(\mathscr{G}/W)
is the sublattice of
U_{\GL,\mu}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
that consists of elements commuting with the endomorphisms of
\omega(\mathscr{G})
induced by
s\in S
.

2.4.2.
Canonical coordinates on
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}

According to (
2.4.2
),

\Def(\mathscr{A}_{x}^{\KS}[p^{\infty}]/W)

resp
.
\Def(\Psi_{x}/W)
admits a structure of a formal torus, with character lattice
U_{\GL,\mu^{-1}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})

resp
.
U_{\GL,\overline{\mu}^{-1}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
. Our main goal is to show that:

Proposition 2.6
.

\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}\subseteq\Def(\mathscr{A}_{x}^{\KS}[p^{\infty}]/W)
is a formal subtorus with cocharacter lattice
U_{\GSpin,\mu}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
.
Furthermore,
\pi_{x}^{-1}
is an isomorphism of formal tori, whose induced morphism on the character lattices is exactly the composition

U_{\GSpin,\mu^{-1}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\xrightarrow{c}U_{\SO,\mu^{c,-1}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\xrightarrow{r}U_{\GL,\overline{\mu}^{-1}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p}),

where the morphisms
r,c
are defined in §
2.3.3
.

Remark 2.7
.

As a consequence of Theorem
2.6
, we can write the torus structure on
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}
into the following three equivalent forms:

\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}\simeq\left\{\begin{aligned} &U_{\GSpin,{\mu}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge},\\
&U_{\SO,{\mu}^{c}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge},\\
&U_{\GL,\overline{\mu}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}.\end{aligned}\right.

these three different forms originate from distinct contexts. As previously noted, the first torus structure arises from the canonical coordinates on
\Def(\mathscr{A}_{x}[p^{\infty}]/W)
, while the third torus structure comes from the canonical coordinates on
\Def(\Psi_{x}/W)
. The second torus structure, on the other hand, arises from the canonical coordinates on the deformation space of the K3
F
-crystal
\mathbf{L}_{p,x}
, see
[
12
, Theorem 2.1.7]
. The three tori are canonically identified via the isomorphisms

U_{\GSpin,\mu}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\xrightarrow{c}U_{\SO,\mu^{c}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\xrightarrow{r}U_{\GL,\overline{\mu}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p}).

Proof of Theorem
2.6
. The first statement is proven in

[
44
, Proposition 2.6]
. However, we give a different argument. Define
\tau_{x}
as the composition of
\pi_{x}^{-1}
with the embedding of
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}\subseteq\Def(\mathscr{A}_{x}^{\KS}[p^{\infty}]/W)
. From (
2.3.11
) we see that
\tau_{x}
can be identified as

\widehat{U}_{\GL,{\overline{\mu}}^{-1},W}\xrightarrow{\widehat{c}^{-1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.3014pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.3014pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\widehat{r}^{-1}}\widehat{U}_{\GSpin,{\mu}^{-1},W}\subseteq\widehat{U}_{\GL,{\mu}^{-1},W}.

Fix a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-basis
\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
of
U_{\GL,\mu}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
such that
\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{b}
is a basis of
U_{\GSpin,{\mu}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
. For
1\leq i\leq b
, let
\overline{u}_{i}=rc(u_{i})
. So
\{\overline{u}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{b}
is a basis of
U_{\GL,\overline{\mu}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
. We can view
\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
as linear functions on the variety
{U}_{\GL,{\mu}^{-1}}
, hence identifying
{U}_{\GL,{\mu}^{-1}}=\Spec\mathbb{Z}_{p}[u_{1},...,u_{N}]
,
\widehat{U}_{\GL,{\mu}^{-1},W}=\Spf W[[u_{1},...,u_{N}]]
and
\widehat{U}_{\GSpin,{\mu}^{-1},W}=\Spf W[[u_{1},...,u_{b}]]
. Similarly, we have
\widehat{U}_{\GL,\overline{\mu}^{-1},W}=\Spf W[[\overline{u}_{1},...,\overline{u}_{b}]]
. The morphism
\tau_{x}
corresponds to the ring homomorphism

\tau_{x}^{*}:W[[u_{i}]]\rightarrow W[[\overline{u}_{i}]],\,\,u_{i}\rightarrow\left\{\begin{aligned} &\overline{u}_{i},\,\,i\leq b,\\
&0,\,\,i>b.\end{aligned}\right.

Let
\mathbb{G}_{m,W}^{\wedge}=\Spf W[[q-1]]
with group structure
q\rightarrow q\otimes q
. By (
2.4.1
), the dual basis
\{u_{i}^{\vee}\}_{i=1}^{N}
gives rise to morphisms
f_{i}:U_{\GL,\mu}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m,W}^{\wedge}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m,W}^{\wedge}
. In other words, it gives rise to morphisms
f_{i}^{*}:W[[q-1]]\rightarrow W[[u_{1},...,u_{N}]]
. Let
q_{i}=f_{i}^{*}(q)
. Then
\widehat{U}_{\GL,\mu^{-1},W}=\Spf W[[q_{i}-1]]
with group structure
q_{i}\rightarrow q_{i}\otimes q_{i}
, i.e.,
\{q_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}

is the set of canonical coordinates on
\Def(\mathscr{A}_{x}^{\KS}[p^{\infty}]/W)
corresponding to
\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
. Similarly, let
\{\overline{q}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{b}
be the canonical coordinates corresponding to
\{\overline{u}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{b}
, so that
\widehat{U}_{\GL,\overline{\mu}^{-1},W}=\Spf W[[\overline{q}_{i}-1]]
, with group structure
\overline{q}_{i}\rightarrow\overline{q}_{i}\otimes\overline{q}_{i}
.

Pick Frobenius on
W[[u_{i}]]
extending the Frobenius on
W
and sending
u_{i}
to
u_{i}^{p}
. Similarly, pick Frobenius on
W[[\overline{u}_{i}]]
extending the Frobenius on
W
and sending
\overline{u}_{i}
to
\overline{u}_{i}^{p}
. Then
[
12
, Theorem 1.4.2]
implies that there are identifications of the coordinates ( see
[
44
]
for a detailed computation):

q_{i}=E_{p}(u_{i}),\,\,\overline{q}_{i}=E_{p}(\overline{u}_{i})

where
E_{p}(t)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{t^{p^{k}}}{p^{k}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[t]]
is the Artin-Hasse exponential. It then follows that

\tau_{x}^{*}(q_{i})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\overline{q}_{i},\,\,i\leq b\\
&1,\,\,i>b\end{aligned}\right..

Therefore,
\tau_{x}
is a group homomorphism with image
\Spf W[[q_{1},q_{2},...,q_{b}]]
. This is a subtorus of
\widehat{U}_{\GL,{\mu}^{-1},W}
with character lattice
U_{\GSpin,\mu^{-1}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. On the other hand, it is from the definition that the image of
\tau_{x}
is
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}
, so we deduce that
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{W}
is a subtorus of
\Def(\mathscr{A}_{x}^{\KS}[p^{\infty}]/W)
with cocharacter lattice
U_{\GSpin,\mu}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. The second assertion of the proposition is clear.
\hfill\square

3.
Monodromy of local systems

We review the notion of monodromy for étale lisse sheaves and
F
-isocrystals, which will play a fundamental role in our paper. For simplicity, we will mostly stick to local systems with coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}_{u}
, where
u
is a finite place of
\mathbb{Q}
, but the treatment extends to local systems with coefficients in finite extensions of
\mathbb{Q}_{u}
. For a more comprehensive and broader understanding of these notions, readers are suggested to refer to
[
17
, §2,§3]
. We will always assume that
X_{0}
is a geometric connected smooth variety over a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with
X=(X_{0})_{\mathbb{F}}
, and
x
is an
\mathbb{F}
-point of
X_{0}
.

3.1.
Monodromy of étale lisse sheaves

Let
u
be a finite place of
\mathbb{Q}
, including
p
. Consider
\LS(X_{0},\mathbb{Q}_{u})
, the category of étale lisse sheaves of
\mathbb{Q}_{u}
-vector spaces over
X_{0}
. This category is equivalent to the category of continuous
\pi_{1}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X_{0},x)
-representations. It is also a neutral Tannakian category with fiber functor

\displaystyle\omega_{x}:\LS(X_{0},\mathbb{Q}_{u})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Vect_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{x}.

The
monodromy group
of an object
\mathcal{E}
in
\LS(X_{0},\mathbb{Q}_{u})
at
x
is the Tannakian fundamental group of the tensor Abelian subcategory
\langle\mathcal{E}\rangle^{\otimes}
with fiber functor
\omega_{x}
, denoted
G(\mathcal{E},x)
. Since
\LS(X_{0},\mathbb{Q}_{u})
is equivalent to the category of continuous
\pi_{1}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X_{0},x)
-representations,
G(\mathcal{E},x)
is nothing other than the Zariski closure of the image of
\pi_{1}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X_{0},x)
in
\GL(\mathcal{E}_{x})
.

There is also a notion of Weil lisse sheaves, which is more widely used (cf.
[
17
]
). A Weil lisse sheaf over
X_{0}
is a (geometric) étale lisse sheaf
\mathcal{V}
over
X
, together with a Frobenius structure
F^{*}\mathcal{V}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{V}
, where
F
is the geometric Frobenius of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with respect to
\mathbb{F}
. Let
W(X_{0},x)\subseteq\pi_{1}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X_{0},x)
be the Weil group of
X_{0}
. The category of Weil lisse sheaves is equivalent to the category of continuous
W(X_{0},x)
-representations, and is a neutral Tannakian category with fiber functor
\omega_{x}
. The monodromy group at
x
of a Weil lisse sheaf
\mathcal{V}
is the Tannakian fundamental group of
\langle\mathcal{V}\rangle^{\otimes}
with fiber
\omega_{x}
. It is the Zaraski closure of
W(X_{0},x)
in
\GL(\mathcal{V}_{x})
.

An étale lisse sheaf
\mathcal{E}
in
\LS(X_{0},\mathbb{Q}_{u})
is automatically a Weil lisse sheaf via pullback, and its subquotients as Weil lisse sheaves are objects in
\LS(X_{0},\mathbb{Q}_{u})
. In other words, the monodromy group of
\mathcal{E}
as an étale lisse sheaf over
X_{0}
equals the monodromy group of
\mathcal{E}
as a Weil lisse sheaf. In this paper, we almost only work in the category
\LS(X_{0},\mathbb{Q}_{u})
.

3.2.
Monodromy of
F
-isocrystals

In this section, we use
F
to denote the absolute Frobenius. Let
\FIsoc(X_{0})
be the tensor Abelian category of
F
-isocrystals over
X_{0}
. Consider an object
\mathcal{M}
in
\FIsoc(X_{0})
. We denote
\langle\mathcal{M}\rangle^{\otimes}
the tensor Abelian subcategory generated by
\mathcal{M}
. Let
e
be the smallest positive integer such that the slopes of
\mathcal{M}_{x}
multiplied by
e
lie in
\mathbb{Z}
. The fibre functor

\displaystyle\omega_{x}:\langle\mathcal{M}\rangle^{\otimes}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{e}}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Vect_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{e}}}

\displaystyle(\mathcal{N},F)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\{v\in\mathcal{N}_{x}|\exists i\in\mathbb{Z},\,\,({F^{e}_{x}}-p^{i})v=0\}.

makes
\langle\mathcal{M}\rangle^{\otimes}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{e}}}
– the scalar extension of
\langle\mathcal{M}\rangle^{\otimes}
by
\mathbb{Q}_{p^{e}}
(cf.
[
1
, §1.4.1]
) – a neutral Tannakian category. The functor
\omega_{x}
is essentially the same as the
Dieudonné–Manin fiber functor
in
[
16
, Construction 3.1.4, Definition 3.1.6]
, and is also a minor generalization of the fiber functor found in
[
4
]
.
The fundamental group
\text{Aut}^{\otimes}(\omega_{x})\subseteq\GL(\omega_{x}(\mathcal{M}))
is called the
(global) monodromy group
of
\mathcal{M}
at
x
, denoted
G(\mathcal{M},x)
. Let
\mathcal{M}^{/x}\in\FIsoc(X^{/x})
be the base change of
\mathcal{M}
. The subcategory
\langle\mathcal{M}^{/x}\rangle^{\otimes}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{e}}}
is again a Tannakian category with the fiber functor
\omega_{x}
, see
[
14
, §3.3]
. The corresponding monodromy group is called the
local monodromy group
of
\mathcal{M}
at
x
, denoted
G(\mathcal{M}^{/x},x)
. We have
G(\mathcal{M}^{/x},x)\subseteq G(\mathcal{M},x)\subseteq\GL(\omega_{x}(\mathcal{M}))
.

We will mainly be interested in
F
-isocrystals with constant slopes. If
\mathcal{M}
has constant slopes, then
[
25
, Corollary 2.6.2]
and
[
28
, Corollary 4.2]
imply that
\mathcal{M}
admits the slope filtration

0=\mathcal{M}_{0}\subseteq...\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{l}=\mathcal{M},

where each graded piece
\mathcal{M}_{i}/\mathcal{M}_{i-1}
has pure slope
s_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}
and
s_{1}<...<s_{l}
. We will write
\gr\mathcal{M}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathcal{M}_{i}/\mathcal{M}_{i-1}
. Let
U(\mathcal{M},x)

resp
.
U(\mathcal{M}^{/x},x)
be the kernel of the natural projection
G(\mathcal{M},x)\rightarrow G(\gr\mathcal{M},x)

resp
.
G(\mathcal{M}^{/x},x)\rightarrow G(\gr\mathcal{M}^{/x},x)
. They are all unipotent, with
U(\mathcal{M}^{/x},x)\subseteq U(\mathcal{M},x)
. The monodromy groups for an
F
-isocrystal with constant slopes is relatively easy to understand:

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{M}
has constant slopes and let
\nu
be the Newton cocharacter of
\mathcal{M}_{x}
. Identify
G(\mathcal{M},x),G(\gr\mathcal{M},x),U(\mathcal{M},x)
and their local counterparts as subgroups of
\GL(\omega_{x}(\mathcal{M}))
. The following are true:

(1)

G(\gr\mathcal{M}^{/x},{x})=\im\nu
,

(2)

G(\mathcal{M},x)=U(\mathcal{M},x)\rtimes G(\gr\mathcal{M},x)
, where
G(\gr\mathcal{M},x)
acts on
U(\mathcal{M},x)
via conjugation,

(3)

G(\mathcal{M}^{/x},x)=U(\mathcal{M}^{/x},x)\rtimes G(\gr\mathcal{M}^{/x},x)
, where
G(\gr\mathcal{M}^{/x},x)
acts on
U(\mathcal{M}^{/x},x)
via conjugation.

Proof.

(1)

follows since a uniroot
F
- isocrystal over
X^{/x}_{\mathbb{F}}
is constant.

(2)

There is a map
\gr:\langle\mathcal{M}\rangle^{\otimes}\subseteq\langle\gr\mathcal{M}\rangle^{\otimes}
sending an
F
-isocrystal to its graded object, inducing a section
G(\gr\mathcal{M},x)\hookrightarrow G(\mathcal{M},x)
to the natural map
G(\mathcal{M},x)\rightarrow G(\gr\mathcal{M},x)
, hence we have the semi-direct product. The claim that
G(\gr\mathcal{M},x)
acts on
U(\mathcal{M},x)
via conjugation is clear from the way that they embed into
\GL(\omega_{x}(\mathcal{M}))
.

(3)

is similar to (3).
\hfill\square

3.2.1.
The case of ordinary
p
-divisible groups

We now review Chai’s result on local and global monodromy of ordinary
p
-divisible groups. Let
\mathscr{G}
be an ordinary
p
-divisible group over
X_{0}
, which is an extension of
\mathscr{G}^{\loc}
and
\mathscr{G}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}
. Write
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{D}(\mathscr{G})
,
\mathcal{M}^{0}=\mathbb{D}(\mathscr{G}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}})
and
\mathcal{M}^{1}=\mathbb{D}(\mathscr{G}^{\loc})
, so
\mathcal{M}
admits a slope filtration with
\gr\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}^{0}\oplus\mathcal{M}^{1}
.

As previously noted in §
2.3.1
, we use
\mu:\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\GL(\omega_{x}(\mathcal{M}))
to denote the Hodge cocharacter of
\mathcal{M}_{x}
. Since
\mathscr{G}
is ordinary, this coincides with the Newton cocharacter.
Routinely, the notations
U_{\GL,\mu}
and
U_{\GL,\mu^{-1}}
represent the unipotent and the opposite unipotent of
\mu
in
\GL(\omega_{x}(\mathcal{M}))
. Now, we reconsider the Serre–Tate pairing (
2.4.3
):

q_{\mathscr{G}}:U_{\GL,\mu^{-1}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\rightarrow\mathbb{G}^{\wedge}_{m}(X^{/x}).

Define
N_{x}(\mathcal{M})=\ker(q_{\mathscr{G}})^{\perp}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
, the subspace of
U_{\GL,\mu}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
which pairs to 0 with
\ker(q_{\mathscr{G}})_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
. It can also be viewed as a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-unipotent subgroup of
U_{\GL,\mu}
.

Theorem 3.2

(Chai)
.

Notations as above. Identify
G(\mathcal{M},x),G(\gr\mathcal{M},x),U(\mathcal{M},x)
and their local counterparts as subgroups of
\GL(\omega_{x}(\mathcal{M}))
and regard
N_{x}(\mathcal{M})
as a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-unipotent subgroup of
U_{\GL,\mu}
. We have

(1)

U(\mathcal{M}^{/x},x)=N_{x}(\mathcal{M})
and
G(\mathcal{M}^{/x},x)=N_{x}(\mathcal{M})\rtimes\im\mu
, where
\im\mu
acts on
N_{x}(\mathcal{M})
via conjugation.

(2)

U(\mathcal{M},x)=N_{x}(\mathcal{M})
and
G(\mathcal{M},x)=N_{x}(\mathcal{M})\rtimes G(\gr\mathcal{M},x)
, where
G(\gr\mathcal{M},x)
acts on
U(\mathcal{M}^{/x},x)
via conjugation.

Proof.

Using Lemma
3.1
, we deduce (1) from
[
4
, Theorem 3.3]
and (2) from
[
4
, Theorem 4.4]
(note that
[
4
, Theorem 4.4]
is originally stated for a variety over
\mathbb{F}
, but the proof works for
X_{0}
). See also
[
15
, Theorem 3.4.4]
for a generalization of this theorem.

\hfill\square

3.3.
Monodromy of overconvergent
F
-isocrystals

We use the same setups and notation as in §
3.2
. Let’s denote
\FIsoc^{\dagger}(X_{0})
as the tensor Abelian category of overconvergent
F
-isocrystals over
X_{0}
. There is a forgetful functor
\text{Fgt}:\FIsoc^{\dagger}(X_{0})\rightarrow\FIsoc(X_{0})
. Let
\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}=(\mathcal{M}^{\dagger},F^{\dagger})\in\FIsoc^{\dagger}(X_{0})
and let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},F)
be its image in
\FIsoc(X_{0})
forgetting the overconvergent structure. Recall that
e
is the smallest positive integer such that the slopes of
\mathcal{M}_{x}
multiplied by
e
lie in
\mathbb{Z}
. The fiber functor

\omega_{x}^{\dagger}=\omega_{x}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\text{Fgt}:\langle\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}\rangle^{\otimes}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{e}}}\rightarrow\text{Vect}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{e}}}

makes
\langle\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}\rangle^{\otimes}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{e}}}
a neutral Tannakian category. The Tannakian fundamental group thus arises is called the
overconvergent monodromy group
of
\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}
at
x
, denoted
G(\mathcal{M}^{\dagger},x)
. Note that we have
G(\mathcal{M},x)\subseteq G(\mathcal{M}^{\dagger},x)\subseteq\GL(\omega_{x}(\mathcal{M}))
.

Theorem 3.3

(D’Addezio)
.

The following are true:

(1)

Suppose
\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}
admits slope filtration, then
G(\mathcal{M},x)\subseteq G(\mathcal{M}^{\dagger},x)
is the parabolic subgroup fixing the slope filtration of
\mathcal{M}_{x}
.

(2)

Suppose
g:\mathscr{A}\rightarrow X_{0}
is an Abelian scheme. Let
D^{\dagger}(\mathscr{A})=R^{1}g_{*,{\mathrm{cris}}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{A},{\mathrm{cris}}}
, then the overconvergent monodromy group
G(D^{\dagger}(\mathscr{A}),x)
is reductive.

(3)

Setup being the same as (2). If
\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}
is an overconvergent
F
-isocrystal in
\langle D^{\dagger}(\mathscr{A})\rangle^{\otimes}
that has constant slopes, then
G(\gr\mathcal{M},x)
is reductive.

Proof.

(1)

follows from
[
16
, Theorem 5.1.2]
.

(2)

is
[
17
, Corollary 3.5.2]
.

(3)

Since
G(D^{\dagger}(\mathscr{A}),x)
is reductive by (2), the group
G(\mathcal{M}^{\dagger},x)
is also reductive. By (1),
G(\mathcal{M},x)
is the parabolic subgroup of
G(\mathcal{M}^{\dagger},x)
fixing the slope filtration on
\mathcal{M}_{x}
. Since
G(\gr\mathcal{M},x)
is a quotient of
G(\mathcal{M},x)
by Lemma
3.1
(3), it is also reductive.
\hfill\square

3.4.
Monodromy of compatible systems

We refer to
[
17
, §3]
and
[
27
, §2]
for the notion of coefficient objects and compatible systems. Roughly speaking, a coefficient object over
X_{0}
is an
l
-adic lisse sheaf or an overconvergent
F
-isocrystal. A coefficient object
\mathcal{E}_{0}
over
X_{0}
is equipped with a characteristic polynomial
P(\mathcal{E}_{x_{0}},t)
for every closed point
x_{0}\in|X_{0}|
. For a number field
E
, together with a set of finite places
\Sigma
containing all places that do not divide
p
, an
E
-compatible system of coefficient objects

\{\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,0}\}_{\lambda\in\Sigma}
is a collection of coefficient objects
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,0}
over
E_{\lambda}
indexed by
\lambda\in\Sigma
, such that the characteristic polynomials of all
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,0}
at all closed points of
X_{0}
are equal and are
E
-polynomials. In
[
17
, Theorem 1.2.1, 1.2.4]
, D’Addezio proved that the monodromy groups of semi-simple coefficient objects in an
E
-compatible system are
\lambda
-independent.

In this paper, we will mainly consider the coefficient objects that arise from the first
l
-adic and the first crystalline cohomology of an Abelian scheme
\mathscr{A}_{0}
over
X_{0}
. We will use
\mathrm{fpl}(E)
to denote the set of finite places of
E
. Let
\mathcal{E}_{l,0}:=H^{1}_{l,{\text{\'{e}t}}}(\mathscr{A}_{0})[\frac{1}{l}]
(
l\neq p
) and
\mathcal{E}_{p,0}:=H^{1}_{{\mathrm{cris}}}(\mathscr{A}_{0})[\frac{1}{p}]
. Then
\{\mathcal{E}_{l,0}\}_{l\in\mathrm{fpl}(\mathbb{Q})-\{p\}}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-compatible system.
Indeed, for a closed point
x_{0}
in
X_{0}
, the characteristic polynomials
P(\mathcal{E}_{l,0,x_{0}},t)
are all equal to the characteristic polynomial
P({\mathscr{A}_{0,x_{0}}},t)\in\mathbb{Q}[t]
defined by
P({\mathscr{A}_{0,x_{0}}},n)=\deg([n]_{\mathscr{A}_{0,x_{0}}}-F_{\mathscr{A}_{0,x_{0}}})
, where
F_{\mathscr{A}_{0,x_{0}}}
is the geometric Frobenius, see
[
19
, Theorem 12.18]
. For the place
p
, it is a bit more subtle. Indeed, the Frobenius that appears in
H^{1}_{{\mathrm{cris}}}(\mathscr{A}_{0})[\frac{1}{p}]
as an
F
-isocrystal is the absolute Frobenius. In order to fit
\mathcal{E}_{p,0}:=H^{1}_{{\mathrm{cris}}}(\mathscr{A}_{0})[\frac{1}{p}]
in a compatible system with various
\mathcal{E}_{l,0}
’s, we need to consider
\mathcal{E}_{p,0}^{\prime}
, the
F^{N}
-isocrystal associated to
H^{1}_{{\mathrm{cris}}}(\mathscr{A}_{0})[\frac{1}{p}]
, where
N=\log_{p}q
. Then
\{\mathcal{E}_{l,0}\}_{l\in\mathrm{fpl}(\mathbb{Q})-\{p\}}\cup\{\mathcal{E}_{p,0}^{\prime}\}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-compatible system.

Nevertheless, we will still call the collection
\{\mathcal{E}_{u,0}\}_{u\in\mathrm{fpl}(\mathbb{Q})}
a
weakly compatible system
. In general, a collection of coefficient objects
\{\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,0}\}_{\lambda\in\Sigma}
will be termed a
weakly
E
-compatible system
, if for every
\lambda|p
, after replacing
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,0}
by an associated
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,0}^{\prime}
obtained by raising the Frobenius to a power, the new collection
\{\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,0}\}_{\lambda\in\Sigma,\lambda\nmid p}\cup\{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\lambda,0}\}_{\lambda\in\Sigma,\lambda|p}
is an
E
-compatible system.

Lemma 3.4

(D’Addezio)
.

If
\{\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,0}\}_{\lambda\in\Sigma}
is a weakly compatible system of semi-simple coefficient objects over
X_{0}
, then the neutral components of the monodromy groups of
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,0}
are
\lambda
-independent in the sense of
[
17
, Theorem 1.2.1]
. Furthermore, there is a finite étale cover of
X_{0}^{\prime}/X_{0}
, such that the monodromy groups of the elements in the pullback system
\{\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,0}|_{X^{\prime}_{0}}\}_{\lambda\in\Sigma}
are connected.

Proof.

Let
\{\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,0}\}_{\lambda\in\Sigma,\lambda\nmid p}\cup\{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\lambda,0}\}_{\lambda\in\Sigma,\lambda|p}
be the
E
-compatible system obtained from
\{\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,0}\}_{\lambda\in\Sigma}
by replacing
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,0}
where
\lambda|p
with
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\lambda,0}
, as in the definition. Up to base change, the monodromy groups of
\mathcal{E}_{p,0}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{p,0}
have the same neutral component. So by
[
17
, Theorem 1.2.1]
, the neutral components of the monodromy groups of
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,0}
are
\lambda
-independent. For the second assertion, we know from
[
17
, Theorem 1.2.4]
that the
\pi_{0}
of the monodromy groups for elements in
\{\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,0}\}_{\lambda\in\Sigma,\lambda\nmid p}
are
\lambda
-independent. Since there are only finitely many
\lambda
dividing
p
, there is a finite étale cover
X_{0}^{\prime}/X_{0}
satisfying the assertion made in the lemma.
\hfill\square

4.
Constructions and conjectures

In this section we introduce a reductive group
{\MTT}(f)
for a product of GSpin Shimura varieties, and use it to construct a special subvariety
\mathcal{X}_{f}
. As the notation suggests, it is the characteristic
p
analogue of the Mumford–Tate group. We reveal several basic properties of
{\MTT}(f)
and
\mathcal{X}_{f}
, which will enable us to make precise statements of the conjectures.

4.1.
Constructions and first properties

Suppose that
\mathbf{I}
is a finite index set. For each
i\in\mathbf{I}
, let
(L_{i},Q_{i})
be an even quadratic
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice which is self dual at
p
and has signature
(2,b_{i})
, and
H_{i}=\Cl(L_{i})
. For each
i
, we choose a hyperspecial level structure
\mathbb{K}_{i}
. As a result, for each
i
, we obtain a GSpin Shimura variety
\mathcal{S}_{i}
. It has a
canonical integral model
\mathscr{S}_{i}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
, together with a Kuga–Satake Abelian scheme
\mathscr{A}_{i}^{\KS}
over
\mathscr{S}_{i}
and local systems
\mathbf{H}_{i,\bullet},\mathbf{L}_{i,\bullet}
.

Let
(L_{\mathbf{I}},Q_{\mathbf{I}}):=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbf{I}}(L_{i},Q_{i})
,
H_{\mathbf{I}}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbf{I}}H_{i}
. For a
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra
R
, let
\SO^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},R}):=\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\SO(L_{i,R})
and
\GSpin^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},R})=\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\GSpin(L_{i,R})
.
Let

\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{I}}
be the product
\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\mathscr{S}_{i}
and
\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\mathcal{S}_{i}
, respectively. Furthermore, we also denote by
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{I}}
,
\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{I},\bullet}
,
\mathbf{L}_{\mathbf{I},\bullet}
the products over
\mathbf{I}
of the Kuga–Satake Abelian schemes and the corresponding local systems.

4.1.1.
\mathcal{X}_{f}
and
\MTT(f)

Suppose that
(X,x)
is a smooth and connected pointed variety over
\mathbb{F}
with a morphism
f
into
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. Let
X_{0}/\mathbb{F}_{q}
