be a finite field model of
X
such that
f
is the base change of a morphism
f_{0}:X_{0}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}_{p}}
. For later use, we also let
\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
be the smallest formal subtorus of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}^{/x}
containing the image of the morphism
f^{/x}:X^{/x}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}}^{/x}
.

We denote by
\tilde{x}
the canonical lift of
x
,
which lies in
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}}(W)
(cf.
[
44
, Proposition 2.5]
). Let
\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}
be the base change of
\tilde{x}
to
\mathbb{C}
along the embedding
W\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
(§
1.5
). Fix a trivialization
\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{I},\text{B}}|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{I}}}\simeq\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{I}}\times H_{\mathbf{I}}
where
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{I}}
is a universal cover of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{I}}
. Then we can identify
H_{\mathbf{I}}\simeq\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{I},\text{B},\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}}
, and there is a cocharacter
h_{\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}}:\Res_{\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\GSpin^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{R}})
corresponding to the rational Hodge structure
\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},\text{B},\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}}
.

Let
\eta
be the generic point of
X
and
\overline{\eta}
be a geometric point lying over
\eta
. There exists a connected étale cover
X^{\prime}/X
and a morphism
\overline{\eta}\rightarrow X^{\prime}
, such that the restriction

\End^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{\mathbf{I},X^{\prime}}^{\KS})\rightarrow\End^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{\mathbf{I},\overline{\eta}}^{\KS})
is an isomorphism (see Lemma
4.2
below). Choose a lifting of
x
to
X^{\prime}
and still call it
x
. Then

(4.1.1)

\displaystyle\End^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{\mathbf{I},\overline{\eta}}^{\KS})\subseteq\End^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{\mathbf{I},X^{\prime}}^{\KS})=

\displaystyle\End^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{\mathbf{I},x}^{\KS})

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\End^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{\mathbf{I},\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}}^{\KS})=\End_{h_{\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}}}(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}})\subseteq\End(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}}).

The embedding
\End^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{\mathbf{I},\overline{\eta}}^{\KS})\subseteq\End^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{\mathbf{I},x}^{\KS})
resulting from the first line of (
4.1.1
) is independent of the choices made. Let
\MTT^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)
be the connected component of the commutant of
\End^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{\mathbf{I},\overline{\eta}}^{\KS})
in
\GSpin^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}})
. It is a reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}

8
8

8

This can be checked using the Cartan involution: let
\theta=\ad h_{y}(i)
be the Cartan involution over
\GSpin^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{C}})
, so
\GSpin^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{C}})^{(\theta)}=\{g\in\GSpin^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{C}})|\theta(g)=\theta(\overline{g})\}

is a compact Lie group over
\mathbb{R}
. Since
h_{y}
factors through
{\MTT}^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)
, it makes sense to talk about
{\MTT}^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)(\mathbb{C})^{(\theta)}
, which is a subgroup of
\GSpin^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{C}})^{(\theta)}
consisting of elements that commute with
\End^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{\mathbf{I},\overline{\eta}}^{\KS})
. This imposes a closed condition on
\GSpin^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{C}})^{(\theta)}
, so
{\MTT}^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)(\mathbb{C})^{(\theta)}
is compact.
.

Definition 1
.

(1)

{\MTT}^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)
gives rise to a
Shimura subvariety
\mathcal{X}_{f}
of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{I}}
with level structure
\MTT^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)\cap\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\mathbb{K}_{i}
.

(2)

Let
\MTT(f)
be the generic Mumford–Tate group of
\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{B},\mathcal{X}_{f}}
.
It is equipped with a standard representation
{\rho}_{f}:{\MTT}(f)\hookrightarrow\GSpin^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}})\subseteq\GL(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}})
.

(3)

Let
{\Hg}(f)
be the generic Hodge group of
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{B},\mathcal{X}_{f}}
. It is the image of
{\MTT}(f)
in
\SO^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}})
, which comes with a standard representation
{\varrho}_{f}:{\Hg}(f)\hookrightarrow\SO^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}})\subseteq\GL(L_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}}).

Remark 4.1
.

(1)

The axioms of Shimura datum imply that
{\MTT}^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)^{\ad}_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\MTT(f)^{\ad}_{\mathbb{R}}
admit the same set of non-compact simple factors.
Since
{\MTT}^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)
is cut out by endomorphisms, whereas
\MTT(f)
is cut out by all Hodge cycles, we have
\MTT(f)\subseteq{\MTT}^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)
.
It is not hard to show that in the single GSpin case, two groups are equal (but we won’t be using this).

(2)

Let
\mathbf{T}\subseteq\GL(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}})
be the rank one torus of homotheties, then
\Hg(f)=\MTT(f)/\mathbf{T}
.

(3)

When
X_{0}
is an ordinary point, then
\MTT(f)
is nothing other than the Frobenius torus of
\mathscr{A}_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}^{\KS}
. In this case, it is known that the base change of
\MTT(f)
to
\mathbb{Q}_{l}

resp
.
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is isomorphic to the neutral component of the
l
-adic
resp
. crystalline monodromy of
\mathscr{A}_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}^{\KS}
.

The following is a well-known lemma on extending endomorphisms of Abelian schemes, which will be used several times in the paper. We include it here for readers’ convenience.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{A}
is an Abelian scheme over a Noetherian integral scheme
S
. Let
F
be a field extension of
K(S)
and
w\in\End(\mathscr{A}_{F})
. Then
w
extends to a finite cover
S^{\prime}/S
with
K(S^{\prime})\subseteq F
. If
S
is normal, one can further require
S^{\prime}/S
to be étale.

Proof.

By

[
38
, Proposition 2.3]
, every component of the group End-scheme
\mathcal{E}nd(\mathscr{A}/S)
is finite and unramified over
S
.
Note that
w
corresponds to an
F
-point of
\mathcal{E}nd(\mathscr{A}/S)
dominating
S
. We can let
S^{\prime}
be the irreducible component of
\mathcal{E}nd(\mathscr{A}/S)
containing the image of
F
. If
S
is normal, then a irreducible component of
\mathcal{E}nd(\mathscr{A}/S)
that dominants
S
is flat over
S
(cf.
[
23
, Theorem 18.10.1]
), hence étale.
\hfill\square

4.1.2.
Mod
p
fiber of
\mathcal{X}_{f}

Let
\mathbf{E}
be the reflex field of
\mathcal{X}_{f}
.
\mathbf{E}
contains a place
\mathbf{p}
determined by the identification
\mathbb{C}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}_{p}
(cf. §
1.5
). Let
\mathscr{X}_{f}
be the Zariski closure of
\mathcal{X}_{f}
in
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}}O_{\mathbf{E},(\mathbf{p})}
. We call this a
naïve integral model
of
\mathcal{X}_{f}
. Since
h_{\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}}
factors through
\MTT(f)
, we have
\tilde{x}\in\mathscr{X}_{f}(W)
and
x\in\mathscr{X}_{f}(\mathbb{F})
. In particular,
O_{\mathbf{E},(\mathbf{p})}\subseteq W
. Let
\mathscr{X}^{/x}_{f,W}
be the completion of
{\mathscr{X}}_{f,W}
at
x
. It follows from Noot’s result
[
39
, Theorem 3.7]
that
\mathscr{X}^{/x}_{f,W}
is flat and is a finite union of torsion translates of subtori of
the Serre–Tate torus
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{\mathbf{I},W}
. Furthermore, there is a unique irreducible component
\mathscr{X}^{/x,+}_{f,W}\subseteq\mathscr{X}^{/x}_{f,W}
that contains
\tilde{x}
, and it is a formal subtorus of
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{\mathbf{I},W}
. As a consequence, there is a unique component
\mathscr{X}^{+}_{f}\subseteq\mathscr{X}_{f}
that contains
\tilde{x}
, and its generic fiber
\mathcal{X}^{+}_{f}
is the unique component of
\mathcal{X}_{f}
containing
\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

The following are true:

(1)

Any irreducible component of
\mathscr{X}^{/x}_{f,W}
is a torsion translate of
\mathscr{X}^{/x,+}_{f,W}
. In particular,
\mathscr{X}_{f,\mathbb{F},\red}^{/x}
equals
\mathscr{X}_{f,\mathbb{F}}^{/x,+}
, and is a subtorus of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}^{/x}
with rank
\dim\mathcal{X}_{f}
.

(2)

\End(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{I},\overline{\eta}})
deforms to
\mathscr{X}^{/x,+}_{f,W}
.

(3)

f
factors through
\mathscr{X}_{f,\mathbb{F}}
.

Proof.

For a lattice
\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma_{0}=\End(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{I},\overline{\eta}})
of finite index, let
\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}:=\Def(\Gamma/\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{\mathbf{I},W})\subseteq\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{\mathbf{I},W}
, see (
2.3.2
). We first study the structure of
\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}
. Let
\Lambda=\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}U_{\GSpin,\mu^{-1}_{i}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
. It follows from Proposition
2.6
and Serre–Tate theory that there exists a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-sublattice
\Lambda_{f}\subseteq\Lambda
such that
\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}\simeq\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\Lambda/\Lambda_{f},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge})
. Taking saturation and arguing as in (
2.4.5
), we see that
\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}
decomposes as a formal subtorus
\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}^{+}
and a finite flat group scheme. Since
\Gamma
is of finite index in
\Gamma_{0}
,
\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}^{+}
is independent of
\Gamma
chosen. Indeed, we always have
\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}^{+}=\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma_{0}}^{+}
. Therefore
\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}
is a torsion translate of
\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}^{+}
, and is flat over
W
. Furthermore, all irreducible components of
\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}
reduce to the same torus over
\mathbb{F}
.

Since
\mathcal{X}_{f}
is defined by
\MTT^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)
, there is a sufficiently small sublattice
\Gamma
, which is of finite index in
\Gamma_{0}
, such that
\Gamma
deforms to
\mathscr{X}^{/x}_{f,W}
. In other words,
\mathscr{X}^{/x}_{f,W}\subseteq\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}
, whence
\mathscr{X}^{/x,+}_{f,W}\subseteq\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}^{+}=\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma_{0}}^{+}\subseteq\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma_{0}}
. So we have (
2
).
Furthermore, the generic fibers of
\mathscr{X}^{/x,+}_{f,W}
and
\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}^{+}
have the same dimension. Since
\mathscr{X}^{/x,+}_{f,W}
and
\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}^{+}
are both formal tori, we have
\mathscr{X}^{/x,+}_{f,W}=\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}^{+}
. Hence every irreducible component of
\mathscr{X}^{/x}_{f,W}
is an irreducible component of
\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}
, and is a torsion translate of
\mathscr{X}^{/x,+}_{f,W}
. Therefore we have
(
1
). Note that
\rk\mathscr{X}_{f,\mathbb{F},\red}^{/x}=\dim\mathcal{X}_{f}
follows simply from flatness.

Finally,
f^{/x}
factors through
\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma,\mathbb{F}}
for the reason that
\Gamma
deforms to
X^{/x}
. Since
X^{/x}
is smooth,
f^{/x}
factors through
\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma,\mathbb{F},\red}=\mathscr{X}_{f,\mathbb{F},\red}^{/x}
. Therefore
f
factors through
\mathscr{X}_{f,\mathbb{F}}
. This proves (
3
).
\hfill\square

4.2.
\MTT(f)
and monodromy groups

Let
u
be a finite place of
\mathbb{Q}
. Recall from §
3
that
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},u})
is a
\mathbb{Q}_{u}
-space. The étale–Betti and crystalline–de Rham–Betti comparison isomorphisms yield identifications
\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{I},\text{B},\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{u}\simeq\omega_{x}(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},u})
. Composing with the base changes of the identification
H_{\mathbf{I}}\simeq\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{I},\text{B},\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}}
, we have identifications
H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{u}}\simeq\omega_{x}(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},u})
. In a similar and compatible manner, we also have identifications
L_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{u}}\simeq\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{I},u})
.

These enable us to regard the monodromy group
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},x)

as a subgroup of
\GL(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{u}})
, so that the standard representation of the later group restricts to the monodromy representation of the former group. We can also identify
{\MTT}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
as a subgroup of
\GL(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{u}})
via
{\rho}_{f}
. In a similar and compatible manner,
{\Hg}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
and
G(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},x)
are both subgroups of
\GL(L_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{u}})
.
The following is a characteristic
p
analogue of Deligne’s proof of half of the Mumford–Tate conjecture (
[
13
, §I]
):

Proposition 4.4
.

G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}\subseteq{\MTT}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
and
G(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}\subseteq{\Hg}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show the first assertion. We first treat the case where
u\neq p
. Let
\eta_{0}
be the generic point of
X_{0}
and let
\overline{\eta}_{0}\rightarrow\eta_{0}
be a geometric point. Let
\epsilon
be the generic point of
\mathscr{X}_{f}^{+}
and let
\overline{\epsilon}\rightarrow\epsilon
be a geometric point. Let
\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}_{f}^{+}
be the normalization of
\mathscr{X}_{f}^{+}
. By Lemma
4.3
(3),
f
factors through
\mathscr{X}_{f}^{+}
.
The induced map
{\eta}_{0}\rightarrow\mathscr{X}_{f}^{+}
lifts to
\eta_{0}^{\prime}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}_{f}^{+}
for some finite map
\eta_{0}^{\prime}\rightarrow\eta_{0}
.
We have the following relations between several étale fundamental groups:

{\pi_{1}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X_{0},x)}\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi_{1}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X_{0},\overline{\eta}_{0})\xrightarrow{g_{1}}{\pi_{1}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(\eta_{0},\overline{\eta}_{0})}\xleftarrow{g_{2}}{\pi_{1}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(\eta_{0}^{\prime},\overline{\eta}_{0})}\rightarrow\pi_{1}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}_{f}^{+},\overline{\eta}_{0})\xleftarrow{\sim}\pi_{1}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}_{f}^{+},\overline{\epsilon})\xleftarrow{g_{3}}\pi_{1}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}).

The map
g_{1}
and
g_{3}
are surjective since
X_{0}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}_{f}^{+}
are normal (cf.
[
3
, 0BQM]
). The map
g_{2}
is of finite index. Choosing appropriate identifications of the fibers, we see that
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}
, being the neutral component of the Zariski closure of the image of
\pi_{1}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X_{0},x)
in
\GL(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{u}})
, is contained in the neutral component of the Zariski closure of the image of
\pi_{1}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(\eta_{0}^{\prime},\overline{\eta}_{0})
in
\GL(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{u}})
. But the later group is the
u
-adic monodromy of
\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},u,\epsilon}
, which is known to be contained in
\MTT(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
(cf.
[
13
, §I]
).

Now we treat the crystalline case.
It is possible to compare the tensor category of crystalline local systems and the tensor category of de Rham local systems on certain formal thickenings, and then compare these with the Betti ones. However, we need more theoretical buildups to achieve this. Instead, we give an interesting self-contained ad hoc argument involving the parabolicity conjecture.

Claim
.

G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}\subseteq{\MTT}^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.

We begin by noting that as a subgroups of
\GL(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
,
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},p,{X_{0}}},x)^{\circ}
and
{\MTT}^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
further lie in
\GSpin^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
. Under the above discussed identification of the fibers, we have

{\End^{0}(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{I},{X_{0}}})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\subseteq\End^{0}(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},p,{X_{0}}})\subseteq\End^{0}(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
, and
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},p,{X_{0}}},x)^{\circ},\MTT^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\subseteq\GSpin^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{p}})\subseteq\End^{0}(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
. Possibly passing to a finite étale cover of
X_{0}
, we can assume that
\End^{0}(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{I},\overline{\eta}})=\End^{0}(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{I},{X_{0}}})
. Note that
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},p,{X_{0}}},x)^{\circ}
commutes with
\End^{0}(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},p,{X_{0}}})
, hence commuting with
\End^{0}(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{I},{X_{0}}})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. In other words,
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},p,{X_{0}}},x)^{\circ}
is contained in the commutant of
\End^{0}(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{I},{X_{0}}})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
in
\GSpin^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
. However, the later group is exactly
{\MTT}^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
from (
4.1.1
). Therefore
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}\subseteq{\MTT}^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.

Now Theorem
3.3
implies that the Levi
Lv_{G,\mu}
of the parabolic subgroup of
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}
corresponding to the canonical Hodge cocharacter
\mu
associated to
x
(§
2.3.1
) is the monodromy of
\gr\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}}
, which is a direct sum of twists of uni-root
F
-isocrystals. It follows from
[
9
, Theorem 2.1]
that
Lv_{G,\mu}
is generated by
\im\mu
and the neutral component of the Zariski closure of the image of
\pi^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}_{1}(X_{0},x)
in
\GL(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
. Recall that we have chosen an embedding
W\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, and under the crystalline–de Rham–Betti comparison, the base change of
\mu
to
\mathbb{C}
is identified with the Hodge cocharacter
\mu_{\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}}
of
\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},\mathrm{B},\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}}
. An étale fundamental group argument as in the
u\neq p
case shows that
Lv_{G,\mu,\mathbb{C}}
is contained in the Levi of the parabolic subgroup of
\MTT(f)_{\mathbb{C}}
corresponding to
\mu_{\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}}
. Since
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}_{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq{\MTT}^{\mathrm{pre}}(f)_{\mathbb{C}}
by
Claim
, Remark
4.1
(
1
) implies that the unipotent and opposite unipotent of
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}_{\mathbb{C}}
corresponding to
\mu_{\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}}
are also contained
\MTT(f)_{\mathbb{C}}
. Consequently,
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}_{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq\MTT(f)_{\mathbb{C}}
, hence
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}\subseteq\MTT(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.

\hfill\square

Now we establish a characteristic
p
analogue of
[
46
, Theorem 1.3.1]
. In the following, for an algebraic group
G
, we denote by
Z^{0}G
the connected center of
G
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Z^{0}G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}=Z^{0}{\MTT}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
and
Z^{0}G(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}=Z^{0}{\Hg}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
.

Proof.

Note that the torus
\mathbf{T}
from Remark
4.1
(
2
) is contained in the center of
\MTT(f)
. Moreover,
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
is contained in the center of
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}
. So again, it suffices to show the first assertion. To simplify the notation, we write
G_{u}
for
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}
.

First, we claim that
Z^{0}G_{u}\subseteq Z^{0}{\MTT}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
. By Tate’s isogeny theorem over function fields (crystalline isogeny theorem when
u=p
) and Lemma
4.2
, we have

(4.2.1)

\End_{G_{u}}^{0}(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{u}})=\End^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{\mathbf{I},\overline{\eta}}^{\KS})_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}\subseteq\End^{0}_{\MTT(f)^{\mathrm{pre}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}}(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{u}})\subseteq\End^{0}_{\MTT(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}}(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{u}}).

In fact, combining Proposition
4.4
, we even have
\End_{G_{u}}^{0}(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{u}})=\End^{0}_{\MTT(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}}(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{u}})
. Note that
G_{u}
and
\MTT(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
embed into
\End^{0}(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{u}})
, and we have

\displaystyle ZG_{u}=G_{u}\cap\End_{G_{u}}^{0}(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{u}})\subseteq\MTT(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}\cap\End^{0}_{\MTT(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}}(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{u}})=Z{\MTT}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}.

This proves one inclusion.

Now by
[
17
, Theorem 4.2.11]
, there is a point
y
of
X
such that the Frobenius tori of
y
is a maximal torus of
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},y)^{\circ}
. After choosing suitable identification of fiber functors, we can regard it as a maximal torus of
G_{u}
. By definition,
\MTT(f)=\MTT(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{B},\mathcal{X}_{f}^{+}})
. Denote by
M\subseteq\MTT(f)
the group
\MTT(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{B},\tilde{y}_{\mathbb{C}}})
, here
\tilde{y}
is a quasi-canonical lift of
y
with
\tilde{y}_{\mathbb{C}}\in\mathcal{X}_{f}^{+}
. We know from Remark
4.1
(
3
) that
M_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
is a maximal torus of
G_{u}
. We then have

Z^{0}G_{u}\subseteq M_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}\subseteq G_{u}\subseteq\MTT(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}.

As a result,
M_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
is an almost direct product of
Z^{0}G_{u}
with a torus
T_{u}\subseteq G_{u}^{\der}
. On the other hand, the abelianization
\MTT(f)^{\mathrm{ab}}
corresponds to a variation of CM Hodge structures over
\mathcal{X}_{f}^{+}
, which is constant. Therefore

M
surjects onto
\MTT(f)^{\mathrm{ab}}
. Since
Z^{0}G_{u}\subseteq Z^{0}{\MTT}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
and
T_{u}\subseteq G_{u}^{\der}\subseteq{\MTT}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}^{\der}
, we see that
Z^{0}G_{u}
also surjects onto
\MTT(f)^{\mathrm{ab}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
. This forces
Z^{0}G_{u}=Z^{0}{\MTT}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
.
\hfill\square

4.3.
Quasi-weakly special subvarieties

In this section we introduce a notion of quasi-weakly special subvariety for
\mathcal{A}_{g,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
, which is in analogy with the weakly special subvariety in char 0, and is needed for stating the char
p
analogue of the André–Oort conjecture. The classical definitions of weakly special subvariety can be found in the statement of
[
34
, Theorem 4.3]
. Roughly speaking, it is a product of a special subvariety with a possibly non-special point.

In the following, we will use
K
for
W[1/p]
,
K^{\prime}
for a finite extension of
K
and
W^{\prime}
its ring of topological bounded elements. Let
(\GSp_{2g},\mathfrak{H}^{\pm})
be a Siegel Shimura datum, and let
\mathbb{K}
be a hyperspecial level structure at
p
. We denote by

\mathcal{A}_{g,\mathbb{K},W^{\prime}}
the canonical integral model. For all Siegel modular schemes in this section, the level structures will be assumed to be hyperspecial. To ease notation, we will often omit the level structures and keep them as implicit.

4.3.1.
Ordinary naïve integral models

Let
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{g,K^{\prime}}
be a special subvariety, and let
\mathscr{X}
be the Zariski closure of
\mathcal{X}
in
\mathcal{A}_{g,W^{\prime}}
. We call
\mathscr{X}
a
naïve integral model
of
\mathcal{X}
in
\mathcal{A}_{g,W^{\prime}}
(the model
\mathscr{X}_{f,W}
as introduced in §
4.1.2
is an example of this). If the reduction
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
has non-empty ordinary locus, we define the
ordinary naïve integral model

{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\subseteq\mathscr{X}
as the open subscheme obtained by throwing out non-ordinary loci of the special fiber. Let
\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
be the normalization of
{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. We write
{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}

resp
.
\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
for the special fiber of
{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}

resp
.
\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. While
\mathscr{X}
depends on the embedding, the following lemma says that
{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
is more or less intrinsic to its generic fiber. As a consequence, we can often treat ordinary naïve integral models as canonical, and forget the ambient embedding.

Lemma 4.6

(Ordinary naïve integral models are “canonical”)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{g_{1},\mathbb{K}_{1},K^{\prime}}
,
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{g_{2},\mathbb{K}_{2},K^{\prime}}
be special subvarieties, and let
\pi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be a special morphism, i.e.,
\Gamma_{\pi}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{g_{1},\mathbb{K}_{1},K_{1}}\times\mathcal{A}_{g_{2},\mathbb{K}_{2},K_{2}}
is a special subvariety. Let
\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}
be naïve integral models of
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
with respect to the embeddings.

(1)

\mathscr{X}_{\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\neq\emptyset\Rightarrow\mathscr{Y}_{\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\neq\emptyset
. If
\pi
is surjective, then the inverse implication holds.

(2)

Suppose that
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
and
\mathscr{Y}_{\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
are non-empty, then
\pi
extends uniquely to a morphism
\tilde{\pi}:\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. If
\pi
is finite, so is
\tilde{\pi}
. If
\pi
is an isomorphism, so is
\tilde{\pi}
.

Proof.

For (
1
), the image of a special point of
\mathcal{X}
with ordinary reduction at
p
is a special point of
\mathcal{Y}
with ordinary reduction at
p
. So
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\neq\emptyset\Rightarrow\mathscr{Y}_{\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\neq\emptyset
. If
\pi
is surjective, we can lift a special point of
\mathcal{Y}
with ordinary reduction at
p
back to
\mathcal{X}
to get a special point of
\mathcal{X}
with ordinary reduction at
p
.

We now treat (
2
). Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}
be the normalizations of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
. Then
\pi
extends uniquely to a morphism
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
which, by abuse of notation, is still denoted by
\pi
. Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
is the generic fiber of
\mathscr{X}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
,
\Gamma_{\pi}
sits inside
\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\times\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. Let
\overline{\Gamma}_{\pi}
be the Zariski closure of
\Gamma_{\pi}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\times\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. We claim that the projection
p_{1}:\overline{\Gamma}_{\pi}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
is an isomorphism. Since it is already birational, it suffices to show that
p_{1}
is quasi-finite and use Zariski’s main theorem (
[
31
, §4.4 Corollary 4.6]
) to conclude. It is clear that the quasi-finiteness of
p_{1}
can be checked over
\mathbb{F}
-points of the special fiber
\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
.

Now
[
39
, Theorem 3.7, Corollary 3.8]
imply that the completion of
\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
at an
\mathbb{F}
-point admits the structure of a formal torus that comes from the Serre–Tate formal torus of the ambient Siegel modular scheme. Similar for
\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. Let
x\in\overline{\Gamma}_{\pi}(\mathbb{F})
. Since
{\Gamma}_{\pi}
is special, by
[
39
]
again we have that
\overline{\Gamma}^{/x}_{\pi}\subseteq(\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\times\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}})^{/x}
is a torsion translates of formal subtori. Since the projection
(\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\times\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}})^{/x}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}},/x}
is a morphism of formal tori, by looking at the generic fibers, we see that
p_{1}
induces an isomorphism of each irreducible component of
\overline{\Gamma}^{/x}_{\pi}
to
\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}},/x}
. If some point of
\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}(\mathbb{F})
has positive dimensional pre-image under
p_{1}
, we pick
x
to be in the pre-image. This immediately contradicts the fact that
p_{1}
induces isomorphism on components of the completions. Therefore
p_{1}
is quasi-finite, and hence an isomorphism. Composing
p_{1}^{-1}
with
p_{2}:\overline{\Gamma}_{\pi}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
yields the desired extension
\tilde{\pi}
. Uniqueness is easy.

Now suppose that
\pi
is finite. One easily reduces to the case where
\pi
is a finite cover. Note that
\tilde{\pi}
is also surjective: it suffices to check every
\mathbb{F}
-point of
\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
has non-empty fiber; for this, we can lift a
y\in\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}(\mathbb{F})
to a
\tilde{y}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}^{{\mathrm{ord}},/x}(W^{\prime})
, then pick a point in the non-empty set
\pi^{-1}(\tilde{y}_{K^{\prime}})
and spread out to get an element in
\tilde{\pi}^{-1}(y)
. Possibly enlarging
K^{\prime}
, there is a sufficiently small level structure
\mathbb{K}_{2}^{\prime}
and a special subvariety
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{g_{2},\mathbb{K}_{2}^{\prime},K^{\prime}}
, such that (i)
\varpi:\mathcal{A}_{g_{2},\mathbb{K}_{2}^{\prime},W^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{g_{2},\mathbb{K}_{2},W^{\prime}}
is a finite cover, (ii)
\mathcal{Y}=\varpi(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime})
and (iii) there is a finite special morphism
\pi^{\prime}:\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
such that
\pi\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\pi^{\prime}=\varpi|_{\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}}
. Taking Zariksi closure, we see that there is a finite cover
\varpi|_{\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}}:{\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}
.
Note that
\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{F}}
has non-empty ordinary locus, so by restricting, we obtain a finite cover
\varpi|_{{\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}}:{\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. Passing to normalization, it further extends to a finite cover
\widetilde{\varpi}:\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. Now by what we have established so far,
\pi^{\prime}
extends to a surjective morphism
\tilde{\pi}^{\prime}:\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
, and by uniqueness of the extension,
\widetilde{\varpi}=\tilde{\pi}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\tilde{\pi}^{\prime}
. Since
\widetilde{\varpi}
is finite and
\tilde{\pi}^{\prime}
is surjective,
\tilde{\pi}
is finite.

The final assertion on isomorphism follows simply from the unique extension property.

\hfill\square

Definition 2
.

Notation as above. An irreducible subvariety
Y\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{g,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
is called
quasi-weakly special
, if it is either a point, or there is

(1)

an embedding of Shimura data
(G,\mathfrak{X})\hookrightarrow(\GSp_{2g},\mathfrak{H}^{\pm})
and a decomposition
(G^{\ad},\mathfrak{X}^{\ad})=(G_{0},\mathfrak{X}_{0})\times(G_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{1})
,

(2)

for each
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, a special subvariety
\mathcal{X}_{\alpha}
that arises from
(G_{\alpha},\mathfrak{X}_{\alpha})
, such that there is a special finite map
\pi:\mathcal{X}_{0}\times\mathcal{X}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{g,K^{\prime}}
,

(3)

for
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, an ordinary naïve integral model
{\mathscr{X}}_{\alpha}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
of
\mathcal{X}_{\alpha}
,
such that
\dim{\mathscr{X}}_{0,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}>0
,

(4)

a subvariety
X_{1}\subseteq{\mathscr{X}}_{1,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
,

such that the Zariski closure
\overline{Y}
is the image under the map
\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}_{0}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\times\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}_{1}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{g,W^{\prime}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
(as guaranteed by Lemma
4.6
) of the preimage of
{\mathscr{X}}_{0,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\times X_{1}
in
\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}_{0,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\times\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}_{1,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. We say that
Y
is an
almost product
of
{\mathscr{X}}_{0,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
with
X_{1}
.
Loosely speaking, up to a finite cover,
\overline{Y}
splits as the product of a positive dimensional special subvariety and a possibly non-special subvariety.

4.4.
Conjectures, implications and results

In the following we state the conjectures for products of GSpin Shimura varieties. Let
(X,x),X_{0},f,\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
be as in §
4.1.1
.

Conjecture 4.7
.

\mathscr{X}_{f,\mathbb{F},\red}^{/x}=\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
.

Conjecture 4.8

(Tate-linear conjecture for products of GSpin Shimura varieties)
.

Suppose
f
is a locally closed immersion, i.e.,
X
is a subvariety of
\mathscr{S}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}
. If
X
is Tate-linear at
x\in X(\mathbb{F})
, then
X
is special.

Conjecture 4.9

(Characteristic
p
analogue of the Mumford–Tate conjecture for ordinary strata of products of GSpin Shimura varieties)
.

For every
u\in\mathrm{fpl}(\mathbb{Q})
, the inclusion
G(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}\subseteq\Hg(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
in Proposition
4.4
is an equality.

Conjecture 4.10

(Characteristic
p
analogue of the André–Oort conjecture for ordinary strata of products of GSpin Shimura varieties)
.

Let
f
be a locally closed immersion, i.e.,
X
is a subvariety of
\mathscr{S}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}
. Suppose that
X
possesses a Zariski dense collection
\bm{A}
of positive dimensional special subvarieties. Then
X
is quasi-weakly special. If
\#\mathbf{I}=1
, then
X
is special.

The almost product structure of
X
can further be made precise: let
\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}}\subseteq\mathbf{I}
be the set of indices
i
such that
\bm{A}
contains a Zariski dense collection of special subvarieties whose projections to
\mathscr{S}_{i,\mathbb{F}}
are
positive dimensional. Then

(1)

For
i\in\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}}
, the projection
X_{i}
of
X
to
\mathscr{S}_{i,\mathbb{F}}
is a special subvariety,

(2)

X
is the product of a quasi-weakly special subvariety
Y\subseteq\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}},\mathbb{F}}
with a subvariety of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}},\mathbb{F}}
,

(3)

for
i\in\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}}
, decompose the Shimura datum of
X_{i}
from (1) into simple adjoint factors, then there is a collection of ordinary naïve integral models
\{\mathscr{Y}_{j}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\}_{j\in\mathbf{J}}
corresponding to the simple factors, together with a finite morphism
\prod_{j\in\mathbf{J}}\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}_{j,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}},\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
as guaranteed by Lemma
4.6
. Let
\mathbf{J}_{\bm{A}}\subseteq\mathbf{J}
be the set of indices
j
such that
\bm{A}
contains a Zariski dense collection of special subvarieties whose projections
9
9

9

By this we mean first lift to
\prod_{j\in\mathbf{J}}\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}_{j,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
then project to
{\mathscr{Y}}_{j,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
.
to
{\mathscr{Y}}_{j,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
are positive dimensional. Then
Y
is an almost product of a special subvariety of
\prod_{j\in\mathbf{J}_{\bm{A}}}{\mathscr{Y}}_{j,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
with a subvariety of
\prod_{j\in\mathbf{J}-\mathbf{J}_{\bm{A}}}{\mathscr{Y}}_{j,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. In particular, if each
X_{i}
is already simple, then
Y
is a special subvariety.

Proposition 4.11

(Implications between various conjectures)
.

The following are true:

(1)

Conjecture
4.7

\Rightarrow
Conjecture
4.8
, Theorem
1.10
, Theorem
1.9
,

(2)

Conjecture
4.7

+
Conjecture
4.9

\Rightarrow
Theorem
1.3
,

(3)

Conjecture
4.10

\Rightarrow
Theorem
1.4
, Theorem
1.6
, Theorem
1.7
.

Proof.

(1)

Let
X
be as in Conjecture
4.8
. Conjecture
4.7
implies the existence of a Shimura subvariety
\mathcal{X}_{f}
such that
\mathscr{X}_{f,\mathbb{F},\red}^{/x}=\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
. Therefore
\overline{X}
is an irreducible component of
\mathscr{X}_{f,\mathbb{F}}
, hence special.

(2)

From Remark
4.1
(
2
) and the fact that
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}
contains
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
, we have
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}=\MTT(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
. What remains is to show that
\mathcal{X}_{f}
is the smallest special variety whose mod
p
reduction contains
f
. Let
\mathcal{Y}
be an irreducible special subvariety of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{I}}
such that
f
factors through the naïve integral model
\mathscr{Y}
, we need to show that
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
up to Hecke translation. We have at least two arguments: 1) let
\MTT(\mathcal{Y})
be the generic Mumford–Tate group of
\mathcal{Y}
. A similar fundamental group trick as in Proposition
4.4
shows that for
u\neq p
,
\MTT(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}=G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}\subseteq\MTT(\mathcal{Y})_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
. It follows that
\MTT(f)\subseteq\MTT(\mathcal{Y})
up to conjugation. 2) up to Hecke translation, we can assume that
\tilde{x}\in\mathscr{X}_{f}\cap\mathscr{Y}
. Since
\mathcal{X}_{f}\cap\mathcal{Y}
is again special, one can apply
[
39
, Theorem 3.7]
to conclude that
\dim\mathcal{X}_{f}=\rk\mathscr{T}_{f,x}=\dim(\mathcal{X}_{f}\cap\mathcal{Y})
. So
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
.

(3)

is clear.
\hfill\square

Conjecture
4.7
and
4.9
for a single GSpin Shimura variety will be established in §
6
, see Theorem
6.9
,
6.16
. The product version of these conjectures will be established in §
7
, see Theorem
7.11
. Conjecture
4.10
will be treated in the last section §
8
, see Theorem
8.4
,
8.7
.

5.
Lie theory of orthogonal and unitary groups

In this section, we aim to establish the necessary amount of Lie theoretical results for the study of monodromy groups of crystalline local systems. These results serve as an essential technical component in the proof of both Tate-linear and Mumford–Tate conjectures for GSpin Shimura varieties. This section is intended to be used solely for reference.

5.1.
Setups

Let
(M,Q^{\prime})
be a vector space over an algebraic closed field
K
of char 0, equipped with a nondegenerate quadratic pairing
Q^{\prime}
. In our applications, the space
(M,Q^{\prime})
will be a quadratic subspace of
(L,Q)\otimes K
, where
(L,Q)
is the quadratic
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice involved in defining a GSpin Shimura datum. In the following we fix:

(1)

a basis
\{w,v_{1},...,v_{n},w^{\prime}\}
of
M
, such that the pairing
Q^{\prime}
has the form

{Q}^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}&&1\\
\ &Q^{\prime}_{0}&\\
1&&\end{bmatrix},

(2)

a cocharacter
\nu:\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\GL(M),\,\,t\rightarrow\diag(t^{-1},1,1,...,1,t)
,

(3)

quadratic subspaces
M_{0}=\Span_{K}\{v_{1},...,v_{n}\}
and
B=\Span_{K}\{w,w^{\prime}\}
.

We will use
G
to denote a connected reductive subgroup of
\SO(M)
containing the image of
\nu
. Let
Lv_{G,\nu}
be the Levi of
G
corresponding to
\nu
. We will canonically identify it as the subgroup of
G
that commutes with
\nu
. For example,
Lv_{G,\nu}\subseteq Lv_{\SO(M),\nu}=\im\nu\times\SO(M_{0})\subseteq\SO(M)
.

Let
G_{0}
be the projection of
Lv_{G,\nu}
to
\SO(M_{0})
. For example,
\SO(M)_{0}=\SO(M_{0})
. The assumption that
\im\nu\subseteq G
implies that
Lv_{G,\nu}=G_{0}\times\im\nu
. The group
G_{0}
can be canonically identified as a subgroup of
G
, namely,
G_{0}=G\cap\SO(M_{0})
. Denote by
U_{G,\nu}

resp
.
U_{G,\nu^{-1}}
the unipotent
resp
. opposite unipotnent of
G
. Moreover, let
{U}_{G,\nu^{\pm}}
be the subgroup generated by
U_{G,\nu}
and
U_{G,\nu^{-1}}
.

In our applications,
G
will be taken as the group
\SO(M)
itself, or a certain unitary subgroup
\SU(\varphi)\subseteq\SO(M)
. As we will see, these groups arise as the monodromy groups of certain overconvergent sub-
F
-isocrystals of the crystalline local system
\mathbb{L}_{p,X}
over some geometrically connected smooth base
X/\mathbb{F}_{q}
.

5.1.1.
Unitary subgroups

Consider the diagonal embedding
K\hookrightarrow K^{\prime}:=K\times K
. We shall regard it as a quadratic extension of
K
with an involution
\iota\in\Aut_{K}(K^{\prime})
swapping two copies of
K
. We have a good notion of Hermitian forms over a
K^{\prime}
-space.

The quadratic subspace
B
can be endowed with an Hermitian form over
K^{\prime}
. Under the basis
w
and
w^{\prime}
, we let

\varphi_{B}=\begin{bmatrix}&(0,1)\\
(1,0)&\end{bmatrix}.

Then
\Tr{\varphi_{B}}=Q^{\prime}|_{B}
, and
w,w^{\prime}
are both totally isotropic. The unitary subgroup
\SU(\varphi_{B})
is nothing other than
\im\nu
.

We say that
(M,\varphi)
is an
Hermitian space over
K^{\prime}
respecting

Q^{\prime}
, if
M
decomposes into a direct sum of Hermitian spaces
(M_{0},\varphi_{0})\oplus(B,\varphi_{B})
such that
\Tr\varphi_{0}=Q^{\prime}_{0}
. When this is the case, we automatically have
\Tr\varphi=Q^{\prime}
.

These give rise to unitary subgroups
\SU(\varphi)\subseteq\SO(M)
and
\SU(\varphi_{0})\subseteq\SO(M_{0})
, such that
\SU(\varphi)_{0}=\SU(\varphi_{0})
.
Note also that
\varphi_{0}
induces a decomposition
M_{0}=\mathcal{W}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{0}
such that
\mathcal{W}_{0}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{0}
are mutually dual maximal totally isotropic subspaces. In this case,
\SU(\varphi_{0})\simeq\GL(\mathcal{W}_{0})
.

Similarly,
\varphi
induces a decomposition of
M
into mutually dual maximal totally isotropic subspaces
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}_{0}\oplus\Span_{K}\{w\}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}=\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}\oplus\Span_{K}\{w^{\prime}\}
, and we have
\im\nu\subseteq\SU(\varphi)\simeq\GL(\mathcal{W})
.

5.1.2.
Root systems

Suppose
G
contains the image of
\nu
. We fix a maximal torus
T_{G}
that is contained in
Lv_{G,\nu}
. Then the image of
T_{G}
in
G_{0}
is also a maximal torus, which we denote by
T_{G_{0}}
. We always have
T_{G}=T_{G_{0}}\times\im\nu
. In the rest of the paper, we will always choose maximal tori in such way.

Let
\Phi_{G}\subseteq X^{*}(T_{G})_{\mathbb{R}}
and

\Phi_{G_{0}}\subseteq X^{*}(T_{G_{0}})_{\mathbb{R}}

be the root systems that arise from the choice of maximal tori. We have a canonical embedding
X^{*}(T_{G_{0}})_{\mathbb{R}}\subseteq X^{*}(T_{G})_{\mathbb{R}}
, which identifies
\Phi_{G_{0}}
as a sub-root system of
\Phi_{G}
, and
\Phi_{G_{0}}=\Phi_{G}\cap X^{*}(T_{G_{0}})_{\mathbb{R}}
. Note that we always have a splitting
X^{*}(T_{G})=X^{*}(T_{G_{0}})\oplus X^{*}(\im\nu)
. Let
\Phi_{G,\nu}

resp
.
\Phi_{G,\nu}^{+}

resp
.
\Phi_{G,\nu}^{-}
be the set of roots

resp
. positive roots (i.e., the roots generating
U_{G,\nu}
)
resp
. negative roots (i.e., the roots generating
U_{G,\nu^{-1}}
) in
\Phi_{G}-\Phi_{G_{0}}
.

We will also be using Lie algebras. The symbol
\mathfrak{so}(M)

resp
.
\mathfrak{u}(\varphi)

resp
.
\mathfrak{g}

will be used to denote the Lie algebra of
\SO(M)

resp
.
\SU(\varphi)

resp
.
G
. Furthermore, we use
\mathfrak{t}_{\bullet}
to denote the Lie algebra of a torus
T_{\bullet}
. For example,
\mathfrak{t}_{G}
is the Lie algebra of
T_{G}
. If
\alpha\in\Phi_{G}
is a root, we sill use
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
to denote the root sub-algebra of
\mathfrak{g}
associated to
\alpha
.

It is known that,
\Phi_{\SO(M)}
has Dynkin diagram
D_{\lceil\frac{n+1}{2}\rceil}
when
n
is even, and
B_{\lceil\frac{n+1}{2}\rceil}
when
n
is odd. Suppose
(M,\varphi)
is an is an Hermitian space over
K^{\prime}
respecting
Q^{\prime}
in the sense of §
5.1.1
, then
\Phi_{\SU(\varphi)}
has Dynkin diagram
A_{\frac{n}{2}}
.

5.2.
Key lemmas

Our main goal is to prove Lemma
5.2
, which will play an essential role in analysing the structure of certain crystalline monodromy groups.

Lemma 5.1
.

Fix a maximal torus
T_{\SO(M)}
as in §
5.1.2
. Let
\alpha\in\Phi_{\SO(M),\nu}^{+}
. Suppose that
\mathfrak{h}
is an one dimensional sub-algebra of
\Lie{U}_{\SO(M),\nu^{-1}}
such that (1)
\mathfrak{t}:=[\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{so}(M)_{\alpha}]
is an one dimensional sub-algebra of
\mathfrak{t}_{\SO(M)}
and (2)
[\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{h}]\subseteq\mathfrak{h}
. Then
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{so}(M)_{-\alpha}
.

Proof.

This can be checked using root systems. Choose a generator
E_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{so}(M)_{\alpha}
. For each
\beta\in\Phi_{\SO(M),\nu}^{-}
, we also pick a generator
E_{\beta}\in\mathfrak{so}(M)_{\beta}
. Then a generator of
\mathfrak{h}
can be written as
\sum c_{\beta}E_{\beta}
, where
c_{\beta}\in K
. Now (1) implies that
c_{\beta}=0
unless
\beta
equals
-\alpha
or
s(\alpha)
: the reflection of
\alpha
along the
plane perpendicular to
X^{*}(\im\nu)
. Moreover
c_{-\alpha}\neq 0
and
\mathfrak{t}
is generated by
[E_{-\alpha},E_{\alpha}]
. If
-\alpha=s(\alpha)
then we are done. Otherwise, (2) implies that
[[E_{-\alpha},E_{\alpha}],E_{-\alpha}+cE_{s(\alpha)}]\subseteq\mathfrak{h}
. By Jacobi identity, the left hand side is a nonzero multiple of
E_{-\alpha}
. As a result,
c=0
and
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{so}(M)_{-\alpha}
.
\hfill\square

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose
M_{0}
admits an orthogonal decomposition
M_{0}=M_{a,0}\oplus M_{b,0}
. Let
M_{a}=M_{a,0}\oplus B
and
M_{b}=M_{b,0}\oplus B
. Assume that
(M_{b},\varphi_{b})
is an Hermitian space over
K^{\prime}
respecting
Q^{\prime}|_{M_{b}}
in the sense of §
5.1.1
. That is,
(M_{b},\varphi_{b})=(M_{b,0},\varphi_{b,0})\oplus(B,\varphi_{B})
and
\Tr\varphi_{b,0}=Q^{\prime}|_{M_{b,0}}
.
Suppose there is a connected reductive group
G\subseteq\SO(M)
containing the image of
\nu
, such that

(1)

G_{0}\subseteq\SO(M_{a,0})\times\SU(\varphi_{b,0})
,

(2)

U_{G,\nu}=U_{\SO(M_{a}),\nu}\times U_{\SU(\varphi_{b}),\nu}
.

Then either
M_{a,0}=0
and
G=\SU(\varphi_{b})
, or
M_{b,0}=0
and
G=\SO(M)
.

Proof.

We begin by remarking that
U_{\SO(M_{a}),\nu}
and
U_{\SU(\varphi_{b}),\nu}
commute with each other, and have trivial intersection, so condition (
2
) makes sense. We also remind the readers that

\SO(M_{a,0})=\SO(M_{a})_{0}
and
\SU(\varphi_{b,0})=\SU(\varphi_{b})_{0}
. Fix maximal tori
T_{\SO(M_{0})}
,
T_{\SO(M_{a,0})}
,
T_{\SU(\varphi_{b,0})}
and
T_{G_{0}}
, in the manner that
T_{G_{0}}\subseteq T_{\SO(M_{a,0})}\times T_{\SU(\varphi_{b,0})}\subseteq T_{\SO(M_{0})}
. Let
T_{\SO(M)}
,
T_{\SO(M_{a})}
,
T_{\SU(\varphi_{b})}
and
T_{G}
be the products of
\im\nu
with
T_{\SO(M_{0})}
,
T_{\SO(M_{a,0})}
,
T_{\SU(\varphi_{b,0})}
and
T_{G_{0}}
, respectively. To ease notation, we also set
T_{H_{0}}=T_{\SO(M_{a,0})}\times T_{\SU(\varphi_{b,0})}
and
T_{H}=T_{H_{0}}\times\im\nu
.

Claim
.

U_{G,\nu^{-1}}=U_{\SO(M_{a}),\nu^{-1}}\times U_{\SU(\varphi_{b}),\nu^{-1}}.

For dimension reasons, it suffices to show that
U_{G,\nu^{-1}}
contains the right hand side. We will achieve this by showing that
\mathfrak{g}
contains all root sub-algebras of
\mathfrak{so}(M_{a})
and
\mathfrak{u}(\varphi_{b})
that are associated to the negative roots.
Note that
T_{\SO(M_{a,0})}
commutes with
\SU(\varphi_{b})
, whereas
T_{\SU(\varphi_{b,0})}
commutes with
\SO(M_{a})
. In addition,
T_{\SO(M)}
contains the central torus of
\SU(\varphi)
. By considering the adjunction action of the various maximal tori on the root sub-algebras, we see that

(a)

the root sub-algebra of
\mathfrak{so}(M_{a})

resp
.
\mathfrak{u}(\varphi_{b})
associated to a positive root is also a root sub-algebra of
\mathfrak{g}
associated to a positive root.

(b)

the root sub-algebra of
\mathfrak{so}(M_{a})

resp
.
\mathfrak{u}(\varphi_{b})
associated to an arbitrary root is a root sub-algebra of
\mathfrak{so}(M)
.

Let
\varpi:X^{*}(T_{H})_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow X^{*}(T_{G})_{\mathbb{R}}
be the natural map. From (a), we see that
\varpi
carries
\Phi_{\SO(M_{a}),\nu}^{+}\sqcup\Phi_{\SU(\varphi_{b}),\nu}^{+}
bijectively to
\Phi_{G,\nu}^{+}
, and

\mathfrak{g}_{\varpi(\alpha)}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathfrak{so}(M_{a})_{\alpha},\,\,&\alpha\in\Phi_{\SO(M_{a}),\nu}^{+},\\
&\mathfrak{su}(\varphi_{b})_{\alpha},\,\,&\alpha\in\Phi_{\SU(\varphi_{b}),\nu}^{+}.\end{aligned}\right.

From (b), we see that
\mathfrak{g}_{\varpi(\alpha)}
are root sub-algebras of
\mathfrak{so}(M)
, i.e.,
\mathfrak{g}_{\varpi(\alpha)}=\mathfrak{so}(M)_{\alpha}

10
10

10

By abuse of notation, we write a root subalgebra of
\mathfrak{so}(M)
that arises as in (b) as
\mathfrak{so}(M)_{\alpha}
.
. Since
\mathfrak{g}_{-\varpi(\alpha)}
, being the opposite root sub-algebra of
\mathfrak{g}_{\varpi(\alpha)}
, is an
\mathfrak{h}
in the context of Lemma
5.1
. Therefore, we must have
\mathfrak{g}_{-\varpi(\alpha)}=\mathfrak{so}(M)_{-\alpha}
. As a result,
\mathfrak{g}
contains all root sub-algebras of
\mathfrak{so}(M_{a})
and
\mathfrak{u}(\varphi_{b})
that are associated to negative roots. Therefore,
Claim
is true.

Recall that
U_{G,\nu^{\pm}}
is the subgroup of
G
generated by
U_{G,\nu}
and
U_{G,\nu^{-1}}
. By
Claim
and (
2
),
{U}_{\SO(M_{a}),\nu^{\pm}}\times{U}_{\SU(\varphi_{b}),\nu^{\pm}}\subseteq{U}_{G,\nu^{\pm}}
. On the other hand, a simple computation using root systems implies that
{U}_{\SO(M_{a}),\nu^{\pm}}=\SO(M_{a})
and
{U}_{\SU(\varphi_{b}),\nu^{\pm}}=\SSU(\varphi_{b})
. Since
G
contains
\im\nu
, it contains both
\SO(M_{a})
and
\SU(\varphi_{b})
.
Therefore

(5.2.1)

G_{0}=\SO(M_{a,0})\times\SU(\varphi_{b,0}).

In particular,
T_{G_{0}}=T_{H_{0}}
and
T_{G}=T_{H}
. Recall that
T_{H}=T_{\SO(M_{a,0})}\times T_{\SU(\varphi_{b,0})}\times\im\nu
, so
X^{*}(T_{\SO(M_{a})})
and
X^{*}(T_{\SU(\varphi_{b})})
sit inside
X^{*}(T_{H})
. We have
\Phi_{G_{0}}=\Phi_{\SO(M_{a,0})}\sqcup\Phi_{\SUU(\varphi_{b,0})}
and
\Phi_{G,\nu}^{+}=\Phi_{\SO(M_{a}),\nu}^{+}\sqcup\Phi_{\SUU(\varphi_{b}),\nu}^{+}
.
Furthermore,
\Phi_{G}\cap X^{*}(T_{\SO(M_{a})})=\Phi_{\SO(M_{a})}
and
\Phi_{G}\cap X^{*}(T_{\SO(\varphi_{b})})=\Phi_{\SU(\varphi_{b})}
. If
\dim M_{a,0}\times\dim M_{b,0}>0
, one can write down explicit root systems for
\Phi_{\SO(M_{a})}
and
\Phi_{\SU(\varphi_{b})}
and show that
\Phi_{G}
is not closed under reflection along the plane perpendicular to a root, hence not a root system. Therefore
\dim M_{a,0}\times\dim M_{b,0}=0
.
As we noted before,
G
contains both
\SO(M_{a})
and
\SU(\varphi_{b})
. If
M_{b,0}=0
, then
G=\SO(M)
. If
M_{a,0}=0
, then
G=\SU(\varphi_{b})
.
\hfill\square

6.
Tate-linear and Mumford–Tate conjectures: the single case

In this section we prove Conjecture
4.7
and Conjecture
4.9
for single GSpin Shimura varieties. In §
6.1
we introduce several important lattices that arise from the formal torus
\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
. We then relate these lattices to the monodromy of
F
-isocrystals in §
6.2
. After that, we use the monodromy results and Lie theory lemmas (§
5
) to construct a finite subset
\bm{\Delta}\subseteq\End(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{I},\overline{\eta}})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
with certain special properties (such a subset is called
sufficient
, see Definition
3
). The existence of such a subset
is sufficient for proving Conjecture
4.7
in the case of GSpin Shimura varieties (§
6.3
).
This result, together with the independence of monodromy groups in a compatible system, leads us to prove Conjecture
4.9
in the case of GSpin Shimura varieties. As note in Proposition
4.11
, this proves Conjecture
1.1
for GSpin Shimura varieties.

6.1.
The Tate-linear character and cocharacter lattices

We refer the readers to §
4.1.1
and §
4.4
for setups and notations. Let
\Psi_{\mathbf{I}},\formalBr_{\mathbf{I}}
be the products of (extended) formal Brauer groups over indices running through
\mathbf{I}
. These are
p
-divisible groups over
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. Let
{\mu}_{i}:\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\GL(H_{i,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})
be the canonical Hodge cocharactes of
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{i,x}[p^{\infty}]
and
\overline{\mu}_{i}:\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\GL(\overline{L}_{i,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})
be the induced cocharacter, which is indeed the canonical Hodge cocharactes of
\Psi_{i,x}
. We make the following convention

(6.1.1)

\displaystyle\GL^{\prime}(H_{\mathbf{I}})=\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\GL(H_{i}),

\displaystyle\GL^{\prime}(\overline{L}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Z}_{p}})=\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\GL(\overline{L}_{i,\mathbb{Z}_{p}}).

Let
{\mu}_{\mathbf{I}}:\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\GSpin^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})

resp
.
{\mu}_{\mathbf{I}}^{c}:\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\SO^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})

resp
.
\overline{\mu}_{\mathbf{I}}:\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\GL^{\prime}(\overline{L}_{\mathbf{I},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})
be the product of
\mu_{i}
’s
resp
.
\mu_{i}^{c}
’s
resp
.
\overline{\mu}_{i}
’s. As usual, let
U_{\GSpin^{\prime},\mu_{\mathbf{I}}}

resp
.
U_{\SO^{\prime},\mu^{c}_{\mathbf{I}}}

resp
.
U_{\GL^{\prime},\overline{\mu}_{\mathbf{I}}}
be the corresponding unipotent, and let
U_{\GSpin^{\prime},\mu_{\mathbf{I}}^{-1}}

resp
.
U_{\SO^{\prime},\mu^{c,-1}_{\mathbf{I}}}

resp
.
U_{\GL^{\prime},\overline{\mu}_{\mathbf{I}}^{-1}}
be the corresponding opposite unipotent. By abuse of notation, we will also use above notations to denote the generic fibers of the respective group schemes over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. According to Remark
2.7
, the arithmetic deformation space
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{\mathbf{I},W}
admits three equivalent formal torus structures:

(6.1.2)

\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{\mathbf{I},W}\simeq\left\{\begin{aligned} &U_{\GSpin^{\prime},{\mu}_{\mathbf{I}}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge},\\
&U_{\SO^{\prime},{\mu}^{c}_{\mathbf{I}}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge},\\
&U_{\GL^{\prime},\overline{\mu}_{\mathbf{I}}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}.\end{aligned}\right.

Note that the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-lattices
X_{*}(\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{\mathbf{I},W})
and
X_{*}(\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}})
are canonically identified.

Definition 3
.

(1)

The sublattice
X_{*}(\mathscr{T}_{f,x})\subseteq X_{*}(\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{\mathbf{I},W})
is called the Tate-linear cocharacter lattice, denoted by
\mathcal{T}_{f,x}
. Let
T_{f,x}:=\mathcal{T}_{f,x}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Under identifications (
6.1.2
),
{T}_{f,x}
gives rise to unipotent
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-subgroups of
U_{\GSpin^{\prime},{\mu}_{\mathbf{I}}}
,
U_{\SO^{\prime},{\mu}^{c}_{\mathbf{I}}}
and
U_{\GL^{\prime},\overline{\mu}_{\mathbf{I}}}
. When the context is clear, these unipotent subgroups (or their
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-points) will again be denoted by
{T}_{f,x}
.

(2)

Make identification of fibers as in §
4.2
. Let
\bm{\Delta}\subseteq\End(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{I},\overline{\eta}})\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\subseteq\End(H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
be a subset. Recall that
\GSpin^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
acts on
H_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. The subgroup
T\subseteq U_{\GSpin^{\prime},\mu_{\mathbf{I}}}
that
commutes with
\bm{\Delta}
always contains
T_{f,x}
. We say that
\bm{\Delta}
is
sufficient
, if
T=T_{f,x}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

If
\End(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{I},\overline{\eta}})\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
contains a sufficient subset
\bm{\Delta}
, then Conjecture
4.7
holds.

Proof.

Let
\bm{\Delta}_{0}
be a subset that lies in
\End(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{I},\overline{\eta}})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, such that the
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-subspaces spanned by
\bm{\Delta}_{0}
and
\bm{\Delta}
are the same.
As in (
2.3.2
), we have a deformation space
\mathscr{D}_{\bm{\Delta}_{0}}:=\Def(\bm{\Delta}_{0}/\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{\mathbf{I},W})\subseteq\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{\mathbf{I},W}
. By Proposition
2.6
and discussions in §
2.4.1
,
\mathscr{D}_{\bm{\Delta}_{0},\red}
is a formal subtorus of
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{\mathbf{I},W}
.
Combining Lemma
2.5
, the definition of sufficiency implies that

X_{*}(\mathscr{D}_{\bm{\Delta}_{0},\red})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}=T_{f,x}
. As a result,
\mathscr{D}_{\bm{\Delta}_{0},\mathbb{F}}
admits
\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
as the induced reduced structure. On the other hand,
\bm{\Delta}_{0}
deforms to
\mathscr{X}_{f,W}^{/x,+}
by Lemma
4.3
(
2
), hence
\mathscr{X}_{f,\mathbb{F},\red}^{/x}\subseteq\mathscr{D}_{\bm{\Delta}_{0},\mathbb{F},\red}=\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
. Since
f
factors through
\mathscr{X}_{f,\mathbb{F}}
by Lemma
4.3
(
3
), we have
\mathscr{T}_{f,x}\subseteq\mathscr{X}_{f,\mathbb{F},\red}^{/x}
, thus

\mathscr{X}_{f,\mathbb{F},\red}^{/x}=\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
.
\hfill\square

6.2.
Local and global monodromy of
\mathbb{L}^{-}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}}

Let
X,X_{0},f,x
be as in §
4.1.1
.
Let
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}}^{-}
be the underlying
F
-isocrystal of
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}}
and
\omega_{x}
be the fiber functor of the Tannakian category
\langle\mathbb{L}^{-}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}}\rangle^{\otimes}\subseteq\FIsoc(X_{0})
defined in §
3.2
.
We have two exact sequences

(6.2.1)

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\mathbb{D}(\formalBr_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}})^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}})\rightarrow 0.

(6.2.2)

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}})\rightarrow\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}})\rightarrow\mathbb{D}(\formalBr_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}})\rightarrow 0.

The existence of nondegenerate pairings
\mathbf{Q}_{i}
over each
F
-crystal
\mathbf{L}_{i,p}
guarantees that the natural projection

(6.2.3)

G(\mathbb{L}^{-}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}},x)\twoheadrightarrow G(\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}),x)

is an isomorphism. Therefore, to study the monodromy of
\mathbb{L}^{-}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}}
, it suffices to study the monodromy of
\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}})
. Since
\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}
is ordinary, the techniques from §
3.2.1
apply.

Remark 6.2
.

In order to carry out concrete computations, it is convenient to have an explicit description of the isomorphism (
6.2.3
). It suffices to figure out the case when
\#\mathbf{I}=1
. In the following, we assume
\#\mathbf{I}=1
and drop all the subscript
\mathbf{I}
. Arrange the basis
\{e_{i}\}
of
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}})
so that
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\formalBr))=\Span_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\{e_{b+2}\}
,
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\Psi))=\Span_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\{e_{2},...,e_{b+2}\}
and the quadratic pairing over
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{p,x})
is given by

\mathbf{Q}=\begin{bmatrix}&&1\\
\ &\mathbf{Q}_{0}&\\
1&&\end{bmatrix}.

For a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebra
R
, an element
g\in G(\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{X_{0}}),x)(R)
is of the form

(6.2.4)

g=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{v}\\
&\lambda\end{bmatrix},\,\,\lambda\in\mathbb{G}_{m}(R),\,\,\mathbf{B}\in\SO(\mathbf{Q}_{0})(R),\,\,\mathbf{v}\in U_{\GL,\overline{\mu}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\otimes R.

Then the preimage of
g
under (
6.2.3
) is

(6.2.5)

\begin{bmatrix}\lambda^{-1}&-\lambda^{-1}\mathbf{v}^{t}\mathbf{Q}_{0}\mathbf{B}&-\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{-1}\mathbf{v}^{t}\mathbf{Q}_{0}\mathbf{v}\\
&\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{v}\\
&&\lambda\end{bmatrix}.

6.2.1.
Local monodromy

Let
\Psi_{i}^{/x}
be the pullback of
\Psi_{i}
to
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{i,\mathbb{F}}
and write

\Psi_{\mathbf{I}}^{/x}=\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\Psi_{i}^{/x}
. We have
\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X}^{/x})=\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X})^{/x}
. By Lemma
3.1
, the local monodromy is

G(\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X})^{/x},x)=U(\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X})^{/x},{x})\rtimes\im\overline{\mu}_{\mathbf{I}}.

Proposition 6.3
.

Notations being the same as §
3.2
. We have

(1)

Regard
T_{f,x}
as a subgroup of
U_{\GL^{\prime},\overline{\mu}_{\mathbf{I}}}
. We have

U(\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}),{x})=U(\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X})^{/x},{x})=T_{f,x}.

(2)

Regard
T_{f,x}
as a subgroup of
U_{\SO^{\prime},{\mu}^{c}_{\mathbf{I}}}
. We have

U(\mathbb{L}^{-}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}},{x})=U(\mathbb{L}^{-,/x}_{\mathbf{I},p,X},{x})=T_{f,x}.

Proof.

Providing the isomorphism (
6.2.3
), it suffices to prove (
1
).
Let
\Lambda=U_{\GL^{\prime},\overline{\mu}_{\mathbf{I}}^{-1}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
, and let
X^{/x}=\Spf R
.
Consider the pairing
q\in\Hom(\Lambda,\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}(R))
that arises from
\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X}^{/x}
.
Since
R
is reduced,
\ker(q)
is saturated in
\Lambda
. Let
\ker(q)^{\perp}
be the sub-lattice of
\Lambda^{\vee}
that pairs to 0 with
\ker(q)
. Then
\ker(q)^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}
is the smallest subtorus of
\Lambda^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}=\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}^{/x}
through which
f^{/x}
factors. This shows that
\mathcal{T}_{f,x}=\ker(q)^{\perp}
. We are done by Theorem
3.2
.
\hfill\square

6.2.2.
Global monodromy

By Theorem
3.2
and Proposition
6.3
, the structure of the global monodromy can be understood as

(6.2.6)

\displaystyle G(\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}),x)=T_{f,x}\rtimes G(\gr\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}),x),

(6.2.7)

\displaystyle G(\mathbb{L}^{-}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}},x)=T_{f,x}\rtimes G(\gr\mathbb{L}^{-}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}},x).

The following recovers an independence result of Chai (
[
4
]
) in the case of products of GSpin Shimura varieties:

Corollary 6.4
.

The rank of the formal subtorus
\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
is independent of
x
chosen. In particular, if
X_{0}
is Tate-linear at one point, it is Tate-linear at all points.

Proof.

For different
x
, the groups
G(\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}),{x})
are isomorphic. Similarly, for different
x
, the groups
G(\gr\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}),{x})
are also isomorphic. Therefore
\dim U(\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X})^{/x},{x})
are the same for all
x
. It now follows from Proposition
6.3
that
\rk\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
is independent of
x
chosen.
\hfill\square

6.3.
Isocrystals and their monodromy in the single GSpin case

In the following three sections we suppose that
\#\mathbf{I}=1
. We drop the subscript
\mathbf{I}
as usual. Let

b=\dim\mathcal{S}
and
d=\rk\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
. Recall that
\mathbf{Q}
and
\mathbf{Q}_{0}
are the quadratic pairings over
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{p})
and
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\Psi^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}))
, respectively. The following identification is canonical up to scalars

(6.3.1)

\displaystyle U_{\GL,\overline{\mu}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\simeq\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\formalBr))^{\vee}\otimes\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\Psi^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}))\simeq\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\Psi^{{\text{\'{e}t}}})).

From (
6.3.1
),
{T}_{f,x}
can be canonically considered as a quadratic sublattice of
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\Psi^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}))
. In this section, we will show that only very special kind of subtori of
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{\mathbb{F}}
can arise as

\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
. In fact, we will show that, as a quadratic subspace of
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\Psi^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}))
,
T_{f,x}
can only be nondegenerate or totally isotropic (Proposition
6.6
).

6.3.1.
The Tate-linear cocharacter
{T}_{f,x}

Let
T^{\perp}_{f,x}
be the orthogonal complement of
T_{f,x}
in
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\Psi^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}))
and let
U_{f,x}:=T_{f,x}\cap T^{\perp}_{f,x}
. Clearly,
T_{f,x},T^{\perp}_{f,x},U_{f,x}
are subrepresentations of
G(\mathbb{D}(\Psi^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}_{X_{0}}),x)
. Since
G(\mathbb{D}(\Psi^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}_{X_{0}}),x)
is reductive by Theorem
3.3
, there exist
G(\mathbb{D}(\Psi^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}_{X_{0}}),x)
-subrepresentations
V_{f,x}
,
V^{\prime}_{f,x}
and
U_{f,x}^{\prime}
such that

(6.3.2)

\displaystyle\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\Psi^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}))=U_{f,x}\oplus U_{f,x}^{\prime}\oplus V_{f,x}\oplus V_{f,x}^{\prime},

\displaystyle T_{f,x}=V_{f,x}\oplus U_{f,x},

\displaystyle T_{f,x}^{\perp}=U_{f,x}\oplus V_{f,x}^{\prime}.

We note that
U_{f,x}
is totally isotropic,
while
V_{f,x},V_{f,x}^{\prime}
and
U_{f,x}\oplus U_{f,x}^{\prime}
are nondegenerate. Furthermore,
\dim U_{f,x}=\dim U_{f,x}^{\prime}
, and
U_{f,x}
is a maximal totally isotropic subspace of
U_{f,x}\oplus U_{f,x}^{\prime}
. Therefore the map
G(\mathbb{D}(\Psi^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}_{X_{0}}),x)\rightarrow\SO(U_{f,x}\oplus U_{f,x}^{\prime})
factors through a unitary subgroup associated to an
Hermitian form
\varphi_{f,x,0}
with
\Tr\varphi_{f,x,0}=\mathbf{Q}_{0}|_{U_{f,x}\oplus U_{f,x}^{\prime}}
. Consequently, we might and do assume that
U_{f,x}^{\prime}
is totally isotropic and dual to
U_{f,x}
with respect to
\mathbf{Q}_{0}
. In a similar manner, we consider certain distinguished subspaces of
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{p})
. Let
B_{x}=\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\formalBr))\oplus\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\formalBr)^{\vee})
.
We define

(6.3.3)

\displaystyle M^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}_{f,x}:=U_{f,x}\oplus U_{f,x}^{\prime}\oplus V_{f,x},

\displaystyle M_{f,x}:=B_{x}\oplus M^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}_{f,x},

\displaystyle W_{f,x}:=U_{f,x}\oplus\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\formalBr)),

\displaystyle W_{f,x}^{\prime}:=U_{f,x}^{\prime}\oplus\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\formalBr)^{\vee}).

Note that
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\formalBr))
and
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\formalBr)^{\vee})

are one dimensional subspaces of
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{p})
, which are totally isotropic and dual to each other. So
W_{f,x}
and
W_{f,x}^{\prime}
are totally isotropic and dual to each other. Furthermore,
W_{f,x}\oplus W_{f,x}^{\prime}
is equipped with an Hermitian form
\varphi_{f,x}
such that
\Tr\varphi_{f,x}=\mathbf{Q}|_{W_{f,x}\oplus W_{f,x}^{\prime}}
and
\varphi_{f,x,0}=\varphi_{f,x}|_{U_{f,x}\oplus U^{\prime}_{f,x}}
.

Lemma 6.5
.

We have a splitting
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{p})=V_{f,x}^{\prime}\oplus M_{f,x}
as
G(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}},x)
-representations. Equivalently,
\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}}=\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{p,f}\oplus\mathbb{M}_{p,f}
in
\mathbf{F}
-
\mathbf{Isoc}^{\dagger}(X_{0})
.
If
V_{f,x}=0
, then we have a further splitting
M_{f,x}=W_{f,x}\oplus W_{f,x}^{\prime}
in the category of
G(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}},x)
-representations. Equivalently,
\mathbb{M}_{p,f}=\mathbb{W}_{p,f}\oplus\mathbb{W}_{p,f}^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{F}
-
\mathbf{Isoc}^{\dagger}(X_{0})
, and
\mathbb{W}_{p,f}
is dual to
\mathbb{W}_{p,f}^{\prime}
. (Here we denote by
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{p,f}
,
\mathbb{M}_{p,f}
,
\mathbb{W}_{p,f}
and
\mathbb{W}_{p,f}^{\prime}
the overconvergent
F
-isocrystals arising from
V_{f,x}^{\prime}
,
M_{f,x}
,
W_{f,x}
and
W_{f,x}^{\prime}
via Tannakian formalism. The subscript
p
, as usual, stands for “
\mathrm{cris}
”).

Proof.

Easy computation involving explicit formulas in Remark
6.2
shows that
V_{f,x}^{\prime}
and
M_{f,x}
are
G(\mathbb{L}^{-}_{p,X_{0}},x)
-subrepresentations of
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{p})
. So at least, we have
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{p})=V_{f,x}^{\prime}\oplus M_{f,x}
in the category of
G(\mathbb{L}^{-}_{p,X_{0}},x)
-subrepresentations. Let
\mathbb{V}^{\prime-}_{p,f}
and
\mathbb{M}_{p,f}^{-}
be the
F
-isocrystals corresponding to
V^{\prime}_{f,x}
and
M_{f,x}
. We have
\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}}^{-}=\mathbb{V}^{\prime-}_{p,f}\oplus\mathbb{M}_{p,f}^{-}
in the category of
F
-isocrystals. Note that, there is an idempotent
\alpha\in\End(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}}^{-})
such that
\ker\alpha=\mathbb{V}^{\prime-}_{p,f}
and
\im\alpha=\mathbb{M}_{p,f}^{-}
.
Since
\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}}
is overconvergent, Kedlaya’s result
[
26
]
implies that
\alpha\in\End(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}})
. Therefore
\ker\alpha
and
\im\alpha
are also overconvergent and
V_{f,x}^{\prime}
and
M_{f,x}
are
G(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}},x)
-subrepresentations of
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{p})
.

Now suppose that
V_{f,x}=0
, easy computation again shows that
M_{f,x}=W_{f,x}\oplus W_{f,x}^{\prime}
in the
category of
G(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}}^{-},x)
-representations. Let
\mathbb{W}^{-}_{p,f}
and
\mathbb{W}^{\prime-}_{p,f}
be the
F
-isocrystals corresponding to
W_{f,x}
and
W^{\prime}_{f,x}
. So
\mathbb{M}_{p,f}=\mathbb{W}_{p,f}^{-}\oplus\mathbb{W}^{\prime-}_{p,f}
at least in the category of
F
-isocrystals. Similar to the previous case,
[
26
]
again implies that
\mathbb{W}_{p,f}^{-}
and
\mathbb{W}^{\prime-}_{p,f}
are overconvergent. The assertion that
\mathbb{W}_{p,f}
and
\mathbb{W}^{\prime}_{p,f}
are mutually dual follows easily.
\hfill\square

6.3.2.
Structure of monodromy groups

As a direct consequence of Lemma
6.5
, we have

(6.3.4)

G(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}\subseteq G(\mathbb{V}_{p,f}^{\prime},x)^{\circ}\times G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)^{\circ},

and the projection of
G(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}
to each factor is surjective.

Proposition 6.6
.

Either
V_{f,x}=0
or
U_{f,x}=0
. In other words,
T_{f,x}
is either nondegenrate or totally isotropic. Furthermore, we have

G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)^{\circ}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\SO(M_{f,x}),\,\,&U_{f,x}=0,\\
&\SU(\varphi_{f,x}),\,\,&V_{f,x}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.5
, there is an overconvergent sub-
F
-isocrystal
\mathbb{M}_{p,f}\subseteq\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}}
. Let
\mathbb{M}_{p,f}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}\subseteq\mathbb{D}(\Psi^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}_{X_{0}})
be the sub-
F
-isocrystal corresponding to
M^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}_{f,x}
. Theorem
3.3
implies that
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)^{\circ}
is a reductive subgroup of
\SO(M_{f,x})
which admits
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f}^{-},x)^{\circ}
as the parabolic subgroup corresponding to
\mu^{c}
. The Levi of
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f}^{-},x)^{\circ}
is
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}},x)^{\circ}\times\im\mu^{c}
. The projection of
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}},x)^{\circ}
to
\GL(V_{f,x})

resp
.
\GL(U_{f,x}\oplus U_{f,x}^{\prime})
lies in
\SO(V_{f,x})

resp
.
\SU(\varphi_{f,x,0})
, so we have

(6.3.5)

G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}},x)^{\circ}\subseteq\SO(V_{f,x})\times\SU(\varphi_{f,x,0}).

The unipotent of
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f}^{-},x)^{\circ}
corresponding to
\mu^{c}
is
U(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}}^{-},x)
, which by Proposition
6.3
is
T_{f,x}
(considered as a subgroup of
U_{\SO,\mu^{c}}
). Therefore

(6.3.6)

U(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}}^{-},x)=U_{\SO(V_{f,x}\oplus B_{x}),\mu^{c}}\times U_{\SU(\varphi_{f,x}),\mu^{c}}.

Upon base changing to
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, we are in the situation of §
5
. In fact, let
K,M,M_{0}
and
B
in §
5
be
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p},M_{f,x},M_{f,x}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}
and
B_{x}
, respectively. Let
Q^{\prime},\nu,G
and
G_{0}
in §
5
be
\mathbf{Q}|_{M_{f,x}},\mu^{c}|_{\SO(M_{f,x})}
,
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)^{\circ}
and
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}},x)^{\circ}
, respectively.

We wish to apply Lemma
5.2
to
G=G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)^{\circ}
. In fact, it suffices to take
M_{a,0}=V_{f,x}
,
M_{b,0}=U_{f,x}\oplus U_{f,x}^{\prime}
and
(M_{b},\varphi_{b})=(U_{f,x}\oplus U_{f,x}^{\prime}\oplus B_{x},\varphi_{f,x})
(which is an Hermitian space respecting
\mathbf{Q}|_{M_{b}}
in the sense of §
5.1.1
).
Now (
6.3.5
) and (
6.3.6
) translates to the fact that
G
meets conditions (1) and (2) of Lemma
5.2
. It follows from the lemma that either
U_{f,x}=0
and
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)^{\circ}=\SO(M_{f,x})
, or
V_{f,x}=0
and
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)^{\circ}=\SU(\varphi_{f,x})
.

\hfill\square

Remark 6.7
.

The identity (
5.2.1
) from Lemma
5.2
also translates to the following:

G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}},x)^{\circ}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\SO(T_{f,x}),\,\,&T_{f,x}\text{ is nondegenerate},\\
&\SU(\varphi_{f,x,0}),\,\,&T_{f,x}\text{ is totally isotropic}.\end{aligned}\right.

Corollary 6.8
.

(
6.3.4
) is an equality.

Proof.

Possibly base change to an étale cover of
X_{0}
, we can assume that
G(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}},x)
is connected. Since any object in the Tannakian subcategory
\langle\mathbb{V}_{p,f}^{\prime}\rangle^{\otimes}
has zero slope, it suffices to show the following:

Claim
.

An object in
\langle\mathbb{M}_{p,f}\rangle^{\otimes}
has zero slope only when it is a direct sum of trivial objects.

Indeed, by Goursat’s lemma, there is a common quotient
H
of
G(\mathbb{V}_{p,f}^{\prime},x)
and
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)
, such that

G(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}},x)=G(\mathbb{V}_{p,f}^{\prime},x)\times_{H}G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)
. A faithful representation of
H
gives rise to an object that lies in both
\langle\mathbb{V}_{p,f}^{\prime}\rangle^{\otimes}
and
\langle\mathbb{M}_{p,f}\rangle^{\otimes}
. If the
Claim
is true, then any faithful representation of
H
is trivial, so
H=\{1\}
. Therefore
G(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}},x)=G(\mathbb{V}_{p,f}^{\prime},x)\times G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)
.

We now prove the
Claim
. Let
G=G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)

and
\nu=\mu^{c}|_{\SO(M_{f,x})}
. An object
\mathbb{X}\subseteq\langle\mathbb{M}_{p,f}\rangle^{\otimes}
with zero slope gives rise to a representation
G\rightarrow\GL(\omega_{x}(\mathbb{X}))
. Let
N
be the kernel. Then
N
is a normal subgroup containing
\im\nu
and
U_{G,\nu}
. It suffices to show that
N=G
. By Proposition
6.6
,
G
is either
\SO(M_{f,x})
or
\SU(\varphi_{f,x})
, depending on whether
T_{f,x}
is nondegenerate or totally isotropic. In the nondegenerate case,
N=\SO(M_{f,x})
is immediate. For the totally isotropic case, we note that
\SSU(\varphi_{f,x})
is simple. So at least
\SSU(\varphi_{f,x})\subseteq N
. Since
N
also contains
\im\nu
, we have
N=\SU(\varphi_{f,x})
.
The proof of the
Claim
is complete.
\hfill\square

6.4.
Conjecture
4.7
for GSpin Shimura varieties

Possibly replacing
X_{0}
by a finite étale cover, we can assume that
G(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}},x)
is connected. Let
\eta
be the generic point of
X
. Let
\dim V_{f,x}^{\prime}=r
. By Lemma
6.5
, we have an overconvergent
F
-isocrystal
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{p,f}\subseteq\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}}
. From Remark
2.2
, we know that there is a chain of embeddings

(6.4.1)

\det{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}_{p,f}\subseteq\wedge^{r}\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}nd(\mathbb{H}_{p,X_{0}}).

Since
G(\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{p,f},x)\subseteq\SO(V_{f,x}^{\prime})
, the uniroot
F
-isocrystal
\det\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{p,f}
is constant. Pick a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-generator
{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathbf{v},x}
of
\det V_{f,x}^{\prime}
. By
the equivalence between the category of Dieudonné crystals and the category of
p
-divisible groups over
X_{0}
(
[
10
]
) and
crystalline isogeny theorem over finite generated fields (
[
11
]
), we have

{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathbf{v},x}\in\End(\mathbb{H}_{p,\eta})=\End(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\eta})\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}.

Now suppose
V_{f,x}=0
, i.e.,
T_{f,x}
is totally isotropic and
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{p})=W_{f,x}\oplus W^{\prime}_{f,x}\oplus V^{\prime}_{f,x}
. Let
\pm 1\neq\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}
. We define an isometry
{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathbf{w},x}\in\End(\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}}))
by

(6.4.2)

{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathbf{w},x}(v)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\gamma v,\,\,\,v\in W_{f,x},\\
&\gamma^{-1}v,\,\,\,v\in W_{f,x}^{\prime},\\
&v,\,\,\,v\in V^{\prime}_{f,x}.\end{aligned}\right.

Since
G(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}},x)\subseteq\SU(W_{f,x}\oplus W_{f,x}^{\prime})\times\SO(V_{f,x}^{\prime})
,
{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathbf{w},x}
is also an isometric isomorphism of
G(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}},x)
-representations. By funtoriality of the Kuga–Satake construction, the equivalence between the category of Dieudonné crystals and the category of
p
-divisible groups, and the
crystalline isogeny theorem over finite generated fields, we again have
{\bm{\delta}}_{\mathbf{w},x}\in\End(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\eta})\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

Theorem 6.9
.

Conjecture
4.7
is true for a single GSpin Shimura variety.

Proof.

We construct sufficient sets in the sense of Definition
3
(
2
). The theorem will then follow from Lemma
6.1
.
When
T_{f,x}
is nondegenerate, let
\bm{\Delta}=\{\bm{\delta}_{\mathbf{v},x}\}
. The subgroup of
U_{\GSpin,\mu}
that commutes with
\bm{\Delta}
is the subgroup of
U_{\SO,\mu^{c}}
that preserves
V^{\prime}_{f,x}
. It is then the subgroup of
U_{\GL,\overline{\mu}}
that preserves the subspace
T_{f,x}\oplus\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\formalBr))\subseteq\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\Psi))
. This subgroup is exactly
T_{f,x}
(viewed as a subgroup of
U_{\GL,\overline{\mu}}
), so
\bm{\Delta}
is sufficient. When
T_{f,x}
is totally isotropic, let
\bm{\Delta}=\{\bm{\delta}_{\mathbf{v},x},\bm{\delta}_{\mathbf{w},x}\}
. The subgroup of
U_{\GSpin,\mu}
that commutes with
\bm{\Delta}
is the subgroup of
U_{\SO,\mu^{c}}
that preserves both
V^{\prime}_{f,x}
and
W_{f,x}
. It is then the subgroup of
U_{\GL,\overline{\mu}}
that preserves the subspace
W_{f,x}\subseteq\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\Psi))
. This group is again
T_{f,x}
, so
\bm{\Delta}
is sufficient.

\hfill\square

6.5.
Conjecture
4.9
for GSpin Shimura varieties

For
u\in\mathrm{fpl}(\mathbb{Q})
, we will make the identification
L_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}\simeq\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{u})
as explained in §
4.2
, thus identifying
{\Hg}(f)
as a subgroup of
\GL(L_{\mathbb{Q}})
and
G(\mathbb{L}_{u,X_{0}},x)
as a subgroup of
\GL(L_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}})
. We will be using the fact that
G(\mathbb{L}_{u,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}\subseteq{\Hg}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
(cf. Proposition
4.4
) without mentioning.

6.5.1.
The structure of
{\Hg}(f)

Proposition 6.10
.

Recall that we have a representation
\varrho:{\Hg}(f)\hookrightarrow\SO(L_{\mathbb{Q}})
. We write
(L_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\varrho},Q^{\varrho})
for the subspace of
(L_{\mathbb{Q}},Q)
over which
{\Hg}(f)
acts trivially (so
Q^{\varrho}
is necessarily negative definite). The possible structures of
{\Hg}(f)
can be described in the following two cases:

(a)

Orthogonal case
. There is a quadraple
(F,\tau,\mathbf{M}_{f},{Q}_{f})
where
F

is totally real field,
\tau:F\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is an embedding, and
(\mathbf{M}_{f},{Q}_{f})
is a quadratic space over
F
, such that

(
a_{1}
)

\Hg(f)=\Res_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\SO(Q_{f})
. The corresponding Hodge structure has real multiplication by
F
.

(
a_{2}
)

Q=\Tr_{F/\mathbb{Q}}({Q}_{f})\oplus Q^{\varrho}
.

(
a_{3}
)

Q_{f}
has signature
(2,d)
at place
\tau
and is negative definite at all other real places.

(
a_{4}
)

T_{f,x}
is nondegenerate.

(
a_{5}
)

Let
\mathfrak{p}
be the place of
F
given by
F\xrightarrow{\tau}\mathbb{C}\simeq\mathbb{C}_{p}
. Then
F_{\mathfrak{p}}=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
(\mathbf{M}_{f},{Q}_{f})_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}\simeq(M_{f,x},\mathbf{Q}|_{M_{f,x}})
.

(b)

Unitary case
. There is a quintuple
(E,F,\tau,\mathbf{M}_{f},{{\phi}}_{f})
where
F
is a totally real field,
\tau:F\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is an embedding,
E/F
is a quadratic imaginary extension, and
(\mathbf{M}_{f},{\bm{\phi}}_{f})
is an Hermitian space over
E
, such that

(
b_{1}
)

\Hg(f)=\Res_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\SU({{\phi}}_{f})
. The corresponding Hodge structure has complex multiplication by
E
.

(
b_{2}
)

Q=\Tr_{E/\mathbb{Q}}({\phi}_{f})\oplus Q^{\varrho}
.

(
b_{3}
)

\phi_{f}
has signature
(1,d)
at place
\tau
and is negative definite at all other real places.

(
b_{4}
)

T_{f,x}
is totally isotropic.

(
b_{5}
)

Let
\mathfrak{p}
be the place of
F
given by
F\xrightarrow{\tau}\mathbb{C}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}_{p}
. Then
F_{\mathfrak{p}}=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
,
\mathfrak{p}
splits completely in
E
, and
(\mathbf{M}_{f},\phi_{f})_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}\simeq(M_{f,x},\varphi_{f,x})
.

Proof.

Zarhin’s result
[
48
]
implies that
\Hg(f)
must be the scalar restriction of an orthogonal group or a unitary group over some totally real field with certain signature conditions.

If
\Hg(f)
is the scalar restriction of an orthogonal group, then there is a quadraple
(F,\tau,\mathbf{M}_{f},{Q}_{f})
where
F

is totally real field,
\tau:F\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is an embedding, and
(\mathbf{M}_{f},{Q}_{f})
is a quadratic space over
F
satisfying
(a)(

a
1

)
and
(a)(

a
2

)
, and that
Q_{f}
has signature
(2,l)
for some integer
l
at place
\tau
and is negative definite at all other real places. We claim that
(a)(

a
3

)
\sim
(a)(

a
5

)
hold. First, we have
\dim\mathcal{X}_{f}=l
. On the other hand, Proposition
4.3
(
1
) and Theorem
6.9
for GSpin Shimura varieties implies that
\dim\mathcal{X}_{f}=\rk\mathscr{T}_{f,x}=d
. It follows that
l=d
, so
(a)(

a
3

)
holds. Let
\mathcal{P}
be a place of
F
lying over
p
and

\mathfrak{p}
be the place of
F
given by
F\xrightarrow{\tau}\mathbb{C}\simeq\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. Let
\mu^{c}:\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow L_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
be the canonical Hodge cocharacter at point
x
. Note that
\im\mu^{c}\subseteq G(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}\subseteq\Hg(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. In other words,
\mu^{c}
factors through

(6.5.1)

\Hg(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}=\prod_{\mathcal{P}|p}\Res_{F_{\mathcal{P}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\SO(Q_{f})_{F_{\mathcal{P}}}.

Indeed, it further factors through
\Res_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\SO(Q_{f})_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}
by the comparison between
\mu^{c}
and
\mu^{c}_{\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}}
, where
\mu_{\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}}
is the Hodge cocharacter of
\tilde{x}_{\mathbb{C}}
splitting the filtration. By base change to
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, we have

(6.5.2)

\left(\Res_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\SO(Q_{f})_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}\right)\times\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}=\prod_{\sigma:F_{\mathfrak{p}}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}\SO(Q_{f})_{\sigma,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}.

Then
\SO(Q_{f})_{\tau,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
is the unique factor that
\mu^{c}
factors through. On the other hand,
g\in\Gal(F_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
acts transitively on the right of (
6.5.2
), and
g\cdot\mu^{c}
factors through
\SO(Q_{f})_{g\cdot\tau,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
. Since
\mu^{c}
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, we have
g\cdot\mu^{c}=\mu^{c}
. Consequently, there is only one embedding of
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
into
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. Hence

F_{\mathfrak{p}}=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. It then follows from Theorem
3.3
, Proposition
6.3
and (
6.5.1
) that

T_{f,x}=U(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}}^{-},x)\subseteq U_{\Hg(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},\mu^{c}}=U_{\SO(Q_{f})_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}},\mu^{c}}.

Since
\dim U_{\SO(Q_{f})_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}},\mu^{c}}=d
, we have
T_{f,x}=U_{\SO(Q_{f})_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}},\mu^{c}}
. Thus
T_{f,x}
is nondegenerate,
(\mathbf{M}_{f},Q_{f})_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}\simeq(M_{f,x},\mathbf{Q}|_{M_{f,x}})
, and
(a)(

a
4

)
,
(a)(

a
5

)
hold.

If
\Hg(f)
is the scalar restriction of a unitary group, then there is a quintuple
(E,F,\tau,\mathbf{M}_{f},{{\phi}}_{f})
where
F
is a totally real field,
\tau:F\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is an embedding,
E/F
is a quadratic imaginary extension, and
(\mathbf{M}_{f},{\bm{\phi}}_{f})
is an Hermitian space over
E
satisfying
(b)(

b
1

)
and
(b)(

b
2

)
, and that
\phi_{f}
