has signature
(1,r)
for some integer
r
at place
\tau
and is negative definite at all other real places. We claim that
(b)(

b
3

)
\sim
(b)(

b
5

)
hold. First we have
\dim\mathcal{X}_{f}=r
. On the other hand, Proposition
4.3
(
1
) and Theorem
6.9
for GSpin Shimura varieties implies that
\dim\mathcal{X}_{f}=\rk\mathscr{T}_{f,x}=d
. It follows that
r=d
, so
(b)(

b
3

)
holds. Similar to the orthogonal case,
\mu^{c}
factors through
\Res_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\SU(\phi_{f})_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}
. A similar argument shows that
F_{\mathfrak{p}}=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
\mathfrak{p}
splits completely in
E
. Furthermore, we have

T_{f,x}=U(\mathbb{L}_{p,X_{0}}^{-},x)\subseteq U_{\Hg(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},\mu^{c}}=U_{\SU(\phi_{f})_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}},\mu^{c}}.

Since
\dim U_{\SU(\phi_{f})_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}},\mu^{c}}=d
, we have
T_{f,x}=U_{\SU(\phi_{f})_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}},\mu^{c}}
. It then follows that
T_{f,x}
is totally isotropic,
(\mathbf{M}_{f},\phi_{f})_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}\simeq(M_{f,x},\varphi_{f,x})
, and
(b)(

b
4

)
,
(b)(

b
5

)
hold.
\hfill\square

Remark 6.11

(Passing to Heegner cycles)
.

The notion of Heegner cycles can serve as a technical simplification. Let
L^{\varrho}_{\mathbb{Q}}
be the subspace of
L_{\mathbb{Q}}
fixed by
\Hg(f)
. Then the embedding
\GSpin({L}^{\varrho,\perp}_{\mathbb{Q}})\hookrightarrow\GSpin({L}_{\mathbb{Q}})
defines a Shimura subvariety
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{S}
. We will call it the
Heegner cycle
cut out by
{L}^{\varrho}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Up to component,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is cut out from
\mathcal{S}
by certain
special endomorphisms
of
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}
(
[
32
, §6]
). The Shimura variety
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is equipped with a Kuga–Satake embedding
\GSpin({L}^{\varrho,\perp}_{\mathbb{Q}})\hookrightarrow\GSp(\Cl({L}^{\varrho,\perp}_{\mathbb{Q}}))
compatible with
\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\GSp(H_{\mathbb{Q}})
, in the sense that the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\GSpin({L}^{\varrho,\perp}_{\mathbb{Q}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\GSp(\Cl({L}^{\varrho,\perp}_{\mathbb{Q}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\GSpin(L_{\mathbb{Q}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\GSp(H_{\mathbb{Q}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{g}}

commute: Here
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{h}
is the Zariski closure in
\mathcal{A}_{g}
of the Siegel modular variety
A^{\prime}_{h}
defined by
\GSp(\Cl({L}^{\varrho,\perp}_{\mathbb{Q}}))
, and
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
is the Zariski closure of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{S}
(or equivalently, in
\mathcal{A}_{g}
). The universal Abelian scheme over
{A}^{\prime}_{h}
is a subscheme of the universal Abelian scheme over
A_{g}
. In fact, the later Abelian scheme is canonically isogenous to
2^{\dim L^{\varrho}_{\mathbb{Q}}}
copies of the former one (
[
32
, Lemma 6.4]
). Up to a finite cover, the relation between universal Abelian schemes as described above extends to the naïve integral model
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{h}
(Lemma
4.2
), and via pulling back we obtain (up to finite cover) an Abelian scheme
{\mathscr{A}^{\prime}}^{\KS}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
, as well as the corresponding local systems
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{\bullet}
. From the above discussion, we see that
{\mathscr{A}}^{\KS}
is isogenous to
2^{\dim L^{\varrho}_{\mathbb{Q}}}
copies of
{\mathscr{A}^{\prime}}^{\KS}
,
\mathbb{H}_{\bullet}
is a direct sum of
2^{\dim L^{\varrho}_{\mathbb{Q}}}
copies of
\mathbb{H}_{\bullet}^{\prime}
, and
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}=\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}^{\varrho}
, where
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}^{\varrho}
is the trivial local system corresponding to
L_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\varrho}
. Therefore,
\mathbb{H}_{\bullet}^{\prime}

resp
.
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}^{\prime}
are sub local systems with the same monodromy as
\mathbb{H}_{\bullet}

resp
.
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
.

Note that
\mathcal{X}_{f}
(or at least
\mathcal{X}_{f}^{+}
) is contained in the Heegner cycle
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
cut out by
L^{\varrho}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. The Zariski closure of
\mathcal{X}_{f}
inside
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
is nothing other than
\mathscr{X}_{f}
. By the explicit classification Theorem
6.10
, the Heegner cycle in the orthogonal
resp
. unitary case is defined by
\GSpin(\Tr_{F/\mathbb{Q}}{Q}_{f})

resp
.
\GSpin(\Tr_{E/\mathbb{Q}}\phi_{f})
.

6.5.2.
Splitting the local systems

We now study the splitting of local systems
\{\mathbb{L}_{u,X_{0}}\}_{u\in\mathrm{fpl}(\mathbb{Q})}
. By passing to the Heegner cycle
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
cut out by
L^{\varrho}_{\mathbb{Q}}
and using the sub local system
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}^{\prime}
instead of
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
(see Remark
6.11
), we can assume that
L^{\varrho}_{\mathbb{Q}}=0
.
Note that
\{\mathbb{L}_{u,X_{0}}\}_{u\in\mathrm{fpl}(\mathbb{Q})}
is a weakly compatible system as per §
3.4
. By the second assertion of Lemma
3.4
, one can replace
X_{0}
by a finite étale cover, so that
G(\mathbb{L}_{u,X_{0}},x)
is connected for all
u
.

Let
F,\tau
be the totally real field and its complex embedding from Proposition
6.10
. We fix a sufficiently large Galois extension
K/\mathbb{Q}
, as well as an embedding
K\subseteq\mathbb{C}
, such that all embeddings
F\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
factor through
K
. Let
\Sigma_{F}
be the set of embeddings of
F
into
K
. There is an
F
-group
\mathcal{G}
, which is either
\SO(\mathbf{M}_{f})
or
\SU(\phi_{f})
,
such that

\Hg(f)=\Res_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\mathcal{G}
. The group
\mathcal{G}
is equipped with a standard representation
r_{f}:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\GL(\mathbf{M}_{f})
. For a
\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}
, we let
\mathcal{G}_{\sigma,K}
,
\mathbf{M}_{f,\sigma,K}
,
r_{f,\sigma,K}
be the base changes of
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathbf{M}_{f}
,
r_{f}
to
K
via the embedding
\sigma
. We have the following decompositions

(6.5.3)

\displaystyle\Hg(f)_{K}=\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}}\mathcal{G}_{\sigma,K},

(6.5.4)

\displaystyle L_{K}=\bigoplus_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}}\mathbf{M}_{f,\sigma,K},

(6.5.5)

\displaystyle\varrho_{f,K}=\bigoplus_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}}{r}_{f,\sigma,K}|_{\Hg(f)_{K}}.

In what follows, we denote by
v\in\mathrm{fpl}(K)
a place lying over a place
u\in\mathrm{fpl}(\mathbb{Q})
. The base change local system
\mathbb{L}_{u,X_{0}}\otimes K_{v}
will be denoted by
\mathbb{L}_{v,X_{0}}
. Recall that
G(\mathbb{L}_{u,X_{0}},x)\subseteq\Hg(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
, so we can compose this with
\varrho
and use Tannakian formalism to construct sub-local systems of
\mathbb{L}_{u,X_{0}}
.

Definition 4
.

Let
\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,v,f}\subseteq\mathbb{L}_{v,X_{0}}
be the sub-local system that arises from
r_{f,\sigma,K_{v}}
. This yields a collection of coefficient objects
\{\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,v,f}\}_{(\sigma,v)\in\Sigma_{F}\times\mathrm{fpl}(K)}
. We also define
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{\sigma,v,f}=\bigoplus_{\sigma\neq\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{F}}\mathbb{M}_{\sigma^{\prime},v,f}
.

Note that
\mathbb{L}_{v,X_{0}}=\bigoplus_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}}\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,v,f}
. It is immediate that for every pair
(\sigma,v)
:

(6.5.6)

\displaystyle G(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,v,f},x)\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{\sigma,{K}_{v}},

(6.5.7)

\displaystyle G(\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{\sigma,v,f},x)\subseteq\prod_{\sigma\neq\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{F}}G(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma^{\prime},v,f},x),

(6.5.8)

\displaystyle G(\mathbb{L}_{v,X_{0}},x)\subseteq G(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,v,f},x)\times G(\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{\sigma,v,f},x).

The coefficient objects that we have just constructed are compatible in the following sense:

Lemma 6.12
.

For each
\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}
, the collections
\{\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,v,f}\}_{v\in\mathrm{fpl}(K)}
and
\{\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{\sigma,v,f}\}_{v\in\mathrm{fpl}(K)}
are weakly
K
-compatible systems of coefficient objects.

Proof.

We only prove the assertion for
\{\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,v,f}\}_{v\in\mathrm{fpl}(K)}
, the assertion for
\{\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{\sigma,v,f}\}_{v\in\mathrm{fpl}(K)}
is similar. Let
x_{0}\in|X_{0}|
be a closed point. Since
x_{0}
is ordinary, we can canonical lift the geometric Frobenius of
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{x_{0}}
to characteristic 0. This gives rise to a conjugacy class of elements in
\GL(L_{\mathbb{Q}})
. We denote by
P(L_{\mathbb{Q},\tilde{x}_{0}},t)
the corresponding characteristic polynomial. Then the discussion in §
3.4
implies that

P(L_{\mathbb{Q},\tilde{x}_{0}},t)=P(\mathbb{L}_{v,x_{0}},t)
for all
v\nmid p
. Furthermore, the equality holds for
v|p
if we replace the Frobenius of
\mathbb{L}_{v,X_{0}}
by a suitable power. Now for
v|p
, we can also replace the Frobenius of
\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,v,f}
by a suitable power, so that from (
6.5.4
) and (
6.5.5
), we have
P(L_{\mathbb{Q},\tilde{x}_{0}},t)=\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}}P(\mathbf{M}_{f,\sigma,K,\tilde{x}_{0}},t)
and
P(\mathbb{L}_{v,x_{0}},t)=\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}}P(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,v,f},t)
for all
v\in\mathrm{fpl}(K)
. It then follows the definition that
P(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,v,f},t)=P(\mathbf{M}_{f,\sigma,K,\tilde{x}_{0}},t)
. Therefore
P(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,v,f},t)
is independent of
v
.
\hfill\square

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
\mathfrak{p}
be the place of
F
corresponding to the embedding
\tau
(cf. Proposition
6.10
). Take a place
\mathfrak{P}|p
of
K
lying over
\tau\mathfrak{p}
. Then (
6.5.6
) and (
6.5.8
) are equalities for
(\sigma,v)=(\tau,\mathfrak{P})
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbb{M}_{\tau,\mathfrak{P},f}
and
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{\tau,\mathfrak{P},f}
are nothing other than
\mathbb{M}_{p,f}\otimes K_{\mathfrak{P}}
and
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{p,f}\otimes K_{\mathfrak{P}}
. So the lemma follows from Proposition
6.6
, Corollary
6.8
and Proposition
6.10
.
\hfill\square

Remark 6.14
.

When
\mathcal{G}
is unitary, possibly replacing
K
by a quadratic extension, we can assume that the Hermitian
form splits over
K
. Then each
\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,v,f}
further splits as a direct sum of mutually dual coefficient objects
\mathbb{W}_{\sigma,v,f}
and
\mathbb{W}_{\sigma,v,f}^{\prime}
. For fixed
\sigma
,
\{\mathbb{W}_{\sigma,v,f}\}_{v\in\mathrm{fpl}(K)}
and
\{\mathbb{W}_{\sigma,v,f}^{\prime}\}_{v\in\mathrm{fpl}(K)}
form weakly
K
-compatible systems. When
(\sigma,v)=(\tau,\mathfrak{P})
, these coeffient objects are just
\mathbb{W}_{p,f}\otimes K_{\mathfrak{P}}
and
\mathbb{W}_{p,f}^{\prime}\otimes K_{\mathfrak{P}}
.

6.5.3.
Galois twist on coefficients

Note that
\Gal({K}/\mathbb{Q})
acts on
\Sigma_{F}\simeq\Gal(K/\mathbb{Q})/\Gal(K/\tau F)
. Let
g\in\Gal({K}/\mathbb{Q})
, the
g
-twist
of a
K_{v}
-scheme
X
is the
K_{gv}
-scheme
X\times_{g}\Spec K_{gv}
, denoted by
gX
. There is also an obvious notion of
g
-twist of morphisms between
K_{v}
-schemes. There is also a notion of
g
-twist of
K_{v}
-coefficient objects
defined as
g\mathcal{E}:=\mathcal{E}\otimes_{g}K_{gv}
.
It follows from the definitions that

(6.5.9)

\displaystyle g\mathcal{G}_{\sigma,{K}_{v}}=\mathcal{G}_{g\sigma,{K}_{gv}},

\displaystyle g\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,v,f}=\mathbb{M}_{g\sigma,gv,f},

\displaystyle gG(\mathcal{E},x)=G(g\mathcal{E},x),\,\,\mathcal{E}=\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,v,f},\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{\sigma,v,f},\mathbb{L}_{v,X_{0}}.

The following lemma will be used in the next chapter.

Lemma 6.15
.

Fix a place
\mathfrak{Q}|p
of
K
, then
\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,\mathfrak{Q},f}
has nonzero slopes if and only if
\sigma\mathfrak{p}\subseteq\mathfrak{Q}
. Furthermore, there is a unique
\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}
with this property.

Proof.

As we noted in the proof of Lemma
6.13
, if
\mathfrak{P}
is a place lying above
\tau\mathfrak{p}
, then
\mathbb{M}_{\tau,\mathfrak{P},f}=\mathbb{M}_{p,f}\otimes K_{\mathfrak{P}}
. Since
\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{P},X_{0}}=\bigoplus_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}}\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,\mathfrak{P},f}
,
\mathbb{M}_{\tau,\mathfrak{P},f}
is the unique one among all
\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,\mathfrak{P},f}
that has nonzero slopes. Therefore, for any
g\in\Gal(K/\mathbb{Q})
, the
g
-twist
g\mathbb{M}_{\tau,\mathfrak{P},f}
is the unique one among all
g\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,\mathfrak{P},f}
that has nonzero slopes.
The lemma follows easily from this point.

\hfill\square

Theorem 6.16
.

Conjecture
4.9
is true for a single GSpin Shimura variety.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\Hg(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}=G(\mathbb{L}_{u,X_{0}},x)
for every
u\in\mathrm{fpl}(\mathbb{Q})
. Let
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{P}
be as in Lemma
6.13
. We have

G(\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{P},X_{0}},x)=G(\mathbb{M}_{\tau,\mathfrak{P},f},x)\times G(\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{\tau,\mathfrak{P},f},x),\,\,G(\mathbb{M}_{\tau,\mathfrak{P},f},x)=\mathcal{G}_{\tau,K_{\mathfrak{P}}}.

Lemma
6.12
and Lemma
3.4
then imply that there is a finite extension
\mathfrak{K}/K
with the property that for each
v\in\mathrm{fpl}(K)
and
\mathfrak{v}\in\mathrm{fpl}(\mathfrak{K})
with
\mathfrak{v}|v
, the base changes of (
6.5.6
) and (
6.5.8
) to
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathfrak{v}}
are equalities. But this implies that (
6.5.6
) and (
6.5.8
) are already equalities over
K_{v}
, i.e.,

(6.5.10)

G(\mathbb{L}_{v,X_{0}},x)=G(\mathbb{M}_{\tau,v,f},x)\times G(\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{\tau,v,f},x),\,\,G(\mathbb{M}_{\tau,v,f},x)=\mathcal{G}_{\tau,{K}_{v}}.

We now use (
6.5.9
) to show that (
6.5.10
) holds for any other
\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}
. In fact, Chebotarev’s density theorem guarantees the existence of a place
v_{0}
and an element
g\in\Gal(K/\mathbb{Q})
fixing
v_{0}
such that
g\tau=\sigma
. Let
v=v_{0}
in (
6.5.10
) and consider its
g
-twist, we have

(6.5.11)

G(\mathbb{L}_{v_{0},X_{0}},x)=G(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,v_{0},f},x)\times G(\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{\sigma,v_{0},f},x),\,\,G(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma,v_{0},f},x)=\mathcal{G}_{\sigma,{K}_{v_{0}}}.

Lemma
3.4
then implies that for any finite place
v
of
K
, (
6.5.11
) still holds. Since
\sigma
is arbitrary, we find that, for any
v
,

(6.5.12)

G(\mathbb{L}_{v,X_{0}},x)=\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}}\mathcal{G}_{\sigma,{K}_{v}}.

Together with (
6.5.3
), we obtain

(6.5.13)

G(\mathbb{L}_{u,X_{0}},x)=\Hg(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}=(\Res_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\mathcal{G})_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}.

\hfill\square

7.
Tate-linear and Mumford–Tate conjectures: the product case

In this section we prove Conjecture
4.7
and Conjecture
4.9
for products of GSpin Shimura varieties. The main input will be the single cases of the conjectures which are established in §
6
.

7.1.
Notation and conventions

We use the same notation as in §
4.1
, so
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}}=\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\mathscr{S}_{i}
is a finite product of integral GSpin Shimura varieties attached to lattices
(L_{i},Q_{i})
, and we have a pointed variety
(X,x)
with a map
f
to
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. We denote by
f_{i}
the composition of
f
with the projection
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{i,\mathbb{F}}
. For an index subset
\mathbf{J}\subset\mathbf{I}
, let
f_{\mathbf{J}}
be the composition of
f
with the projection
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{J},\mathbb{F}}
. In particular,
f=f_{\mathbf{I}}
.

7.1.1.
An index-wise notation

For each
i
we have a Shimura subvariety
\mathcal{X}_{f_{i}}
and its naïve integral model
\mathscr{X}_{f_{i}}
. We have
\mathcal{X}_{f}\subseteq\mathscr{X}_{f}\subseteq\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\mathscr{X}_{f_{i}}
, and the morphism
f
factors through
\mathscr{X}_{f,\mathbb{F}}
. The projection of
\mathscr{T}_{f}
to each
\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{i,\mathbb{F}}
is
\mathscr{T}_{f_{i}}
(more generally, the projection of
\mathscr{T}_{f}
to
\prod_{i\in\mathbf{J}}\mathscr{S}^{/x}_{i,\mathbb{F}}
is
\mathscr{T}_{f_{\mathbf{J}}}
) and Theorem
6.9
implies that
\mathscr{T}_{f_{i}}=\mathscr{X}^{/x}_{f_{i},\mathbb{F},\red}
.

We can also apply Proposition
6.10
to each
i
. So there is a totally real field
F_{i}
with a place
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
and an embedding
\tau_{i}
, an orthogonal or unitary group
\mathcal{G}_{i}
over
F_{i}
satisfying certain signature conditions, such that
\Hg(f_{i})=\Res_{F_{i}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathcal{G}_{i}
. We know from Theorem
6.16
that the monodromy group
G(\mathbb{L}_{i,u,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}

resp
.
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}},x)^{\circ}
equals
(\Res_{F_{i}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathcal{G}_{i})_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}

resp
.
\mathcal{G}_{i,\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
.

7.1.2.
An intrinsic perspective

For
i\in\mathbf{I}
, we will also view
\mathscr{X}_{f_{i}}
as a subscheme of the Heegner cycle
\mathscr{S}_{i}^{\prime}
cut out by
L^{\varrho_{i}}_{i,\mathbb{Q}}
(see Remark
6.11
).
By abuse of notation, we will again use
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{i},\mathbb{H}_{i,\bullet},\mathbb{L}_{i,\bullet}
for the Abelian scheme and various local systems that arise from the Kuga–Satake embedding of
\mathscr{S}_{i}^{\prime}
. In order to establish the conjectures, it suffices to work with these Abelian schemes and local systems. This is what we will do in the rest of the paper.

From §
4.3.1
, the ordinary naïve integral model
\mathscr{X}_{f_{i}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
and
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{i},\mathbb{H}_{i,\bullet},\mathbb{L}_{i,\bullet}
are more or less intrinsic to
\mathcal{X}_{f_{i}}
. This suggests that we can forget the ambient space
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}}
and work intrinsically with
\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\mathscr{X}_{f_{i}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. Though we won’t make this precise, and will continue to work in the extrinsic framework, we encourage the readers to think intrinsically, as this brings much convenience and flexibility.

7.1.3.
Separated collections

Several notions are needed for studying products of groups.
A finite collection of surjections
\{G\twoheadrightarrow G_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbf{I}}
between (algebraic) groups is called
separated
, if the induced morphism
G\rightarrow\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}G_{i}
is injective. For example, the collection of projections of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-vector spaces
\{T_{f,x}\twoheadrightarrow T_{f_{i},x}\}
is separated. Moreover, Tannakian formalism implies that the collection
\{G(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},x)\twoheadrightarrow G(\mathbb{L}_{i,u,X_{0}},x)\}_{i\in\mathbf{I}}
is separated. More generally, the collection of projections from the monodromy group of a direct sum of local systems to the monodromy group of each direct summand is separated. It is easy to make sense of morphsms and products of separated collections: they are morphisms and products on each index that make obvious diagrams commute.

7.1.4.
Iso-graphs

The structure of a separated collection can be understood via Goursat’s lemma. A frequently occurring configuration is as follows: say,
\{G\twoheadrightarrow G_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbf{I}}
is a separated collection such that each
G\twoheadrightarrow G_{i}
is an isomorphism. In this case, we call
G
an
iso-graph
over
\{G_{i}\}_{{i\in\mathbf{I}}}
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
\{G\twoheadrightarrow G_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbf{I}}
be a separated system of connected algebraic groups over a field, and suppose that each
G_{i}
is simple. Then there is a partition
\mathbf{I}=\prod_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathbf{I}^{h}
and an iso-graph
G^{h}
over
\{G_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbf{I}_{h}}
for each
h
, such that
G=\prod_{h\in\mathcal{H}}G^{h}
.

Proof.

We recall that an algebraic group is simple if it is nonabelian and contains no nontrivial connected normal subgroup. The claim follows simply from Goursat’s lemma and induction on the cardinality of
\mathbf{I}
. The nonabelianess is needed for showing that a connected normal subgroup of
\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}G_{i}

must be a product of
G_{i}
’s over an index subset
\mathbf{J}\subseteq\mathbf{I}
.

\hfill\square

Remark 7.2
.

Being a product of iso-graphs is a very strong condition: if we understand the structure of
G
when projecting down to every pair of indices, we then understand the whole group.

7.2.
Exceptional morphisms and splittings

It is known that unitary groups are simple, while in most situations, orthogonal groups are simple and there is no isogeny between orthogonal and unitary groups. However, in low dimensions there do exist exceptional splittings and isogenies. The exceptional behaviors of the corresponding Shimura varieties add subtle difficulties to our work, e.g., in the situation that
\mathcal{X}_{f_{i}}
splits exceptionally. In this section, we study the exceptional behaviors, and reduce the conjectures to the non-exceptional case.

7.2.1.
Group theory in exceptional cases

In this section, we denote by
F/B
a finite extension where
B
is either
\mathbb{Q}
or
\mathbb{Q}_{u}
. Let
E/F
be a quadratic étale algebra. We also let
K/B
be a Galois extension that contains
F
, write
\Sigma_{F}
the set of embeddings of
F
into
K
. Let
\phi
be an Hermitian form over
E
of rank 2 and let
R=\Tr_{E/F}\phi
, a rank 4 split quadratic form. The following sequence summarizes the exceptional behaviors of orthogonal and unitary groups:

(7.2.1)

\SUU(\phi)\times\SUU(\phi)\xrightarrow{/\{\pm(I,I)\}}\SO(R)\xrightarrow{\pm I}\SO(q)\times\SO(q).

Here
q
is a certain quadratic form over
F
with rank 3, and
\SUU(\phi)/\{\pm I\}=\SO(q)
. In order to distinguish, lets use
\phi_{0}
and
\SO(q_{0})
to denote the left hand side of the products in (
7.2.1
), while using
\phi_{1}
and
\SO(q_{1})
to denote the right hand side.

We briefly review the spin representations in the exceptional cases. First we have
\Spin(R)=\Spin(q_{0})\times\Spin(q_{1})
.
It is well known that the standard representation
\Spin(R)\curvearrowright\Cl(R)
splits into a direct sum of
\Spin(q_{\alpha})\curvearrowright\Cl(q_{\alpha})
,
\alpha=\{0,1\}
. Let
\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{n}
be the elements of
\Sigma_{F}
, then
\Spin(\sigma_{i}R)\curvearrowright\Cl(\sigma_{i}R)
splits into
\Spin(\sigma_{i}q_{\alpha})\curvearrowright\Cl(\sigma_{i}q_{\alpha})
. Taking tensor product and descending down to
F
, we see that
\Res_{F/B}\Spin(R)\curvearrowright\Cl(\Tr_{F/B}R)
admits splittings:

(7.2.2)

\Cl(\Tr_{F/B}R)\otimes_{B}K=\bigoplus_{(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\Cl(\sigma_{1}q_{\alpha_{1}})\boxtimes\Cl(\sigma_{2}q_{\alpha_{2}})...\boxtimes\Cl(\sigma_{n}q_{\alpha_{n}}).

(7.2.3)

\Cl(\Tr_{F/B}R)=\Cl(\Tr_{F/B}q_{0})\oplus\Cl(\Tr_{F/B}q_{1})\oplus V_{\text{else}},

where for
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
,
\Cl(\Tr_{F/B}q_{\alpha})_{K}=\Cl(\sigma_{1}q_{\alpha})\boxtimes\Cl(\sigma_{2}q_{\alpha})...\boxtimes\Cl(\sigma_{n}q_{\alpha})
.

7.2.2.
Shimura varieties in exceptional cases

Back to §
7.1.1
, an index
i
is called
exceptionally unitary
, if
\mathcal{G}_{i}=\SU(\phi_{f_{i}})
for a rank 2 Hermitian form
\phi_{f_{i}}
, so
\mathcal{X}_{f_{i}}
is also an orthogonal Shimura curve; it is called
exceptionally split
if
\mathcal{G}_{i}=\SO(Q_{f_{i}})
for a split rank 4 quadratic form
Q_{f_{i}}
, so
\mathcal{X}_{f_{i}}
splits into two unitary Shimura curves. Both types of indices are called
exceptional
.
Let
\mathbf{I}_{\text{exu}}\sqcup\mathbf{I}_{\text{exs}}=\mathbf{I}_{\text{ex}}\subseteq\mathbf{I}
be the subset of exceptionally unitary, exceptionally split, and exceptional indices.

If
i\in\mathbf{I}_{\text{exu}}
, we define
Q_{f_{i}}=\Tr_{E_{i}/F_{i}}\phi_{f_{i}}
; if
i\in\mathbf{I}_{\text{exs}}
, we let
\phi_{f_{i}}
be a rank 4 quadratic form such that
\Tr_{E_{i}/F_{i}}\phi_{f_{i}}=Q_{f_{i}}
. Therefore for any
i\in\mathbf{I}_{\text{ex}}
, we have
\phi_{f_{i}}
and
Q_{f_{i}}
, and there is a rank 3 quadratic form
q_{f_{i}}
over
F_{i}
such that the groups
\SUU(\phi_{f_{i}})
,
\SO(Q_{f_{i}})
and
\SO(q_{f_{i}})
sit in an sequence like (
7.2.1
). Note that
\Tr_{E_{i}/\mathbb{Q}}\phi_{f_{i}}=\Tr_{F_{i}/\mathbb{Q}}Q_{f_{i}}
is a direct summand of
L_{i,\mathbb{Q}}
, but this is not true for
\Tr_{F_{i}/\mathbb{Q}}q_{f_{i}}
. However, there is another integral lattice
(L_{i}^{\prime\prime},Q_{i}^{\prime\prime})
of rank
(2,b_{i}^{\prime\prime})
which is self-dual at
p
, so that
L_{i,\mathbb{Q}}^{\prime\prime}
splits as
\Tr_{F_{i}/\mathbb{Q}}q_{f_{i}}^{\prime\prime}
direct sum a negative definite form.

To ease notation, let us use
F,\phi,R,q,(L,Q),(L^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime})
to denote
F_{i},\phi_{f_{i}},Q_{f_{i}},q_{f_{i}},(L_{i},Q_{i}),(L_{i}^{\prime\prime},Q_{i}^{\prime\prime})
. Just like what we did in §
7.2.1
, we use subscripts
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
to label the two copies of
\phi
’s and
q
’s. Let
\mathcal{S}

resp
.
\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}
be GSpin Shimura varieties that arise from
(L,Q)

resp
.
(L^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime})
, with certain hypersepcial level structures and integral canonical models
\mathscr{S}

resp
.
\mathscr{S}^{\prime\prime}
.

Let
\mathrm{GR}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\SO(q_{\alpha})
be the preimage of
\Res_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\SO(q_{\alpha})
in
\GSpin(\Tr_{F/\mathbb{Q}}q_{\alpha})
, whereas
\mathrm{GR}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\UU(\phi_{\alpha})
and
\mathrm{GR}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\SO(R)
be the preimages of respective groups in
\GSpin(\Tr_{F/\mathbb{Q}}R)
. We have a chain of morphisms

(7.2.4)

\mathrm{GR}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\UU(\phi_{\alpha})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{GR}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\SO(R)\rightarrow\mathrm{GR}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\SO(q_{\alpha})

compatible with (
7.2.1
) and the splitting of representations (
7.2.3
). For suitable level structures, we have Shimura varieties
\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
that arise from
\mathrm{GR}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\UU(\phi_{\alpha})\subseteq\mathrm{GR}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\SO(R)\subseteq\GSpin(L,Q)
and Shimura varieties
\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}
that arise from
\mathrm{GR}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\SO(q_{\alpha})\subseteq\GSpin(L^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime})
, such that (up to components) there are finite surjections
\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{0}\times\mathcal{Y}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}
.

Let
\mathscr{Y}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathscr{W}\subseteq\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Z}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathscr{S}^{\prime\prime}
be the naïve integral models. There are also ordinary naïve integral models
\mathscr{Y}_{\alpha}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\subseteq\mathscr{W}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. Lemma
4.6
(
1
) implies that
\mathscr{Z}_{\alpha,\mathbb{F}}
has non-empty ordinary locus. By Lemma
4.6
, there are finite covering maps
\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{\alpha}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{\alpha}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{0}\times\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{1}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{0}\times\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{1}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{W}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
.

Let
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathscr{Y}_{\alpha}}
(
resp
.
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathscr{W}}
,
resp
.
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\alpha}}
) be the pullback Abelian scheme on
\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{\alpha}
(
resp
.
\widetilde{\mathscr{W}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}

resp
.
\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{\alpha}
) from the integral Heegner cycles (§
7.1.2
). By definition, the pullback of
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathscr{W}}
to
\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{\alpha}
is
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathscr{Y}_{\alpha}}
. On the other hand, the generic Mumford–Tate groups of
\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha}
,
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}
are nothing other than
\mathrm{GR}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\UU(\phi_{\alpha}),\mathrm{GR}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\SO(R)
and
\mathrm{GR}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\SO(q_{\alpha})
. Using (
7.2.4
) and the splitting of representations (
7.2.3
), together with Lemma
4.2
, we obtain (up to a finite cover) a splitting (up to isogeny):

(7.2.5)

\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathscr{W}}\simeq\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{W},0}\times\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{W},1}\times\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{W},\mathrm{else}}.

In fact, (
7.2.2
) even implies that, up to a finite cover, the motive of
\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathscr{W}}
, upon base change to a Galois closure of
F
, is already generated by
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{W},0}
and
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{W},1}
. In addition, up to a finite cover and isogeny,
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{W},\alpha}|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{\alpha}}=\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\alpha}}
, and
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{W},1-\alpha}|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{\alpha}}
is constant and CM, for
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
.

We can take various cohomology of the above Abelian schemes to obtain parallel splitting results for various local systems.

7.2.3.
Reduction to the non-exceptional case

We say that
f
is
non-exceptional
, if
\mathbf{I}_{\text{ex}}=\emptyset
. In this section we reduce Conjecture
4.7
and
4.9
to the non-exceptional case, at the expense of enlarging
\mathbf{I}
. In fact, starting with an
f
, we can associate to it a non-exceptional
{f}^{\prime}
via the following process:

Construction 3
.

Suppose that
i\in\mathbf{I}_{\text{exs}}
. From the process in §
7.2.2
, for each
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, there is an orthogonal Shimura curve
\mathcal{Z}_{i,\alpha}
sitting inside a GSpin Shimura variety
\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}_{i,\alpha}
, together with an ordinary naïve integral model
\mathscr{Z}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{i}\subseteq\mathscr{S}^{\prime\prime}_{i,\alpha}
. Furthermore, there is a finite cover
\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{i,0}\times\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{i,1}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{f_{i}}
. Suppose that
i\in\mathbf{I}_{\text{exu}}
. From the process in §
7.2.2
, there is an orthogonal Shimura curve
\mathcal{Z}_{i,0}
sitting inside a GSpin Shimura variety
\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}_{i,0}
, together with an ordinary naïve integral model
\mathscr{Z}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{i,0}\subseteq\mathscr{S}^{\prime\prime}_{i,0}
and a finite cover
\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{i,0}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{f_{i}}
.

Let
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}=\{i|i\in\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{I}_{\text{ex}}\}\sqcup\{i^{0}|i\in\mathbf{I}_{\text{exu}}\}\sqcup\{i^{0},i^{1}|i\in\mathbf{I}_{\text{exs}}\}
. We associate to each
i^{0}
, where
i\in\mathbf{I}_{\text{exu}}
, the GSpin Shimura variety
\mathcal{S}_{i^{0}}:=\mathcal{S}_{i,0}^{\prime\prime}
. Similarly, we associate to each
i^{0}
resp.
i^{1}
, where
i\in\mathbf{I}_{\text{exs}}
, the GSpin Shimura variety
\mathcal{S}_{i^{0}}:=\mathcal{S}_{i,0}^{\prime\prime}
resp.
\mathcal{S}_{i^{1}}:=\mathcal{S}_{i,1}^{\prime\prime}
. For
i\in\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{I}_{\text{ex}}
, we leave
\mathcal{S}_{i}
untouched. We then “
lift
”
f
to an
f^{\prime}:(X^{\prime},x^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}^{\prime},\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}=\prod_{j\in\mathbf{I}^{\prime}}\mathscr{S}_{j,\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
: First take an irreducible component
\breve{X}
of the pullback of
X
to

\prod_{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{I}_{\text{ex}}}\mathscr{X}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{f_{i},\mathbb{F}}\times\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}_{\text{exu}}}\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{i,0,\mathbb{F}}\times\prod_{\mathbf{I}_{i\in\mathbf{I}_{\text{exs}}}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{i,0,\mathbb{F}}\times\widetilde{\mathscr{Z}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{i,1,\mathbb{F}})
, with a marked point
\breve{x}
over
x
. Then find a smooth scheme
(X^{\prime},x^{\prime})\twoheadrightarrow(\breve{X},\breve{x})
and define
f^{\prime}:(X^{\prime},x^{\prime})\twoheadrightarrow(\breve{X},\breve{x})\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}^{\prime},\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
.

It is clear from construction that
\mathcal{X}_{f^{\prime}_{i}}=\mathcal{X}_{f_{i}}
if
i\in\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{I}_{\text{ex}}
,
\mathcal{X}_{f^{\prime}_{i^{0}}}=\mathcal{Z}_{i,0}
if
i\in\mathbf{I}_{\text{exu}}
, and
\mathcal{X}_{f^{\prime}_{i^{\alpha}}}=\mathcal{Z}_{i,\alpha}
if
i\in\mathbf{I}_{\text{exs}}
, so
f^{\prime}
is non-exceptional. We informally say that
f^{\prime}
“lifts”
f
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Conjecture
4.7
and
4.9
are true if they hold in the non-exceptional case.

Proof.

We start by an
f:(X,x)\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
. There is a “lifting”
f^{\prime}:(X^{\prime},x^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}^{\prime}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
as in Construction
3
, which is non-exceptional. It is easy to see that
\mathcal{X}_{f^{\prime}}
projects to
\mathcal{X}_{f}
, and
\rk\mathscr{X}_{f,\mathbb{F},\red}^{/x}=\rk\mathscr{X}_{f^{\prime},\mathbb{F},\red}^{/x}
. If Conjecture
4.7
is true for
f^{\prime}
, it then follows that
\mathscr{X}_{f,\red}^{/x}=\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
. So Conjecture
4.7
holds for
f
. Moreover, there is a morphism
\beta:\MTT(f)\rightarrow\MTT(f^{\prime})
corresponding to the projection
\mathcal{X}_{f^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{f}
, which becomes an isomorphism when passing to adjoints. Furthermore,
\beta_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
takes
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}
to
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I}^{\prime},u,X_{0}^{\prime}},x^{\prime})^{\circ}
. If Conjecture
4.9
holds for
f^{\prime}
,
then
\MTT(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{u}}
and
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},x)^{\circ}
have the same adjoint. By Proposition
4.5
, Conjecture
4.9
also holds for
f
.
\hfill\square

7.3.
Further analysis on local systems

Setup being the same as in §
7.1
. In light of Lemma
7.3
, we will assume that
f
is non-exceptional. By base changing to an étale cover, we will assume that
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{I},u,X_{0}},x)
is connected for every
u
. Therefore all monodromy groups for subquotient local systems are connected.

7.3.1.
Crystalline local systems and Tate-linear lattices

Recall from §
6.3
that for each
i\in\mathbf{I}
we have an overconvergent
F
-isocrystal
\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}}
over
X_{0}
. Let
\mathbb{M}_{p,f}
be the direct sum of them. Let
\gr_{0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f}
,
\gr_{\neq 0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f}
(
resp
.
\gr_{0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}}
,
\gr_{\neq 0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}}
) be the slope zero and slope nonzero summands of the graded object
\gr\mathbb{M}_{p,f}

(
resp
.
\gr\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}}
). The notation here is slightly different from the earlier ones: indeed,
\gr_{0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}}=\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}}^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}
and
\gr_{\neq 0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}}=\mathbb{D}(\formalBr_{i,X_{0}})\oplus\mathbb{D}(\formalBr_{i,X_{0}})^{\vee}
.
For slope reasons, we have
G(\gr\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)=G(\gr_{0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)\times G(\gr_{\neq 0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)
. The following diagram summarizes the morphisms between separated collections (cf. §
7.1.3
) of monodromy groups (to ease notation, we have abbreviated
\{G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)\twoheadrightarrow G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}},x)\}_{i\in\mathbf{I}}
as
\{G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)\}_{\mathbf{I}}
):

(7.3.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{G(\gr_{0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)\}_{\mathbf{I}}\times\{G(\gr_{\neq 0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)\}_{\mathbf{I}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{T_{f,x}\}_{\mathbf{I}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)^{\der}\}_{\mathbf{I}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{G(\gr\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)\}_{\mathbf{I}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{G(\mathbb{M}^{-}_{p,f},x)\}_{\mathbf{I}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)\}_{\mathbf{I}}}}

The conjugation action
G(\gr\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)\curvearrowright T_{f,x}
restricts to faithful representations
G(\gr_{0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)\curvearrowright T_{f,x}
and
G(\gr_{\neq 0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)\curvearrowright T_{f,x}
, and these actions commute with their projections to each index. Since
G(\gr\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)
contains a maximal torus of
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)
, this provides us with a lot of information, as we will see later.

On the other hand, the structure of
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)^{\ad}
is also easy to understand. We know from §
6.5
that
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}},x)=\mathcal{G}_{i,\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
. Note that
\mathcal{G}_{i,\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
can be
p
-adically split, even if
\mathcal{G}_{i}
is simple. So we need to put some effort to deal with the
p
-adic exceptional spitting.
An index
i
is called
p
-
simple
, if
\mathcal{G}_{i,\mathfrak{p}_{i}}^{\ad}=G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}},x)^{\ad}
is simple. Otherwise it is called
p
-
split
. In this case,
\SO(Q_{f_{i},\mathfrak{p}_{i}})
sits in the middle of an exceptional sequence like (
7.2.1
). We will write
\SO(Q_{f_{i},\mathfrak{p}_{i}})^{\ad}=\SO(q_{{i^{0}}})\times\SO(q_{{i^{1}}})
, and
call
i^{0},i^{1}

p
-
virtual indices
associated to
i
. Every
p
-simple index
i
is also regarded as
p
-virtual. Let
\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{pvt}}
be the set of
p
-virtual indices. To ease notation, let
G_{i}
be the group
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}},x)
. When
i
is
p
-split, we denote by
G_{i^{0}}

resp
.
G_{i^{1}}
the group
\SO(q_{i^{0}})

resp
.
\SO(q_{i^{1}})
. The structure of
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)^{\ad}
is now easy to describe. Applying Lemma
7.1
to the separated collection
\{G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)^{\ad}\twoheadrightarrow G_{i}^{\ad}\}_{i\in\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{pvt}}}
, we obtain the following

Lemma 7.4
.

There is a partition
\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{pvt}}=\prod_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathbf{I}^{h}_{\mathrm{pvt}}
and an iso-graph
G^{h}
over
\{G_{i}^{\ad}\}_{i\in\mathbf{I}^{h}_{\mathrm{pvt}}}
for each
h
, such that
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)^{\ad}=\prod_{h\in\mathcal{H}}G^{h}
.

Corollary 7.5
.

If for every two-element subset
\mathbf{J}\subseteq\mathbf{I}
we can construct a set
\bm{\Delta}_{\mathbf{J}}\subseteq\End(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{J},\overline{\eta}})\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
sufficient over
\mathbf{J}

11
11

11

This means that
T_{f_{\mathbf{J}},x}
is the subgroup of
U_{\GSpin^{\prime},\mu_{\mathbf{J}}}
(the unipotent of
\GSpin^{\prime}(L_{\mathbf{J},\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
with respect to the cocharacter
\mu_{\mathbf{J}}
) that commutes with
\bm{\Delta}_{\mathbf{J}}
.
, then
\bm{\Delta}=\bigcup_{\mathbf{J}}\bm{\Delta}_{\mathbf{J}}
is sufficient (over
\mathbf{I}
).

Proof.

This corollary is in the same spirit as Remark
7.2
. It is easy to see that the structure of
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)^{\ad}
is completely determined by the the structures of its projections to all
G_{i}^{\ad}\times G_{j}^{\ad}
where
i\neq j
runs over
\mathbf{I}
. On the other hand, for
i\in\mathbf{I}
, the lattice
T_{f,x}

resp
.
T_{f_{i},x}
is just the unipotent subgroup of
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)^{\ad}

resp
.
G_{i}^{\ad}
that corresponds to the Hodge cocharacter
\mu_{\mathbf{I}}^{c}
. For a
p
-virtual index
j\in\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{pvt}}
, we also let
T_{f_{j},x}
be the unipotent subgroup of
G_{j}^{\ad}
that corresponds to
\mu_{\mathbf{I}}^{c}
. Taking unipotents of the groups in Lemma
7.4
, we get a “product of iso-graphs” description of
T_{f,x}
as a subgroup (subspace) of
\prod_{j\in\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{pvt}}}T_{f_{j},x}=\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}T_{f_{i},x}
.
Using basic linear algebra, one deduces that
T_{f,x}
is the largest subspace of
\prod_{i\in\mathbf{I}}T_{f_{i},x}
whose projection to every pair
T_{f_{i},x}\times T_{f_{j},x}
coincides with
T_{f_{\{i,j\}},x}
. Now let
T\supseteq T_{f,x}
be the subgroup of
U_{\GSpin^{\prime},\mu_{\mathbf{I}}}
that commutes with
\bm{\Delta}
. Then for each two-element subset
\mathbf{J}\subseteq\mathbf{I}
, the projection of
T
to
U_{\GSpin^{\prime},\mu_{\mathbf{J}}}
coincides with
T_{f_{\mathbf{J}},x}
. As noted before, this implies that
T\subseteq T_{f,x}
. Therefore
T=T_{f,x}
.
\hfill\square

Remark 7.6
.

Let
i\neq j\in\mathbf{I}
be two indices such that
i
is
p
-split, and let
\mathbf{J}=\{i,j\}
. It is easy to describe the possible structures of
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)^{\ad}
and

T_{f_{\mathbf{J}}}
. In fact, we have the following list.

(1)

The trivial case:
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)^{\ad}=G_{i}^{\ad}\times G_{j}^{\ad}
and
T_{f_{\mathbf{J}}}=T_{f_{i}}\times T_{f_{j}}
. This includes the case where
G_{j}^{\ad}
is a trivial group (hence
T_{f_{j}}=\{0\}
).

(2)

j
is
p
-simple,
G_{j}^{\ad}
is nontrivial, and
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)^{\ad}
is the graph of a surjection
G_{i}^{\ad}\twoheadrightarrow G_{j}^{\ad}
. The kernel must be
G_{i^{0}}
or
G_{i^{1}}
, and
\dim T_{f_{\mathbf{J}}}=2
.

(3)

j
is
p
-split and
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)^{\ad}
is the graph of an isomorphism
G_{i}^{\ad}\xrightarrow{\sim}G_{j}^{\ad}
. Again
\dim T_{f_{\mathbf{J}}}=2
.

(4)

j
is
p
-split and
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)^{\ad}
is the fiber product over a diagram
G_{i}^{\ad}\twoheadrightarrow H\twoheadleftarrow G_{j}^{\ad}
. Here
H
is the quotient of
G_{i}^{\ad}
by
G_{i^{0}}
or
G_{i^{1}}
, and the quotient of
G_{j}^{\ad}
by
G_{j^{0}}
or
G_{j^{1}}
. We have
\dim T_{f_{\mathbf{J}}}=3
.

7.3.2.
Compatible systems

For each
i\in\mathbf{I}
, we can apply Proposition
6.10
, and form various local systems as in §
6.5.2
. In the following, we fix a sufficiently large normal number field
K\subseteq\mathbb{C}
containing all embeddings
F_{i}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
for all
i\in\mathbf{I}
. We will also assume that for every
p
-split index
i\in\mathbf{I}
, the form
Q_{f_{i}}
is split upon base changing along every embedding
F_{i}\hookrightarrow K
. Let
\Sigma_{F_{i}}
be the set of embeddings of
F_{i}
into
K
. There are distinguished embeddings
\tau_{i}\in\Sigma_{F_{i}}
, which give rise to
\mathfrak{p}_{i}\in F_{i}
.

Let
\mathbf{J}
be a subset of
\mathbf{I}
. An element of
\Sigma_{F_{i}}

resp
.
\prod_{i\in\mathbf{J}}\Sigma_{F_{i}}
will usually be denoted by
\sigma_{i}

resp
.
\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}=(\sigma_{i})_{i\in\mathbf{J}}
. The
\Gal(K/\mathbb{Q})
-orbit of
(\tau_{i})_{i\in\mathbf{J}}
in
\prod_{i\in\mathbf{J}}\Sigma_{F_{i}}
will be denoted by
\Orb_{\tau_{\mathbf{J}}}
. Let
\mathfrak{P}
be the place of
K
induced from the isomorphism
\mathbb{C}\simeq\mathbb{C}_{p}
. Then
\mathfrak{P}
lies above each
\tau_{i}\mathfrak{p}_{i}
. For each fixed index
i\in\mathbf{I}
, the construction in Definition
4
yields a collection of coefficient objects
\{\mathbb{M}_{\sigma_{i},v,f_{i}}\}_{(\sigma_{i},v)\in\Sigma_{F_{i}}\times\mathrm{fpl}(K)}
, and for each fixed
\sigma_{i}
the sub-collection forms a weakly
K
-compatible system. For a
\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}=(\sigma_{i})_{i\in\mathbf{J}}
, we define
\mathbb{M}_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}},v,f_{\mathbf{J}}}:=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbf{J}}\mathbb{M}_{\sigma_{i},v,f_{i}}
. Then for a fixed
\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}
, the collection
\{\mathbb{M}_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}},v,f_{\mathbf{J}}}\}_{v\in\mathrm{fpl}(K)}
is also a weakly
K
-compatible system.
In the following, we will set

(7.3.2)

\displaystyle G_{\sigma_{i},v}:=G(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma_{i},v,f_{i}},x)=\mathcal{G}_{i,\sigma_{i},K_{v}},\,\,G_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}},v}:=G(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}},v,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x).

Note that
G_{\tau_{i},\mathfrak{P}}=G_{i,K_{\mathfrak{P}}}
, where
G_{i}=G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}},x)
is as per §
7.3.1
. Furthermore, for
g\in\Gal(K/\mathbb{Q})
, we have
gG_{\sigma_{i},v}=G_{g\sigma_{i},gv}
and
gG_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}},v}=G_{g\sigma_{\mathbf{J}},gv}
.

Lemma 7.7
.

If
i,j\in\mathbf{I}
be such that a
p
-virtual index associated to
i
and a
p
-virtual index associated to
j
lie in the same
\mathbf{I}^{h}_{\mathrm{pvt}}
as in Lemma
7.4
(equivalently,
T_{f_{\{i,j\}},x}\subsetneq T_{f_{i},x}\times T_{f_{j},x}
). Then
\tau_{i}F_{i}=\tau_{j}F_{j}
.

Proof.

In the following let
\mathbf{J}=\{i,j\}
. First suppose that
i,j
are both
p
-simple. We then know from Lemma
7.4
that
G_{\tau_{\mathbf{J}},\mathfrak{P}}^{\ad}
is an iso-graph over
\{G_{\tau_{i},\mathfrak{P}}^{\ad},G_{\tau_{j},\mathfrak{P}}^{\ad}\}
. Now Lemma
6.12
and Lemma
3.4
imply that for every
v
,
G_{\tau_{\mathbf{J}},v}^{\ad}
is an iso-graph over

\{G_{\tau_{i},v}^{\ad},G_{\tau_{j},v}^{\ad}\}
. By Galois twist, we see that
G_{g\tau_{\mathbf{J}},v}^{\ad}
is an iso-graph over over
\{{G}_{g\tau_{i},v}^{\ad},{G}_{g\tau_{j},v}^{\ad}\}
.
If
\tau_{i}F_{i}\not\subseteq\tau_{j}F_{j}
, then Chebotarev’s density theorem implies that there is a
g\in\Gal(K/\mathbb{Q})
, such that
g\tau_{i}=\tau_{i}
but
g\tau_{j}\neq\tau_{j}
. Since
\mathbb{M}_{\tau_{i},\mathfrak{P},f_{i}}
has nonzero slopes, the fact that

G_{g\tau_{\mathbf{J}},\mathfrak{P}}^{\ad}
is an iso-graph over

\{G_{g\tau_{i},\mathfrak{P}}^{\ad},G_{g\tau_{j},\mathfrak{P}}^{\ad}\}
would imply that
\mathbb{M}_{g\tau_{j},\mathfrak{P},f_{j}}
also has nonzero slopes, contradicting Lemma
6.15
. Therefore
\tau_{i}F_{i}\subseteq\tau_{j}F_{j}
. A symmetric argument shows that
\tau_{j}F_{j}\subseteq\tau_{i}F_{i}
. So

\tau_{i}F_{i}=\tau_{j}F_{j}
. For the case where one of
i
and
j
is
p
-split, we have a classification as in Remark
7.6
. The argument is not so much different from the
p
-simple case, and is left to the readers.

\hfill\square

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
i\neq j\in\mathbf{I}
, such that
i
is
p
-split, then Remark
7.6
(
2
) or (
4
) can not happen.

Proof.

Suppose that
i
is
p
-split. The group
\mathcal{G}_{i,\tau_{i},K}^{\ad}
splits over
K
. Let’s write
\mathcal{G}_{i,\tau_{i},K}^{\ad}=\mathcal{G}_{i_{0}}\times\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}}
, such that for
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, the base change
\mathcal{G}_{i_{\alpha},{K_{\mathfrak{P}}}}
matches
{G}_{i^{\alpha},K_{\mathfrak{P}}}
(note that
\mathcal{G}_{i,\tau_{i},K_{\mathfrak{P}}}={G}_{i,K_{\mathfrak{P}}}
). Now suppose that
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\{i,j\}}},x)^{\ad}
is the graph of a surjection

\beta^{\ad}:G_{i}^{\ad}\twoheadrightarrow G_{j}^{\ad}
as in Remark
7.6
(
2
). Using Lemma
7.7
, we deduce that
\tau_{i}F_{i}=\tau_{j}F_{j}
. For a place
\mathfrak{Q}
of
K
lying above
\tau_{i}\mathfrak{p}_{i}=\tau_{j}\mathfrak{p}_{j}
, let
\beta^{\ad}_{K_{\mathfrak{Q}}}
be the base change of
\beta^{\ad}
to
K_{\mathfrak{Q}}
. It makes sense to talk about
\Gal(K/\tau_{i}F_{i})
-action on
\beta^{\ad}_{K_{\mathfrak{Q}}}
: in fact,
g\beta^{\ad}_{K_{\mathfrak{Q}}}=\beta^{\ad}_{K_{g\mathfrak{Q}}}
. If
i\notin\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{exs}}
, there is some
g\in\Gal(K/\tau_{i}F_{i})
swapping
\mathcal{G}_{i_{0}}
and
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}}
. So the kernel of
g\beta^{\ad}_{K_{\mathfrak{Q}}}
is different from that of
\beta^{\ad}_{K_{g\mathfrak{Q}}}
, contradiction. Since we have assumed that
\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{ex}}=\emptyset
, Remark
7.6
(
2
) cannot happen. Similar argument works for Remark
7.6
(
4
).
\hfill\square

Lemma 7.9

(Refinement of Lemma
7.4
)
.

There is a partition
\mathbf{I}=\prod_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\mathbf{I}^{k}
, such that for each
k
there is an iso-graph
G^{k}
resp.
T^{k}_{x}
resp.
\widetilde{G}^{k}
over
\{G_{i}^{\ad}\}_{i\in\mathbf{I}^{k}}
resp.
\{T_{f_{i},x}\}_{i\in\mathbf{I}^{k}}
resp.
\{\widetilde{G}_{i}^{\ad}\}_{i\in\mathbf{I}^{k}}
, such that
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)^{\ad}=\prod_{k\in\mathcal{K}}G^{k}
resp.
T_{f,x}=\prod_{k\in\mathcal{K}}T^{k}_{x}
resp.
G(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}},x)^{\ad}=\prod_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\widetilde{G}^{k}
.

Proof.

Lemma
7.8
implies that only Remark
7.6
(
1
) and (
3
) can happen. Combining Lemma
7.4
, we get the assertion for
G^{k}
. By passing to unipotents, we get the assertion for
T_{x}^{k}
. For the assertion for
\widetilde{G}^{k}
, we use the decomposition
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{I},v,X_{0}}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\bigoplus\mathbb{M}_{\sigma_{i},v,f_{i}}
. It follows from the assertion for
G^{k}
, Lemma
7.7
, plus a routine Galois twist argument together with the results on the monodromy in compatible systems.
\hfill\square

7.4.
Proof of the conjectures

We first establish Conjecture
4.7
for products of GSpin Shimura varieties. In the following, we continue to assume that
f
is non-exceptional. It suffices to construct a sufficient set
\bm{\Delta}_{\mathbf{J}}
for every two-element subset
\mathbf{J}\subseteq\mathbf{I}
, as indicated in Corollary
7.5
. Of course, for each
i\in\mathbf{I}
, we already have
a sufficient set
\bm{\Delta}_{i}\subseteq\End(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{i,\overline{\eta}})\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
as constructed in §
6.4
that cut out
\mathcal{X}_{f_{i}}
from
\mathcal{S}_{i}
. So the main goal is to construct crystalline morphisms between Abelian schemes over different indices. This will be done via a case-by-case study.
In the following, let
\mathbf{J}=\{i,j\}
. The assumption that
f
is non-exceptional wipes out many annoying cases and leaves only three:

7.4.1.
Trivial case

T_{f_{\mathbf{J}},x}=T_{f_{i},x}\times T_{f_{j},x}
. Let
\bm{\Delta}_{\mathbf{J}}=\bm{\Delta}_{i}\cup\bm{\Delta}_{j}
. It is trivially sufficient over
\mathbf{J}
.

7.4.2.
Orthogonal case

T_{f_{\mathbf{J}},x}\subsetneq T_{f_{i},x}\times T_{f_{j},x}
, and both
\mathcal{G}_{i}
and
\mathcal{G}_{j}
are simple orthogonal groups over the respective totally real fields. Note that
\mathcal{G}_{i}
and
\mathcal{G}_{j}
admit finite maps to their adjoints, so as a consequence of Lemma
7.9
, there is an isomorphism
\beta:G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}},x)\rightarrow G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{j}},x)
whose graph equals
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)
, and this induces an isomorphism
T_{f_{i},x}\rightarrow T_{f_{j},x}
whose graph is
T_{f_{\mathbf{J}},x}
. We also see that
\tau_{i}F_{i}=\tau_{j}F_{j}
as per Lemma
7.7
.

To construct a sufficient set, we need to construct a certain isomorphism
\theta:\mathbb{H}_{i,p,X_{0}}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{j,p,X_{0}}
. Our first step is to construct a certain isomorphism
\tilde{b}:\mathbb{L}_{i,p,X_{0}}\rightarrow\mathbb{L}_{j,p,X_{0}}
. To see how this is possible, we first use
T_{f_{\mathbf{J}},x}
to construct an isomorphism
b:\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}}\rightarrow\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{j}}
.

To achieve this, we revisit (
7.3.1
) and the paragraph after that. Let
T_{p}
be a maximal torus of
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)
contained in
G(\gr\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)
. Using the faithful actions
G(\gr_{0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)\curvearrowright T_{f_{\mathbf{J}},x}
,
G(\gr_{\neq 0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)\curvearrowright T_{f_{\mathbf{J}},x}
and their projections to each index, one finds that the action
T_{p}\curvearrowright\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}})
is isomorphic to
T_{p}\curvearrowright\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{j}})
(one can explicitly write down the matrices as in Remark
6.2
, details omitted). Since
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)
is connected, theorem of the highest weight (together with a descent argument) shows that the
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)
-representations
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}})
and
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{j}})
are isomorphic. Therefore, there is a linear isomorphism
b:\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}})\rightarrow\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{j}})
compatible with
\beta
, inducing an isomorphism
b:\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}}\rightarrow\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{j}}
. Recall that
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}})
comes with a quadratic pairing
Q_{f_{i},\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
, and similarly
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{j}})
with
Q_{f_{j},\mathfrak{p}_{j}}
. The isomorphism
b
further preserves the pairings up to homothety:
Q_{f_{j},\mathfrak{p}_{j}}(b-,b-)=\lambda Q_{f_{i},\mathfrak{p}_{i}}(-,-)
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{*}
. This is a consequence of the following lemma, which follows easily from
[
8
, Proposition C.3.14]
and Schur’s lemma:

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
F
be a characteristic 0 field and let
V
be an
F
-space with dimension not equal to 2. Suppose that
Q_{1},Q_{2}
are two nondegenerate quadratic forms over
V
. If
\SO(Q_{1})=\SO(Q_{2})
as subgroups of
\GL(V)
, then there is some
\lambda\in F^{*}
such that
Q_{2}=\lambda Q_{1}
.

As we have already seen in Proposition
4.5
, there is some rational Frobenius torus
M\subseteq\Hg(f_{\mathbf{J}})
such that
M_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is a maximal torus of
G(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{J},p,X_{0}},x)
. Let
\mathrm{Fr}
be a generator of
M
and let
n\geq 0
, the above discussion on
\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}}
implies that the characteristic polynomials of
\mathrm{Fr}^{n}
on
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}})
and
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{j}})
are equal. Since the characteristic polynomial of
\mathrm{Fr}^{n}
on
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{i,p,X_{0}})
is a product of Galois twists of the characteristic polynomial on
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}})
, we find that the characteristic polynomials of
\mathrm{Fr}^{n}
on
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{i,p,X_{0}})
and
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{j,p,X_{0}})
are equal. It then implies that the representations of
M
on
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{i,p,X_{0}})
and
\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{j,p,X_{0}})
are isomorphic (say, from Brauer–Nesbitt theorem). As a result, there is an isomorphism
\tilde{b}:\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{i,p,X_{0}})\rightarrow\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{j,p,X_{0}})
of
G(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{J},p,X_{0}},x)
-representations, such that after base change to
K_{\mathfrak{P}}
, (1)
\tilde{b}
splits as a direct sum of isomorphisms of subrepresentations
{b}_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}:\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma_{i},\mathfrak{P},f_{i}})\rightarrow\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma_{j},\mathfrak{P},f_{i}})
, where
\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}\in\Orb_{\tau_{\mathbf{J}}}
and (2),
g{b}_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}={b}_{g\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}
for
g\in\Gal(K_{\mathfrak{P}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. By Lemma
7.10
, each
b_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}
preserves the pairings up to homothety:
Q_{f_{j},\sigma_{j},\mathfrak{P}}(b_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}-,b_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}-)=\lambda_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}Q_{f_{i},\sigma_{i},\mathfrak{P}}(-,-),\lambda_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}\in K_{\mathfrak{P}}^{*}
. The homothety factors also satisfy
g\lambda_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}=\lambda_{g\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}
.

We are ready to construct the promised isomorphism
\theta:\mathbb{H}_{i,p,X_{0}}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{j,p,X_{0}}
.
First, for each
\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}\in\Orb_{\tau_{\mathbf{J}}}
, we can construct an isomorphism of vector spaces

(7.4.1)

\displaystyle\theta_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}:\Cl(\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma_{i},\mathfrak{P},f_{i}}))

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Cl(\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma_{j},\mathfrak{P},f_{j}})),

\displaystyle s_{1}s_{2}...s_{m}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\lambda^{-\lfloor m/2\rfloor}_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}b_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}(s_{1})b_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}(s_{2})...b_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}(s_{m}).

Note that
\Spin(Q_{f_{i},\sigma_{i},\mathfrak{P}})
acts on
\Cl(\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma_{i},\mathfrak{P},f_{i}}))
, and we have an isomorphism
c_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}:\Spin(Q_{f_{i},\sigma_{i},\mathfrak{P}})\rightarrow\Spin(Q_{f_{j},\sigma_{j},\mathfrak{P}})
which reduces to the isomorphism
G(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma_{i},\mathfrak{P},f_{i}},x)\rightarrow G(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma_{j},\mathfrak{P},f_{j}},x)
. It is easy to check that the
\theta_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}
constructed above is compatible with the spin representations. Observe that

\omega_{x}(\mathbb{H}_{i,\mathfrak{P},X_{0}})=\Cl(\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{i,\mathfrak{P},X_{0}}))=\bigotimes_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}\in\Orb_{\tau_{\mathbf{J}}}}\Cl(\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{\sigma_{i},\mathfrak{P},f_{i}})),

and the above discussion implies that
\bigotimes_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}\theta_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}
is an isomorphism of
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{J},\mathfrak{P},X_{0}},x)
-representations. Since
\bigotimes_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}\theta_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}
is invariant under
\Gal(K_{\mathfrak{P}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-action, it descents to the desired isomorphism
\theta:\omega_{x}(\mathbb{H}_{i,p,X_{0}})\rightarrow\omega_{x}(\mathbb{H}_{j,p,X_{0}})
of
G(\mathbb{H}_{\mathbf{J},p,X_{0}},x)
-representations. This then induces an element
\bm{\delta}_{\mathbf{J}}\in\End(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{J},\overline{\eta}})\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. The set
\bm{\Delta}_{\mathbf{J}}=\bm{\Delta}_{i}\cup\bm{\Delta}_{j}\cup\{\bm{\delta}_{\mathbf{J}}\}
is sufficient over
\mathbf{J}
.

7.4.3.
Unitary case

T_{f_{\mathbf{J}},x}\subsetneq T_{f_{i},x}\times T_{f_{j},x}
, and both
\mathcal{G}_{i}
and
\mathcal{G}_{j}
are unitary groups over the respective totally real fields. Similar to the orthogonal case, there is an isomorphism
\beta^{\der}:G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}},x)^{\der}\rightarrow G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{j}},x)^{\der}
whose graph equals
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)^{\der}
, and this induces an isomorphism
T_{f_{i},x}\rightarrow T_{f_{j},x}
whose graph is
T_{f_{\mathbf{J}},x}
. Note that in the unitary case, we also have a decomposition
\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}}=\mathbb{W}_{p,f_{i}}\oplus\mathbb{W}_{p,f_{i}}^{\prime}
into totally isotropic parts, where
\mathbb{W}_{p,f_{i}}^{\prime}
is dual to
\mathbb{W}_{p,f_{i}}
(Lemma
6.5
), and
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}},x)\simeq\GL(\omega_{x}(\mathbb{W}_{p,f_{i}}))
.

Using the faithful actions
G(\gr_{0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)\curvearrowright T_{f_{\mathbf{J}},x}
,
G(\gr_{\neq 0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)\curvearrowright T_{f_{\mathbf{J}},x}
and their projections to each index, one finds that the central torus of
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)
is a rank 1 diagonal torus in
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}},x)\times G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{j}},x)
. It follows that
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)
is the graph of an isomorphism
\beta:G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}},x)\rightarrow G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{j}},x)
extending
\beta^{\der}
. A maximal torus argument as in the orthogonal case (or a direct construction using explicit coordinates) shows that there is an isomorphism of
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)
-representations
c:\omega_{x}(\mathbb{W}_{p,f_{i}})\rightarrow\omega_{x}(\mathbb{W}_{p,f_{j}})
. Dualizing and reversing the arrow, we also get an isomorphism of
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)
-representations
c^{\prime}:\omega_{x}(\mathbb{W}_{p,f_{i}}^{\prime})\rightarrow\omega_{x}(\mathbb{W}_{p,f_{j}}^{\prime})
. As a result, there is an isomorphism of
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{\mathbf{J}}},x)
-representations
b=c\oplus c^{\prime}:\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{i}})\rightarrow\omega_{x}(\mathbb{M}_{p,f_{j}})
, which already preserves the pairings.

Using a strategy similar to the orthogonal case, one can construct an isomorphism
\mathbb{L}_{i,p,X_{0}}\rightarrow\mathbb{L}_{j,p,X_{0}}
that preserves the pairings. As described in Remark
6.14
,
possibly enlarge
K
, we have compatible systems
\{\mathbb{W}_{\sigma_{i},v,f_{i}}\}_{v\in\mathrm{fpl}(K)}
and
\{\mathbb{W}_{\sigma_{i},v,f_{i}}^{\prime}\}_{v\in\mathrm{fpl}(K)}
. Arguing as the orthogonal case, there is an isomorphism
\tilde{b}:\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{i,p,X_{0}})\rightarrow\omega_{x}(\mathbb{L}_{j,p,X_{0}})
of
G(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{J},p,X_{0}},x)
-representations, such that after base change to
K_{\mathfrak{P}}
, (1)

\tilde{b}
splits as a direct sum of isomorphisms of subrepresentations

c_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}:\omega_{x}(\mathbb{W}_{\sigma_{i},\mathfrak{P},f_{i}})\rightarrow\omega_{x}(\mathbb{W}_{\sigma_{j},\mathfrak{P},f_{j}})
and

c_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}^{\prime}:\omega_{x}(\mathbb{W}_{\sigma_{i},\mathfrak{P},f_{i}}^{\prime})\rightarrow\omega_{x}(\mathbb{W}_{\sigma_{j},\mathfrak{P},f_{j}}^{\prime})
, where
\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}\in\Orb_{\tau_{\mathbf{J}}}
, (2)
c_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}^{-1}
is dual to
c_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}^{\prime}
and (3)
g{c}_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}={c}_{g\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}
and
g{c}^{\prime}_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}={c}^{\prime}_{g\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}
for
g\in\Gal(K_{\mathfrak{P}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

Since
b_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}=c_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}\oplus c^{\prime}_{\sigma_{\mathbf{J}}}
preserves the pairings,
\tilde{b}
also preserves the pairings. As a result, it yields an isomorphism
\theta:\mathbb{H}_{i,p,X_{0}}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{j,p,X_{0}}
, which gives rise to an element
\bm{\delta}_{\mathbf{J}}\in\End(\mathscr{A}^{\KS}_{\mathbf{J},\overline{\eta}})\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. The set
\bm{\Delta}_{\mathbf{J}}=\bm{\Delta}_{i}\cup\bm{\Delta}_{j}\cup\{\bm{\delta}_{\mathbf{J}}\}
is sufficient over
\mathbf{J}
.

7.4.4.
The proof

Theorem 7.11
.

Conjecture
4.7
and Conjecture
4.9
are true.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.3
it suffices to treat the case where
f
is non-exceptional. Then Conjecture
4.7
is true by Corollary
7.5
and the discussions made in §
7.4.2
\sim
§
7.4.3
. By virtue of Proposition
4.5
, to show Conjecture
4.9
, it suffices to show that
\Hg(f)^{\ad}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}=G(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}},x)^{\ad}
. We already know that this is true when
\#\mathbf{I}=1
. Let
S=\{\Res_{F_{i}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{\ad}|i\in\mathbf{I}\}
and
S_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}=\{\widetilde{G}_{i}^{\ad}|i\in\mathbf{I}\}
. Then
S
is a collection of simple adjoint groups. Applying Lemma
7.1
to the separated collection
\{\Hg(f)^{\ad}\twoheadrightarrow\Hg(f_{i})^{\ad}\}
, we see that
\Hg(f)^{\ad}
is a product of iso-graphs over
S
. Base change to
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, we then see that
\Hg(f)^{\ad}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is a product of iso-graphs over
S_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. On the other hand, Lemma
7.9
implies that
G(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}},x)^{\ad}\subseteq\Hg(f)^{\ad}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is also a product of iso-graphs over
S_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. Since Conjecture
4.7
is true, the unipotent subgroups of
\Hg(f)^{\ad}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
and
G(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}},x)^{\ad}
with respect to
\mu_{\mathbf{I}}^{c}
are equal. This forces
\Hg(f)^{\ad}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}=G(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbf{I},p,X_{0}},x)^{\ad}
.
\hfill\square

8.
Characteristic
p
analogue of the André–Oort conjecture

In this section we prove the characteristic
p
analogue of the André–Oort conjecture (
4.10
) for products of GSpin Shimura varieties. Suppose
X
contains a Zariski dense collection of positive dimensional special subvarieties. In §
8.2
we will construct a certain large
p
-adic étale lisse sheaves on a finite field model of
X
that arise from these special subvarieties. After that, we use the established cases of the Tate-linear conjecture and the characteristic
p
analogue of the Mumford–Tate conjecture to show that
X
is quasi-weakly special. Routinely, we use
X_{0}
to denote a variety over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
whose base change to
\mathbb{F}
is
X
, such that the immersion
X\subseteq\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}
is the base change of an immersion
X_{0}\subseteq\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}_{q}}
.

8.1.
Setups

Notation being the same as Conjecture
4.10
.
Recall that
\bm{A}
is a collection of special subvarieties on
X
and
\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}}\subseteq\mathbf{I}
is the set of indices
i
such that
\bm{A}
contains a Zariski dense collection of special subvarieties whose projections to
\mathscr{S}_{i,\mathbb{F}}
are
positive dimensional. We will always assume that
\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}}\neq\emptyset
. In the following, the letter “
Z
” is reserved for denoting special subvareities.
\bm{A}
is said to be
normalized
, if

(1)

Each

Z\in\bm{A}
is a positive dimensional connected smooth locally closed subvariety of
X
,

(2)

The projection of any
Z\in\bm{A}
to
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}},\mathbb{F}}
is a single point.

We call
\bm{A}
is
simple
, if it further satisfies

(3)

Any special subvariety in
\bm{A}
has positive dimensional projections to
\mathscr{S}_{i,\mathbb{F}}
for all
i\in\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}}
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Possibly shrinking
\bm{A}
and replacing a special subvariety in
\bm{A}
by an open dense subset, there is a normalized collection
\bm{A}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}^{\prime}}=\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}}
. Therefore, to prove Conjecture
4.10
, we can always assume
\bm{A}
is normalized.

Proof.

First, for any
Z\in\bm{A}
, replace
Z
by its irreducible components
Z_{1},...,Z_{n}
. If
Z_{i}
is zero dimensional, we throw it out. Otherwise, replace
Z_{i}
by an open dense subset which is smooth. As a result, we get a collection
\bm{A}^{\prime\prime}
satisfying (
1
) with
\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}}=\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}^{\prime\prime}}
. Then, let
\bm{B}
be the collection of special subvarieties in
\bm{A}^{\prime\prime}
whose projections to
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}^{\prime\prime}},\mathbb{F}}
are positive dimensional. It can not be a Zariski dense collection, for otherwise there must be an index
i\in\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}^{\prime\prime}}
such that
\bm{A}^{\prime\prime}
contains a Zariski dense collection of special subvarieties whose projections to
\mathscr{S}_{i,\mathbb{F}}
are
positive dimensional, hence
i\in\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}^{\prime\prime}}
, a contradiction. Let
\bm{A}^{\prime}=\bm{A}^{\prime\prime}-\bm{B}
.
\hfill\square

As a result of Lemma
8.1
, we will always assume that
\bm{A}
is normalized. We call a morphism
X^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow X_{0}
an
étale open dense subset
, if
X^{\prime}_{0}
is geometrically connected and finite étale over an open dense subset of
X_{0}
. In this case, we denote
X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
as the base change to
\mathbb{F}
of the map
X^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow X_{0}
. It is again an étale open dense subset. We then denote by
\bm{A}_{X^{\prime}}
the pullback of
\bm{A}
to
X^{\prime}
. The definition of
\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}}
, and the notion of being normalized and simple, extends to
\bm{A}_{X^{\prime}}
. We have
\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}}=\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}_{X^{\prime}}}
. Furthermore,
\bm{A}_{X^{\prime}}
is normalized
resp
. simple, if
\bm{A}
is normalized
resp
. simple.

8.2.
p
-adic lisse sheaves arising from dense collections of special subvarieties

8.2.1.
Tautological deformation spaces

For any subvariety of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}
, we use
\Delta
to denote its immersion into
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}
. There are formal schemes
(X\times X)^{/\Delta}
,
(X\times\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}})^{/\Delta}
over
X
and
(Z\times Z)^{/\Delta}
over
Z
sitting inside following diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Z\times Z)^{/\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\times X)^{/\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\times\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}})^{/\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

These formal schemes shall be thought of as variations of deformation spaces over the base scheme. More precisely, over each
x\in X(\mathbb{F})
, the fiber of
(X\times\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}})^{/\Delta}
at
x
is just
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}^{/x}
, while the fiber of
(X\times X)^{/\Delta}
at
x
is
X^{/x}
.

By Chai’s theory of global Serre–Tate coordinates (
[
4
, §2]
), there is an étale lisse sheaf of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-modules over
X_{0}
, namely,
\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}:=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbf{I}}X_{*}(\formalBr_{i,X_{0}})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}T_{p}(\Psi^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}_{i,X_{0}})^{\vee}
, such that

(8.2.1)

(X\times\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}})^{/\Delta}=\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},X}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}.

Here
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}
stands for the formal torus over
\mathbb{F}
and
\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},X}
is the pullback of
\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}
to
X
. For later use, we also set
\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{J},X_{0}}:=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbf{J}}X_{*}(\formalBr_{j,X_{0}})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}T_{p}(\Psi^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}_{j,X_{0}})^{\vee}
for any subset
\mathbf{J}\subseteq\mathbf{I}
.

Fix a special subvariety
Z\in\bm{A}
.
[
39
, Theorem 3.7]
implies that
(Z\times Z)^{/\Delta}\subseteq(Z\times\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}})^{/\Delta}
is a subtorus of the formal torus (
8.2.1
) over
Z
. There exists a finite field model
Z_{0}/\mathbb{F}_{q^{n}}
of
Z
, such that (1)
Z\subseteq X
is the base change of
Z_{0}\subseteq X_{0,\mathbb{F}_{q^{n}}}
and (2) there is a saturated étale lisse subsheaf
\mathcal{F}_{[Z]}\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},Z_{0}}
over
Z_{0}
, with
(Z\times Z)^{/\Delta}=\mathcal{F}_{[Z],Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}
,
where
\mathcal{F}_{[Z],Z}
is the base change of
\mathcal{F}_{[Z]}
to
Z
. Note that when
Z
runs over
\bm{A}
, the corresponding
n
may not be bounded.

8.2.2.
The induced
p
-adic lisse sheaves

We refer the readers to
[
22
, §10.14]
for basic properties of Noetherian formal schemes. Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a Noetherian formal scheme and
\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{X}}
be its structure sheaf. An open formal subscheme of
\mathfrak{X}
is a pair
(\mathfrak{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{X}}|_{\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}})
. A coherent ideal
\mathscr{A}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{X}}
defines a closed formal subscheme
(\mathfrak{Y},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{X}}/\mathscr{A}|\mathfrak{Y})
, where
\mathfrak{Y}
is the support of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{X}}/\mathscr{A}
, and every closed formal subscheme arise this way. An open formal subscheme of a closed formal subscheme of
\mathfrak{X}
is called a locally closed formal subscheme.

In our setting, we have an ambient Noetherian formal scheme
\mathfrak{X}=(X\times\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}})^{/\Delta}
. For every
Z\in\bm{A}
,
(Z\times Z)^{/\Delta}
is a locally closed formal subscheme of
\mathfrak{X}
. Let
\mathfrak{Z}
be the smallest closed formal subscheme of
(X\times\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}})^{/\Delta}
containing all
(Z\times Z)^{/\Delta},Z\in\bm{A}
. It is a Noetherian subscheme of
\mathfrak{X}
. Since
(X\times X)^{/\Delta}
is a closed formal subscheme containing all
(Z\times Z)^{/\Delta}
, we have
\mathfrak{Z}\subseteq(X\times X)^{/\Delta}
.

Lemma 8.2
.

There is an étale open dense subset
X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
, together with
p
-adic lisse sheaves
\{\mathcal{H}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{n}
over
X^{\prime}
, such that the irreducible components of
\mathfrak{Z}_{X^{\prime}}:=\mathfrak{Z}\times_{X}X^{\prime}
are exactly
\{\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}\}_{k=1}^{n}
.

Proof.

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is a scaling by
1+p^{n}
morphism
\mathfrak{s}_{n}
over
\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},X}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}
. Clearly, every
\mathfrak{s}_{n}
is an isomorphism and takes each
(Z\times Z)^{/\Delta}
to itself. Therefore,
\mathfrak{s}_{n}
takes
\mathfrak{Z}
to itself. By a rigidity result of Chai (
[
6
]
), any irreducible component of
\mathfrak{Z}_{\overline{\eta}}
is a formal subtorus of
\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},\overline{\eta}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}
, where
\eta
is the generic point of
X
. As a result, there is an étale open dense subset
X^{\prime}
of
X
, with generic point
\eta^{\prime}
, such that the irreducible components of
\mathfrak{Z}_{X^{\prime}}
are in bijection with the irreducible components of
\mathfrak{Z}_{\overline{\eta}}
. Let
\mathfrak{Y}_{1},,...,\mathfrak{Y}_{n}
be the irreducible components of
\mathfrak{Z}_{X^{\prime}}
. It follows that every
\mathfrak{Y}_{k,\eta^{\prime}}
is a formal subtorus of
\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},\eta^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}
. Taking cocharacter lattices, we see that
\mathfrak{Y}_{k,\eta^{\prime}}
gives rise to a saturated lisse subsheaf
\mathcal{H}_{k,\eta^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},\eta^{\prime}}
. Since
X^{\prime}
is smooth, there is a surjection
\pi_{1}(\eta^{\prime},\overline{\eta})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(X^{\prime},\overline{\eta})
, so we can spread out
\mathcal{H}_{k,\eta^{\prime}}
to a saturated lisse subsheaf
\mathcal{H}_{k}\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},X^{\prime}}
. Since
\mathcal{H}_{k,\eta^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}=\mathfrak{Y}_{k,\eta^{\prime}}
, by further shrinking
X^{\prime}
, we can assume that
\mathcal{H}_{k}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}=\mathfrak{Y}_{k}
. This finishes the proof.
\hfill\square

Proposition 8.3
.

Suppose that
\bm{A}
is simple and normalized. There is an étale open dense subset
X^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow X_{0}
, as well as a saturated étale lisse subsheaf
\mathscr{F}\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}},X^{\prime}_{0}}
, which satisfies the following properties:

(1)

for each
i\in\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}}
, the projection of
\mathscr{F}
to
\mathscr{E}_{i,X_{0}^{\prime}}
has positive rank,

(2)

\mathscr{F}_{X^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}\subseteq(X^{\prime}\times X)^{/\Delta}
(here
\Delta
is the graph of
X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
).

Proof.

By Lemma
8.2
, there is a finite étale cover
X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
, together with
p
-adic lisse sheaves
\{\mathcal{H}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{n}
over
X^{\prime}
, such that the irreducible components of
\mathfrak{Z}_{X^{\prime}}:=\mathfrak{Z}\times_{X}X^{\prime}
are exactly
\{\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}\}_{k=1}^{n}
. Since
\mathfrak{Z}\subseteq(X\times X)^{/\Delta}
, we have

(8.2.2)

\mathcal{H}_{k}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}\subseteq\mathfrak{Z}_{X^{\prime}}\subseteq(X^{\prime}\times X)^{/\Delta},\,\,1\leq k\leq m.

Fix a
Z\in\bm{A}_{X^{\prime}}
. There exists a sufficiently large finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q^{n}}
, together with
\mathbb{F}_{q^{n}}
-varieties
Z_{0}
and
X^{\prime}_{0}
, such that (a) there is an immersion
Z_{0}\subseteq X^{\prime}_{0}
and an étale open dense set
X^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow X_{0}
, such that
Z\subseteq X^{\prime}

resp
.
X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
is the base change of
Z_{0}\subseteq X^{\prime}_{0}

resp
.
X^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow X_{0}
to
\mathbb{F}
, (b) there is an étale lisse sheaf
\mathcal{F}_{[Z]}
over
Z_{0}
as discussed in §
8.2
and (c)
Z_{0}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{n}})\neq\emptyset
.

Pick a point
x_{0}\in Z_{0}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{n}})
and a point
x\in Z_{0}(\mathbb{F})
mapping to
x_{0}
. Let
{\mathscr{F}}_{x}
be the saturated submodule of
\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},x}
generated by
\{g\cdot\mathcal{F}_{[Z],x}|g\in\pi_{1}(X^{\prime},x)\}
. Since
\mathcal{F}_{[Z],x}
is invariant under
\Gal(x|x_{0})
-action, we can use the fact that

\pi_{1}(X^{\prime}_{0},x)=\pi_{1}(X^{\prime},x)\rtimes\Gal(x|x_{0})
to show that
\mathscr{F}_{x}
is invariant under
\pi_{1}(X^{\prime}_{0},x)
-action. It is then routine to check that
\mathscr{F}_{x}
is a continuous
\pi_{1}(X^{\prime}_{0},x)
-representation. As a result,
\mathscr{F}_{x}
gives rise to a saturated arithmetic lisse subsheaf
\mathscr{F}\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},X^{\prime}_{0}}
.

Since
\bm{A}
is normalized, the projection of
Z
to
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}},\mathbb{F}}
is a single point. As a result, for any
g\in\pi_{1}(X^{\prime},x)
, we have
g\cdot\mathcal{F}_{[Z],x}\subseteq g\cdot\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}},x}\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}},x}
. It follows that
{\mathscr{F}}\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}},X^{\prime}_{0}}
.

Since
\bm{A}_{X^{\prime}}
is simple, for any
i\in\mathbf{I}_{\bm{A}}
, the projection of
Z
to each
\mathscr{S}_{i,\mathbb{F}}
is positive dimensional. As a result, the projection of
Z^{/x}=\mathcal{F}_{[Z],x}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}
to each
\mathscr{S}_{i,\mathbb{F}}^{/x}=\mathscr{E}_{i,x}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}
has positive rank. Therefore, the projection of
\mathcal{F}_{[Z],x}
to each
\mathscr{E}_{i,x}
has positive rank. On the other hand, we have
\mathcal{F}_{[Z],x}\subseteq{\mathscr{F}}_{x}
by construction. It follows that the projection of
\mathscr{F}_{x}
to each
\mathscr{E}_{i,x}
has positive rank, hence (
1
).

Since the irreducible components of
\mathfrak{Z}_{X^{\prime}}
are in bijection with the irreducible components of
\mathfrak{Z}_{\eta^{\prime}}
,
\mathcal{F}_{[Z],Z}
is contained in the restriction to
Z
of one of the lisse sheaves
\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},...,\mathcal{H}_{m}
. Say, it is contained in
\mathcal{H}_{1,Z}
. It follows from definition that
{\mathscr{F}}_{X^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{1}
. But then, (
8.2.2
) implies that
{\mathscr{F}}_{X^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}\subseteq(X^{\prime}\times X)^{/\Delta}
. So we have (
2
).

\hfill\square

8.2.3.
Monodromy representations

Let
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\mathbb{D}(\formalBr_{i,X_{0}})^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{D}(\Psi^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}_{i,X_{0}})
. We see that
\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}
is exactly the sheaf of Frobenius invariant sections of the uni-root
F
-isocrystal
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}(1)
. In fact, it corresponds to
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}(1)
under the equivalence between the category of uni-root
F
-isocrystals and the category of continuous
\pi_{1}(X_{0},x)
-representations with
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-coefficient (
[
9
, Theorem 2.1]
). Take the fiber functors as in §
3
and identify them via the canonical isomorphisms

\omega_{x}(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}})\simeq\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbf{I}}\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\formalBr_{i}))^{\vee}\otimes\omega_{x}(\mathbb{D}(\Psi^{{\text{\'{e}t}}}_{i}))\simeq U_{\GL^{\prime},\overline{\mu}_{\mathbf{I}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\simeq\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},x,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}.

We see that
G(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}},x)\subseteq\GL(\omega_{x}(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}))
is generated by
G(\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}},x)
and the image of the Hodge cocharacter of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}
. On the other hand, there is a conjugation action
G(\gr\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}),x)\curvearrowright U_{\GL^{\prime},\overline{\mu}_{\mathbf{I}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, and the image of
G(\gr\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}),x)\rightarrow\GL(U_{\GL^{\prime},\overline{\mu}_{\mathbf{I}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))
is identified with
G(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}},x)
.

An étale lisse subsheaf
\mathscr{F}\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}
gives rise to a
G(\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}},x)
-subrepresentation
\mathscr{F}_{x,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{\mathbf{I},x,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. By above discussion, it gives rise to a subrepresentation of
G(\gr\mathbb{D}(\Psi_{\mathbf{I},X_{0}}),x)\curvearrowright U_{\GL^{\prime},\overline{\mu}_{\mathbf{I}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, which is again denoted by
\mathscr{F}_{x,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. Write
f
for
X\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}
, and assume that
\mathscr{F}_{x,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\subseteq T_{f,x}
. Then
\mathscr{F}_{x,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
further corresponds to a sub of the conjugation action
G(\gr\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)\curvearrowright T_{f,x}
as per §
7.3.1
. We will apply these observations to the lisse sheaf
\mathscr{F}
that arises from Proposition
8.3
.

8.3.
Proof of the conjectures

We begin with the single case to illustrate the method.

Theorem 8.4
.

Conjecture
4.10
is true for a single GSpin Shimura variety.

Proof.

We omit the subscript
\mathbf{I}
. Let
X^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow X_{0}
be an étale open subset that satisfies Proposition
8.3
and let
\mathscr{F}
be the lisse subsheaf thus arise. Pick
x\in X^{\prime}_{0}(\mathbb{F})
and let
f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
be the composition of the étale open subset
X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
and the immersion
X\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
, and let
\mathscr{T}_{f,x}
and
T_{f,x}
be objects defined in §
6.1
. We have by Proposition
8.3
(
2
),

(8.3.1)

\mathscr{F}_{x}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}\subseteq{X^{\prime}}^{/x}\subseteq\mathscr{T}_{f,x}.

In particular,
\mathscr{F}_{x,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\subseteq{T}_{f,x}
. From §
8.2.3
we see that, after making the identification (
6.3.1
),
\mathscr{F}_{x,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
gives rise to a subrepresentation of
G(\gr\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)\curvearrowright T_{f,x}
. Hence a sub of
G(\gr_{0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)\curvearrowright T_{f,x}
by restriction. By Remark
6.7
,
G(\gr_{0}\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)^{\circ}
is either
\SO(T_{f,x})
or
\SU(\varphi_{f,x,0})
, depending on whether
T_{f,x}
is nondegenerate or totally isotropic.

We now deduce from these facts that
\mathscr{T}_{f,x}=X^{\prime/x}
. Note that the case
\dim X^{\prime}=1
is trivial. So, in the following we assume
\dim X^{\prime}\geq 2
. If
\dim{T}_{f,x}=2
, then it follows from dimension reason that
\mathscr{T}_{f,x}=X^{\prime/x}
. If
\dim T_{f,x}\geq 3
, then the action of
\SO(T_{f,x})
or
\SU(\varphi_{f,x,0})
on
T_{f,x}
is irreducible. This forces
\mathscr{F}_{x,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}=T_{f,x}
. As a result, (
8.3.1
) is a equality and
\mathscr{T}_{f,x}=X^{\prime/x}
. Since
X^{/x}=X^{\prime/x}
,
Theorem
6.9
implies that
X
is special.
\hfill\square

Remark 8.5
.

When
\dim X=\dim T_{f,x}=2
, it can happen that
\dim\mathscr{F}_{x,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}=1
. When this happens,
G(\mathbb{M}_{p,f},x)^{\circ}
is split. Write
\Hg(X)=\Res_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\SO(Q_{f})
as per Proposition
6.10
. Then
\SO(Q_{f})
splits over
\mathfrak{p}
. One the other hand, one can pick
x
that lies on a special subvariety
Z
with
Z^{/x}=\mathscr{F}_{x}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge}
. So there is a
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup
\Hg(Z)\subseteq\Hg(X)
, such that the projection of
\Hg(Z)_{F}
to
\SO(Q_{f})
is a proper normal subgroup upon base changing to
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
, hence
\SO(Q_{f})
is exceptionally split.

Lemma 8.6

(Linear algebra exercise)
.

Let
V_{1},...,V_{n}
be vector spaces over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

(1)

If all
V_{i}
are isomorphic,
V
is an iso-graph over
\{V_{1},...,V_{n}\}
, and
W\subseteq V
is a subspace whose projection to at least one
V_{i}
is surjective. Then
W=V
.

(2)

Let
(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{*})^{n}
act on
\oplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}
by scaling on each summand, and let
V\subseteq\oplus_{n=1}^{m}V_{n}
be a subspace stable under this action. If
V
projects surjectively onto each summand, then
V=\oplus_{n=1}^{m}V_{n}
.

Theorem 8.7
.

Conjecture
4.10
is true.

Proof.

Let
f:X\subseteq\mathscr{S}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{\mathbf{I},\mathbb{F}}
be the immersion. Using Construction
3
, we can reduce to the case where
f
is non-exceptional: up to shrinking we can find a non-exceptional locally closed immersion
f^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{S}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}_{\mathbf{I}^{\prime},\mathbb{F}}
that “lifts”
f
. We can then lift the collection of special subvarieties to
X^{\prime}
. If the conjecture holds for
X^{\prime}
